## modern-en · cicero · 5:12

From this it appears how necessary it is, for any man who aspires to true knowledge, to examine the definitions of earlier authors — either correcting them where they are carelessly set down, or making them himself. For errors in definitions multiply as the reckoning proceeds, and lead men into absurdities which at last they see but cannot avoid, unless they reckon again from the beginning, where the foundation of their errors lies. Hence it happens that those who trust to books do as men do who cast up many little sums into a great one, without considering whether those little sums were rightly cast up to begin with: when at last the error becomes visible, since they do not suspect their first grounds, they do not know how to clear themselves, but spend their time fluttering through their books — like birds that, entering by the chimney and finding themselves shut in a chamber, beat themselves against the false light of a glass window for want of wit to consider which way they came in. So the first use of speech lies in the right defining of names — which is the acquiring of science; and the first abuse lies in wrong definitions, or none at all — from which proceed all the false and senseless tenets that make those men who take their instruction from the authority of books, rather than from their own reflection, fall as far below the condition of ignorant men as men endowed with true science stand above it. For between true science and erroneous doctrine, ignorance is in the middle. Natural sense and imagination are not subject to absurdity; nature itself cannot err; and as men grow in abundance of language, they become either wiser than ordinary or madder than ordinary. Nor is it possible, without letters, for any man to become either excellently wise, or (unless his memory is damaged by disease, or by some defect of the organs) excellently foolish. For words are wise men's counters — they only reckon with them; but they are the money of fools, who value them by the authority of an Aristotle, a Cicero, a Thomas, or any other doctor whatever, so long as he is a man.

Cited repeatedly as an authority-name people trust without understanding, and directly quoted for his remark that there is no absurdity philosophers have not maintained, and for the anecdote of Cassius's "cui bono" method of judging accusations.

## modern-en · livy · 8:6

From this we may conclude that whenever we believe a saying — of whatever kind — to be true, on the basis of arguments drawn not from the thing itself, nor from the principles of natural reason, but from the authority and good opinion we have of the one who said it, then the speaker, or the person we believe in or trust, and whose word we take, is the object of our faith; and the honour done in believing is done to him alone. Consequently, when we believe that the Scriptures are the word of God, having had no direct revelation from God himself, our belief, faith, and trust is in the Church, whose word we take and rest in. And those who believe what a prophet relates to them in the name of God take the prophet's word, do honour to him, and trust and believe in him concerning the truth of what he reports — whether he is a true prophet or a false one. The same goes for all other histories. If I did not believe everything that is written by historians about the glorious deeds of Alexander or Caesar, I do not think the ghost of Alexander or Caesar would have any just cause to be offended — nor anyone else, but the historian. If Livy says the gods once made a cow speak, and we do not believe it, we do not distrust God in this, but Livy. So it is plain that whatever we believe on no other ground than what is drawn from the authority of men only, and their writings, whether they were sent from God or not, is faith in men only.

The Roman historian, cited as an example of an author whose incredible claims (a cow made to speak by the gods) reflect on his own credibility as a witness, not on God's.

## modern-en · alexander-the-great · 3:3

Much memory, or memory of many things, is called Experience. Again, Imagination is only of those things that have formerly been perceived by sense, either all at once or in parts at several times. The first (imagining the whole object as it was presented to the sense) is Simple Imagination, as when one imagines a man or a horse one has seen before. The other is Compound, as when, from the sight of a man at one time, and of a horse at another, we conceive in our mind a centaur. So when a man compounds the image of his own person with the image of the actions of another man—as when a man imagines himself a Hercules, or an Alexander (which often happens to those much taken with the reading of Romances)—it is a compound imagination, and properly nothing but a fiction of the mind. There are also other imaginations that rise in men (though waking) from the great impression made in sense; as from gazing on the sun, the impression leaves an image of the sun before our eyes a long time after; and from being long and intently fixed on geometrical figures, a man in the dark (though awake) will have the images of lines and angles before his eyes—a kind of fancy that has no particular name, being something that does not commonly enter into men's discourse.

Cited as an example of a man whose glorious deeds people believe on trust from historians, and separately for cutting the Gordian knot with his sword rather than untying it.

## modern-en · suarez · 9:26

There is yet another fault in the discourses of some men, which may also be counted among the sorts of madness — namely, the abuse of words, of which I spoke before in the fifth chapter under the name of absurdity. This happens when men speak words which, put together, have no signification at all, but which have been hit upon either by some, through misunderstanding the words they have received and repeat by rote, or by others, from a deliberate intention to deceive by obscurity. This falls only to those who deal in questions of incomprehensible matters, like the Schoolmen, or in questions of abstruse philosophy. The common sort of men seldom speak insignificantly, and are therefore by those other distinguished persons counted idiots. But to be assured that the Schoolmen's words have nothing in the mind to correspond to them, some examples are needed. If anyone wants them, let him take a Schoolman in hand and see if he can translate any chapter on any difficult point — the Trinity, the Deity, the nature of Christ, transubstantiation, free will, and so on — into any modern tongue so as to make it intelligible; or into any tolerable Latin, such as those were familiar with who lived when Latin was the common tongue. What does it mean to say, 'The first cause does not necessarily inflow anything into the second, by virtue of the essential subordination of the second causes, by which it may help it to work'? That is the translation of the title of the sixth chapter of Suárez's first book, Of the Concourse, Motion, and Help of God. When men write whole volumes of such stuff, are they not mad, or do they not intend to make others so? In particular, in the question of transubstantiation: when, after certain words spoken, those who say that the whiteness, roundness, magnitude, quality, corruptibility — all of which are incorporeal — leave the wafer and enter the body of our blessed Saviour, do they not make those 'nesses' and 'tudes' and 'ties' into so many spirits possessing his body? For by spirits they always mean things that, being incorporeal, are nevertheless able to move from one place to another. So this kind of absurdity may rightly be counted among the many sorts of madness; and all the time when, guided by clear thoughts of their worldly lust, they refrain from disputing or writing in this way, those are only lucid intervals. And so much for the intellectual virtues and defects.

The Jesuit scholastic philosopher, quoted (via a mocking, untranslatable chapter title from his first book) as an example of School-Divinity's insignificant speech.

## modern-en · numa-pompilius · 13:19

[The Designs Of The Authors Of The Religion Of The Heathen.] And therefore the first founders and legislators of commonwealths among the Gentiles, whose aims were only to keep the people in obedience and peace, have everywhere taken care: First, to imprint in their minds a belief that the precepts they gave concerning religion should not be thought to come from their own invention but from the dictates of some god or other spirit — or else that they themselves were of a higher nature than mere mortals — so that their laws might more easily be received. Thus Numa Pompilius pretended to receive the ceremonies he established among the Romans from the nymph Egeria; the first king and founder of the kingdom of Peru pretended that he and his wife were children of the Sun; and Mahomet, to set up his new religion, pretended to have conferences with the Holy Ghost, in the form of a dove. Second, they took care to have it believed that the same things were displeasing to the gods which were forbidden by the laws. Third, to prescribe ceremonies, supplications, sacrifices, and festivals, by which they were to believe the anger of the gods might be appeased; and that bad success in war, great epidemics, earthquakes, and every man's private misery came from the anger of the gods, and their anger from the neglect of their worship, or from forgetting or mistaking some point of the required ceremonies. And although among the ancient Romans men were not forbidden to deny what the poets wrote of the pains and pleasures after this life — which many of great authority and gravity in that state openly derided in their public speeches — yet that belief was always more cherished than its opposite.

The legendary second King of Rome, cited as an example of a lawgiver who claimed private revelation -- from the nymph Egeria -- to make his laws more easily received.

## modern-en · homer · 11:44

Nor does it change the question of honour whether an action — provided it is great and difficult, and so a sign of much power — is just or unjust. Honour consists only in the opinion of power. So the ancient heathens did not think they were dishonouring their gods, but rather greatly honouring them, when they introduced them in their poems as committing rapes, thefts, and other great but unjust or unclean acts. Nothing is so much celebrated in Jupiter as his adulteries; nor in Mercury as his frauds and thefts. The greatest of Mercury's praises in a hymn of Homer is that, being born in the morning, he had invented music by noon, and before nightfall had stolen the cattle of Apollo from his herdsmen.

The poet whose hymn is cited for the story of Mercury inventing music and stealing Apollo's cattle in a single day; his verses are also cited as material used for a form of lottery-divination (dipping into Homer for an omen).

## modern-en · perseus-myth · 9:24

There was once a great gathering of people in Abdera, a city of the Greeks, at the performance of the tragedy of Andromeda, on an extremely hot day. As a result, a great many of the spectators, falling into fevers, suffered this peculiar consequence from the heat and the tragedy together: they did nothing but recite iambic verses, with the names of Perseus and Andromeda. This, along with the fever, was cured by the coming on of winter. And the madness was thought to come from the passion imprinted by the tragedy. Likewise a fit of madness once raged in another Greek city, seizing only the young maidens, and causing many of them to hang themselves. Most at the time thought this an act of the devil. But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

Half of the mythological pair (with Andromeda) whose tragedy, performed on a very hot day at Abdera, was blamed for a mass outbreak of fever-induced delirium among the audience.

But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

The mythological princess of the same Abdera tragedy-outbreak anecdote as Perseus.

## modern-en · catiline · 12:20

From the same source, too, it comes that men cannot tell apart, without study and great understanding, one action of many men from many actions of one multitude. For example, they cannot easily distinguish the one action of all the senators of Rome in killing Catiline from the many actions of a number of senators in killing Caesar. And so they are disposed to take for the action of the people what is in fact a multitude of actions done by a multitude of men, led perhaps by the persuasion of one.

The failed Roman conspirator, cited as an example of the difference between one action of a whole body (the Senate condemning Catiline) and a multitude of separate actions by many individual senators (killing Caesar).

## modern-en · aristotle · 2:4

But the philosophy-schools, throughout all the universities of Christendom, grounded on certain texts of Aristotle, teach a different doctrine. They say that the cause of vision is that the thing seen sends out on every side a Visible Species (in English, a Visible Show, Apparition, or Aspect, or a Being Seen); and that the receiving of this into the eye is seeing. As for the cause of hearing, they say the thing heard sends out an Audible Species—that is, an Audible Aspect, or Audible Being Seen—which, entering the ear, produces hearing. And as for the cause of understanding, they also say that the thing understood sends out Intelligible Species, that is, an Intelligible Being Seen, which, coming into the understanding, makes us understand. I do not say this as if I disapproved of the use of universities; but because I shall have to speak hereafter of their office in a Commonwealth, I must let you see on every occasion in passing what things in them stand in need of amendment—among which the frequency of meaningless speech is one.

Cited constantly and at length -- as an authority-name trusted without understanding, as the source of the Schools' "vain philosophy" of Separated Essences and Metaphysiques, and by name for specific doctrines in his Politiques and Ethiques that Hobbes disputes at length in chapters 46-47.

## modern-en · plato · 27:0

By CIVIL LAWS I mean the laws that men are bound to observe because they are members not of this or that commonwealth in particular, but of a commonwealth. For the knowledge of particular laws belongs to those who profess the study of the laws of their several countries; but the knowledge of civil law in general belongs to anyone. The ancient law of Rome was called their civil law, from the word civitas, which means a commonwealth. And those countries which, having been under the Roman Empire and governed by that law, still retain such part of it as they think fit, call that part the civil law, to distinguish it from the rest of their own civil laws. But that is not what I intend to speak of here; my design is not to show what is law here and there, but what law is — as Plato, Aristotle, Cicero, and various others have done, without taking on themselves the profession of the study of law.

The Athenian philosopher who taught in the public walks called Academia (giving his followers the name Academiques), and who required geometry of all who entered his school.

## modern-en · academus · 47:6

After the Athenians, by overthrowing the Persian armies, had gained control of the sea, and through that of all the islands and coastal cities of the Archipelago—both in Asia and Europe—and had grown wealthy, those who had no occupation either at home or abroad had little else to do but, as St. Luke says (Acts 17:21), "tell and hear news," or discourse publicly on philosophy to the young men of the city. Every master took some place for that purpose. Plato used certain public walks called Academia, named after one Academus; Aristotle used the walk of the Temple of Pan, called the Lycaeum; others used the Stoa, the covered walk where merchants' goods were brought ashore; and others used other places, where they spent their leisure time teaching or debating their opinions. Some used any spot where they could gather the city's young people to hear them talk. This was also what Carneades did at Rome when he was an ambassador, which led Cato to advise the Senate to dispatch him quickly, for fear of corrupting the manners of the young men who delighted to hear him say what they took to be fine things.

The figure from whom the Academia -- the public walk where Plato taught -- took its name.

## modern-en · zeno-stoic · 47:7

From this practice came the name Schola for the place where any of them taught and debated, which in their language meant "leisure"; and their debates were called Diatribae, that is, "passing of the time." The philosophers themselves took the names of their sects from these schools: those who followed Plato's doctrine were called Academics; the followers of Aristotle were called Peripatetics, from the walk where he taught; and those whom Zeno taught were called Stoics, from the Stoa—as though we should name people after Moorfields, St. Paul's Church, or the Exchange because they often gather there to prattle and loiter.

Founder of the Stoic school, which took its name (Stoiques) from the Stoa, the covered walk where he taught.

## modern-en · socrates · 47:17

But to what end, someone may ask, is such subtlety in a work of this kind, where I claim to deal with nothing but what is necessary for the doctrine of government and obedience? It is to this end: that people may no longer allow themselves to be misled by those who, by this doctrine of separated essences, built on the empty philosophy of Aristotle, would frighten them away from obeying the laws of their country with empty names—as people frighten birds away from the corn with an empty doublet, a hat, and a crooked stick. On this same ground, when someone has died and been buried, they claim that the soul (that is, the life) can move separately from the body and is observed at night around graves. On the same ground, they say that the figure, color, and taste of a piece of bread continue to exist where, on their own account, no bread is. And on the same ground, they say that faith, wisdom, and the other virtues are sometimes infused into a person, sometimes transmitted into him from heaven—as if the virtuous and their virtues could be parted; along with a great many other claims that serve to lessen the dependence of subjects on the sovereign power of their country. For who will work to obey the laws if he expects obedience to be infused or transmitted into him? Who will not obey a priest who claims to be able to produce God, rather than his sovereign—indeed, rather than God himself? Or who, fearing the appearance of departed spirits, will not show great respect to those who can make the holy water said to drive them away? Let this suffice as an example of the errors brought into the Church from the entities and essences of Aristotle, which he may have known to be false philosophy, but wrote as something agreeable to, and supportive of, the religion of his time—fearing the fate of Socrates.

Cited by name once: Hobbes suggests Aristotle may have known his own philosophy was false but wrote it anyway, "fearing the fate of Socrates" -- his execution for impiety.

## modern-en · varro · 47:39

More examples of vain philosophy brought into religion by the doctors of school-divinity might be produced, but others can notice them for themselves if they please. I will only add this: that the writings of the schoolmen are for the most part nothing but meaningless strings of strange and barbarous words, or of words used in ways foreign to the common usage of the Latin tongue—such as would puzzle Cicero, Varro, and all the grammarians of ancient Rome. If anyone wants to see this proved, let him (as I have said once before) try to translate any school-divine into one of the modern languages—French, English, or any other ample language—since whatever cannot be made intelligible in most of these is not intelligible in the Latin either. This unintelligibility of language, though I cannot mark it down as false philosophy, does have one quality: not only to hide the truth, but also to make people think they have it, and so give up further searching.

The Roman grammarian, named alongside Cicero as an author whose Latin the barbarous jargon of Schoole-Divinity would baffle.

## modern-en · selden · 11:48

Titles of honour — such as duke, count, marquis, and baron — are honourable, as they signify the value set on those bearing them by the sovereign power of the commonwealth. These titles were anciently titles of office and command, some derived from the Romans, some from the Germans and French. Dukes (in Latin duces) were generals in war; counts (comites) were those who accompanied the general out of friendship, and were left to govern and defend conquered and pacified territories; marquises (marchiones) were counts who governed the marches, or boundaries, of the Empire. These titles of duke, count, and marquis came into the Empire about the time of Constantine the Great, from the customs of the German military. But 'baron' seems to have been a Gaulish title, signifying a great man — such as the kings' or princes' men whom they employed in war about their own persons; it seems to come from Vir, becoming Ber and Bar (which in the Gaulish tongue meant what Vir meant in Latin), and so to Bero and Baro. So such men were called Berones, and later Barones, and (in Spanish) Varones. He who wants to know more about the origin of titles of honour will find it, as I have found this, in Mr Selden's excellent treatise on the subject. In the course of time these offices of honour, on account of troubles and for reasons of good and peaceful government, were turned into mere titles, serving mostly to mark the precedence, place, and order of subjects in the commonwealth; men were made dukes, counts, marquises, and barons of places where they had neither possession nor command, and other titles also were devised for the same purpose.

Cited by name ("Mr. Seldens most excellent Treatise") as the authority to consult for the detailed history of titles of honour such as Duke, Count, Marquis, and Baron.

## modern-en · coke · 16:3

The fool has said in his heart that there is no such thing as justice — and sometimes also out loud — seriously claiming that since every man's preservation and contentment is committed to his own care, there could be no reason why every man should not do whatever he thinks conduces to it, and therefore that to make or not make, keep or not keep covenants is not against reason when it conduces to one's own benefit. He does not in this deny that there are covenants, that they are sometimes broken and sometimes kept, and that such breach may be called injustice and the observance of them justice. But he asks whether injustice, taking away the fear of God (for the same fool has said in his heart there is no God), may not sometimes stand with that reason which dictates to every man his own good — particularly when it conduces to such a benefit as will put a man in a condition to disregard not only the disapproval and reviling of others but also their power. The kingdom of God is taken by violence — but what if it could be taken by unjust violence? Would it be against reason so to take it, when it is impossible to be hurt by doing so? And if it is not against reason, it is not against justice; otherwise justice is not to be approved as good. From reasoning of this sort, successful wickedness has gotten the name of virtue; and some who in all other things have disallowed the violation of faith have allowed it when it is for the gaining of a kingdom. And the heathen who believed that Saturn was deposed by his son Jupiter still believed the same Jupiter to be the avenger of injustice — somewhat like a piece of law in Coke's Commentaries on Littleton, where he says that if the rightful heir to the crown is attainted of treason, yet the crown shall descend to him, and Eo Instante (at that very instant) the attainder is void. From such examples a man would be very ready to infer that when the heir apparent of a kingdom kills the one in possession — even his own father — you may call it injustice, or by whatever name you like, but it can never be against reason, since all voluntary actions of men aim at their own benefit, and those actions are most reasonable which most conduce to their ends. This specious reasoning is nevertheless false.

Cited (as "Cokes Commentaries on Litleton") for a point of English law on attainder of treason and descent of the Crown, and later quoted directly on the presumption of guilt from flight in felony cases.

## modern-en · aristides · 22:6

Nevertheless, we are not to understand that by such liberty the sovereign power of life and death is either abolished or limited. For it has already been shown that nothing the sovereign representative does to a subject, on any pretext, can properly be called injustice or injury, because every subject is the author of every act the sovereign does — so that the sovereign never wants right to anything, except insofar as he himself is the subject of God, and is bound by that to observe the laws of nature. And so it may, and often does, happen in commonwealths that a subject may be put to death by the command of the sovereign power, and yet neither party do the other a wrong — as when Jephthah caused his daughter to be sacrificed. In that and similar cases, the one so dying had liberty to do the action for which he is nevertheless, without injury, put to death. The same holds when a sovereign prince puts to death an innocent subject. For though the action is against the law of nature, being contrary to equity (as was David's killing of Uriah), yet it was not an injury to Uriah; it was an injury to God. Not to Uriah, because the right to do as he pleased had been given to David by Uriah himself; and yet to God, because David was God's subject, and forbidden all iniquity by the law of nature. This distinction David himself, when he repented the act, plainly confirmed, saying, 'To thee only have I sinned.' In the same way the people of Athens, when they banished the most powerful man in their commonwealth for ten years, thought they committed no injustice — and yet they never inquired what crime he had done, but only what hurt he might do. They even commanded the banishment of men they did not know: every citizen brought his oyster-shell to the marketplace with the name written on it of the man he wanted banished, without actually accusing him; and they sometimes banished an Aristides for his reputation for justice, and sometimes a scurrilous jester such as Hyperbolus, to make a joke of it. And yet one cannot say that the sovereign people of Athens lacked the right to banish them, or that any Athenian lacked the liberty to jest or to be just.

Cited as an example ("an Aristides") of a man ostracized by the Athenians for his reputation for justice.

## modern-en · marius · 28:9

From defect in reasoning (that is, from error), men are prone to violate the laws in three ways. First, by the presumption of false principles: as when men, observing how in all places and in all ages unjust actions have been authorised by the force and victories of those who committed them, and how powerful men breaking through the cobweb laws of their country have made it so that the weaker sort, and those who failed in their enterprises, were reckoned the only criminals — and have taken from this as principles and grounds of their reasoning that 'justice is but an empty word; that whatever a man can get by his own industry and risk is his own; that the practice of all nations cannot be unjust; that examples from former times are good arguments for doing the same again,' and many more of that kind. Once these are granted, no act in itself can be a crime, but must be made so (not by the law, but) by the success of those who commit it; and the same act is virtuous or vicious as fortune pleases. So that what Marius makes a crime, Sulla shall make meritorious — and Caesar (with the same laws standing) shall turn it back into a crime — to the perpetual disturbance of the peace of the commonwealth.

Roman general, named (with Sylla, Pompey, and Caesar) as one of the popular leaders whose successive civil wars extinguished the Roman democracy; also cited for the observation that what Marius made a crime, Sylla later made meritorious, and Caesar a crime again.

## modern-en · pompey · 30:2

Of which this is one: 'That a man to obtain a kingdom is sometimes content with less power than is necessarily required for the peace and defence of the commonwealth.' From this it follows that when the power thus set aside has, for public safety, to be resumed, the resumption has the look of an unjust act — which disposes great numbers of men, when occasion is presented, to rebel; just as the bodies of children begotten by diseased parents are subject either to untimely death or to purging the ill quality derived from their vicious conception by breaking out into boils and scabs. When kings deny themselves some such necessary power, it is not always (though sometimes) out of ignorance of what is necessary to the office they undertake, but often out of a hope to recover the same again at their pleasure. In which they do not reason well; for those who hold them to their promises shall be backed against them by foreign commonwealths, which, for the good of their own subjects, let slip few occasions to weaken the estate of their neighbours. So Thomas Becket, archbishop of Canterbury, was supported against Henry the Second by the pope — the subjection of churchmen to the commonwealth having been dispensed with by William the Conqueror at his accession, when he took an oath not to infringe the liberty of the Church. So too the barons, whose power had been raised by William Rufus (to gain their help in transferring the succession from his elder brother to himself) to a degree inconsistent with the sovereign power, were upheld in their rebellion against King John by the French. Nor does this happen in monarchy alone. For whereas the style of the ancient Roman commonwealth was 'the Senate and people of Rome,' neither the Senate nor the people claimed the whole power; and this first caused the seditions of Tiberius Gracchus, Gaius Gracchus, Lucius Saturninus, and others; and afterwards the wars between the Senate and the people, under Marius and Sulla, and again under Pompey and Caesar, leading to the extinction of their democracy and the setting up of monarchy.

Named alongside Julius Caesar as one of the popular leaders whose rivalry ended the Roman democracy.

In which they do not reason well; for those who hold them to their promises shall be backed against them by foreign commonwealths, which, for the good of their own subjects, let slip few occasions to weaken the estate of their neighbours. So Thomas Becket, archbishop of Canterbury, was supported against Henry the Second by the pope — the subjection of churchmen to the commonwealth having been dispensed with by William the Conqueror at his accession, when he took an oath not to infringe the liberty of the Church. So too the barons, whose power had been raised by William Rufus (to gain their help in transferring the succession from his elder brother to himself) to a degree inconsistent with the sovereign power, were upheld in their rebellion against King John by the French. Nor does this happen in monarchy alone. For whereas the style of the ancient Roman commonwealth was 'the Senate and people of Rome,' neither the Senate nor the people claimed the whole power; and this first caused the seditions of Tiberius Gracchus, Gaius Gracchus, Lucius Saturninus, and others; and afterwards the wars between the Senate and the people, under Marius and Sulla, and again under Pompey and Caesar, leading to the extinction of their democracy and the setting up of monarchy.

Named with his brother Tiberius in the same account of the seditions that began Rome's civil wars.

## modern-en · lucius-saturninus · 30:2

Of which this is one: 'That a man to obtain a kingdom is sometimes content with less power than is necessarily required for the peace and defence of the commonwealth.' From this it follows that when the power thus set aside has, for public safety, to be resumed, the resumption has the look of an unjust act — which disposes great numbers of men, when occasion is presented, to rebel; just as the bodies of children begotten by diseased parents are subject either to untimely death or to purging the ill quality derived from their vicious conception by breaking out into boils and scabs. When kings deny themselves some such necessary power, it is not always (though sometimes) out of ignorance of what is necessary to the office they undertake, but often out of a hope to recover the same again at their pleasure. In which they do not reason well; for those who hold them to their promises shall be backed against them by foreign commonwealths, which, for the good of their own subjects, let slip few occasions to weaken the estate of their neighbours. So Thomas Becket, archbishop of Canterbury, was supported against Henry the Second by the pope — the subjection of churchmen to the commonwealth having been dispensed with by William the Conqueror at his accession, when he took an oath not to infringe the liberty of the Church. Named (as printed, "Lucius Saturnius") alongside the Gracchi as one of the tribunes whose seditions weakened the Roman Senate's power relative to the People.

## modern-en · solon · 30:3

The people of Athens bound themselves from one action only — that no man, on pain of death, should propose the renewing of the war for the island of Salamis; and yet even by that, if Solon had not caused it to be given out that he was mad, and afterwards, in the gesture and dress of a madman, proposed it in verse to the people who flocked around him, they would have had an enemy perpetually in readiness at the very gates of their city. Such are the damages, or evasions, that all commonwealths are reduced to whose power is even slightly limited.

Named as the Athenian lawgiver from whose laws ("Let no man, for private debt, seize or make servant of his debtor") Hobbes draws an example on debt-bondage.

## modern-en · tarquin · 31:24

It also belongs to the office of the sovereign to make a right application of punishments and rewards. And since the end of punishing is not revenge and the discharge of anger, but the correction of either the offender or, by his example, of others, the severest punishments are to be inflicted for the crimes that are of most danger to the public — such as those that proceed from malice toward the established government; those that spring from contempt of justice; those that provoke indignation in the multitude; and those that, going unpunished, seem authorised — as when they are committed by sons, servants, or favourites of men in authority. For indignation carries men not only against the doers and authors of injustice, but against any power that is likely to protect them — as in the case of Tarquin, who, for the insolent act of one of his sons, was driven out of Rome, and the monarchy itself dissolved. But for crimes of infirmity, such as those that proceed from great provocation, from great fear, great need, or from ignorance whether the act is a great crime or not, there is often room for leniency without prejudice to the commonwealth — and leniency, where there is room for it, is required by the law of nature. The punishment of the leaders and teachers in a commotion — not of the poor seduced people — when they are punished, can benefit the commonwealth by their example. To be severe to the people is to punish that ignorance which may in great part be imputed to the sovereign, whose fault it was that they were no better instructed.

The Roman king cited for the story of his rebellious son's insolent act, which he could not punish without protecting his son from ruinous consequences to himself.

## modern-en · ursinicus · 43:57

The same is also confirmed by the continual practice, even to this day, in the election of the bishops of Rome. For if the bishop of any place had the right of choosing another to the succession of the pastoral office in any city — at such time as he went from there to plant the same in another place — much more would he have had the right to appoint his successor in that place where he last resided and died. And we do not find that ever any bishop of Rome appointed his successor. For they were for a long time chosen by the people, as we may see by the sedition raised about the election between Damasus and Ursinicus; which Ammianus Marcellinus says was so great that Juventius the prefect, unable to keep the peace between them, was forced to go out of the city; and that there were above a hundred men found dead upon that occasion in the church itself. And though they were afterwards chosen, first by the whole clergy of Rome, and afterwards by the cardinals, yet never any was appointed to the succession by his predecessor. If therefore they claimed no right to appoint their successors, I think I may reasonably conclude they had no right to appoint the new power — which none could take from the church to bestow on them, but such as had a lawful authority not only to teach, but to command, the church; which none could do but the civil sovereign.

The antipope (Ursinus) whose disputed election against Damasus in 366 AD, as related by Ammianus Marcellinus, caused a violent sedition at Rome that Hobbes cites as evidence of what the papacy's temporal stakes could provoke.

## modern-en · constantine · 11:48

Titles of honour — such as duke, count, marquis, and baron — are honourable, as they signify the value set on those bearing them by the sovereign power of the commonwealth. These titles were anciently titles of office and command, some derived from the Romans, some from the Germans and French. Dukes (in Latin duces) were generals in war; counts (comites) were those who accompanied the general out of friendship, and were left to govern and defend conquered and pacified territories; marquises (marchiones) were counts who governed the marches, or boundaries, of the Empire. These titles of duke, count, and marquis came into the Empire about the time of Constantine the Great, from the customs of the German military. But 'baron' seems to have been a Gaulish title, signifying a great man — such as the kings' or princes' men whom they employed in war about their own persons; it seems to come from Vir, becoming Ber and Bar (which in the Gaulish tongue meant what Vir meant in Latin), and so to Bero and Baro. So such men were called Berones, and later Barones, and (in Spanish) Varones. He who wants to know more about the origin of titles of honour will find it, as I have found this, in Mr Selden's excellent treatise on the subject. In the course of time these offices of honour, on account of troubles and for reasons of good and peaceful government, were turned into mere titles, serving mostly to mark the precedence, place, and order of subjects in the commonwealth; men were made dukes, counts, marquises, and barons of places where they had neither possession nor command, and other titles also were devised for the same purpose.

The first Christian Roman Emperor, credited with causing Christian religion to be regulated under his own authority by the Bishop of Rome, and cited for the origin of the titles Duke, Count, and Marquis in his reign.

## modern-en · silvester · 43:88

The second book has two conclusions: one, that St Peter was bishop of Rome, and there died; the other, that the popes of Rome are his successors. Both of these have been disputed by others; but supposing them to be true, yet if by 'bishop of Rome' is understood either the monarch of the church, or the supreme pastor of it, not Sylvester but Constantine (who was the first Christian emperor) was that bishop; and as Constantine, so all other Christian emperors were of right supreme bishops of the Roman Empire — I say of the Roman Empire, not of all Christendom; for other Christian sovereigns had the same right in their several territories, as an office essentially adherent to their sovereignty. Which shall serve as answer to his second book.

Named to make the point that the Church's head at Constantine's conversion was Constantine himself, not Sylvester.

## modern-en · julian-apostate · 43:133

But to prove that Christians are not to tolerate infidel or heretical kings, he alleges a passage in Deut. 17, where God forbids the Jews, when they shall set a king over themselves, to choose a stranger; and from this infers that it is unlawful for a Christian to choose a king who is not a Christian. And it is true that he who is a Christian — that is, he who has already obliged himself to receive our Saviour when he shall come, for his king — shall tempt God too much in choosing for king in this world one who he knows will endeavour, both by terror and persuasion, to make him violate his faith. But, it is (he says) the same danger to choose one who is not a Christian for king, as not to depose him when he is chosen. To this I say: the question is not of the danger of not deposing, but of the justice of deposing him. To choose him may in some cases be unjust, but to depose him when he is chosen is in no case just. For it is always violation of faith, and consequently against the law of nature, which is the eternal law of God. Nor do we read that any such doctrine was accounted Christian in the time of the apostles, nor in the time of the Roman emperors, until the popes had the civil sovereignty of Rome. But to this he has replied that the Christians of old deposed not Nero, nor Diocletian, nor Julian, nor Valens an Arian, only because they lacked temporal forces. Perhaps so. But did our Saviour, who for calling for them might have had twelve legions of immortal, invulnerable angels to assist him, lack forces to depose Caesar — or at least Pilate, who unjustly, without finding fault in him, delivered him to the Jews to be crucified? Or if the apostles lacked temporal forces to depose Nero, was it therefore necessary for them in their epistles to the newly made Christians to teach them (as they did) to obey the powers constituted over them (of which Nero in that time was one), and that they ought to obey them, not for fear of their wrath, but for conscience' sake? Shall we say they did not only obey, but also teach, what they did not mean, for want of strength? It is not therefore for want of strength, but for conscience' sake, that Christians are to tolerate their heathen princes, or princes (for I cannot call any one whose doctrine is the public doctrine a heretic) who authorise the teaching of an error. And whereas for the temporal power of the pope, he alleges further that St Paul (1 Cor. 6) appointed judges under the heathen princes of those times, such as were not ordained by those princes — it is not true. For St Paul does but advise them to take some of their brethren to compound their differences as arbitrators, rather than go to law one with another before the heathen judges — which is a wholesome precept, and full of charity, fit to be practised also in the best Christian commonwealths. And for the danger that may arise to religion by the subjects' tolerating of a heathen, or an erring, prince, it is a point of which a subject is no competent judge; or if he is, the pope's temporal subjects may judge also of the pope's doctrine. For every Christian prince, as I have formerly proved, is no less supreme pastor of his own subjects, than the pope of his.

Named among the Roman Emperors -- Nero, Diocletian, Julian, Valens -- whom the early Christians never attempted to depose by force despite persecution.

## modern-en · theodosius · 43:137

His sixth, and last, argument is from examples. To which I answer, first, that examples prove nothing; second, that the examples he alleges do not even amount to a probability of right. The fact of Jehoiada in killing Athaliah (2 Kings 11) was either by the authority of King Joash, or it was a horrible crime in the high priest — who (ever after the election of King Saul) was a mere subject. The fact of St Ambrose in excommunicating Theodosius the emperor (if it is true he did so) was a capital crime. And for the popes — Gregory I, Gregory II, Zachary, and Leo III — their judgements are void, as given in their own cause; and the acts done by them in keeping with this doctrine are the greatest crimes (especially that of Zachary) that are incident to human nature. And thus much of power ecclesiastical, wherein I had been more brief, forbearing to examine these arguments of Bellarmine, if they had been his as a private man, and not as the champion of the papacy, against all other Christian princes and states.

The Emperor whom St. Ambrose excommunicated -- an episode Hobbes cites (with reservation, "if it were true hee did so") among examples of clerical power over sovereigns.

## modern-en · justinian · 27:31

The division of laws has been made in various ways, according to the different methods of those who have written of them. For it is something that depends not on nature, but on the writer's scope, and is subordinate to every man's chosen method. In the Institutes of Justinian, we find seven sorts of civil laws.

Cited for the seven sorts of Civil Laws found in his Institutions.

## modern-en · domitian · 34:0

By the books of Holy SCRIPTURE are meant those that ought to be the canon — that is, the rules — of Christian life. And because all the rules of life that men are in conscience bound to observe are laws, the question of Scripture is the question of what is law throughout all Christendom, both natural and civil. For though Scripture does not determine what laws each Christian king shall constitute in his own dominions, it does determine what laws he shall not constitute. Since therefore I have already proved that sovereigns in their own dominions are the sole legislators, only those books are canonical — that is, law — in every nation which are established as such by the sovereign authority. It is true that God is the sovereign of all sovereigns, and so, when he speaks to any subject, he ought to be obeyed, whatever any earthly potentate commands to the contrary. But the question is not of obedience to God, but of when, and what, God has said. To subjects who have no supernatural revelation, this can be known only by that natural reason which guided them, for the obtaining of peace and justice, to obey the authority of their several commonwealths — that is, of their lawful sovereigns. According to this obligation, I can acknowledge no other books of the Old Testament to be Holy Scripture except those that have been commanded to be acknowledged as such by the authority of the Church of England. What books these are is well enough known without setting them all out here; and they are the same as those acknowledged by St Jerome — who holds the rest (namely, the Wisdom of Solomon, Ecclesiasticus, Judith, Tobias, the first and second of Maccabees — though he had seen the first in Hebrew — and the third and fourth of Esdras) for Apocrypha. Of the canonical, Josephus, a learned Jew who wrote in the time of the Emperor Domitian, reckons twenty-two, making the number agree with the Hebrew alphabet. St Jerome does the same, though they reckon them differently. For Josephus numbers five books of Moses, thirteen of prophets who wrote the history of their own times (how that agrees with the prophetic writings contained in the Bible we shall see hereafter), and four of hymns and moral precepts. But St Jerome reckons five books of Moses, eight of prophets, and nine of other holy writings, which he calls Hagiographa. The Septuagint — seventy learned Jewish men sent for by Ptolemy, king of Egypt, to translate the Jewish law from Hebrew into Greek — have left us no other books for Holy Scripture in the Greek tongue than the same ones that are received in the Church of England.

The Emperor in whose reign Josephus is said to have written his reckoning of the Hebrew scriptural canon.

## modern-en · charlemagne · 43:125

When it is said the pope has not (in the territories of other states) the supreme civil power directly, we are to understand that he does not claim it, as other civil sovereigns do, from the original submission to it of those who are to be governed. For it is plain, and has already been sufficiently demonstrated in this treatise, that the right of all sovereigns is derived originally from the consent of every one of those who are to be governed — whether they who choose him, do it for their common defence against an enemy, as when they agree among themselves to appoint a man, or an assembly of men, to protect them; or whether they do it to save their lives, by submission to a conquering enemy. The pope therefore, when he disclaims the supreme civil power over other states directly, denies no more than that his right comes to him by that way. He does not for all that cease to claim it another way; and that is (without the consent of those who are to be governed) by a right given him by God (which he calls indirectly), in his assumption to the papacy. But by what way soever he claims it, the power is the same; and he may (if it is granted to be his right) depose princes and states, as often as it is for the salvation of souls — that is, as often as he will, for he claims also the sole power to judge whether it is to the salvation of men's souls or not. And this is the doctrine, not only that Bellarmine here, and many other doctors, teach in their sermons and books, but also that some councils have decreed, and the popes have decreed, and the popes have accordingly, when the occasion has served them, put in practice. For the fourth Council of Lateran, held under Pope Innocent the Third (in the third chapter De Haereticis), has this canon: 'If a king at the pope's admonition does not purge his kingdom of heretics, and being excommunicated for the same, does not make satisfaction within a year, his subjects are absolved of their obedience.' And the practice of this has been seen on various occasions: as in the deposing of Chilperic, king of France; in the translation of the Roman empire to Charlemagne; in the oppression of John, king of England; in the transferring of the kingdom of Navarre; and of late years, in the league against Henry the Third of France; and in many more occurrences. I think there are few princes who do not consider this as unjust and inconvenient. But I wish they would all resolve to be either kings or subjects. Men cannot serve two masters: they ought therefore to ease themselves, either by holding the reins of government wholly in their own hands, or by wholly delivering them into the hands of the pope, so that such men as are willing to be obedient may be protected in their obedience. For this distinction of temporal and spiritual power is but words. Power is as really divided, and as dangerously to all purposes, by sharing with another indirect power, as with a direct one. But to come now to his arguments.

Named for the translation of the Roman Empire to him, and separately as the point after which Church Canons (previously voluntary) began to be treated as binding law by Christian princes.

## modern-en · william-conqueror · 25:6

In this distribution, the first law is for the division of the land itself, in which the sovereign assigns to each man a portion according as he — and not as any subject, or any number of subjects — shall judge agreeable to equity and the common good. The children of Israel were a commonwealth in the wilderness, but lacked the commodities of the earth until they were masters of the Land of Promise; which was afterwards divided among them, not by their own discretion but by the discretion of Eleazar the priest and Joshua their general. When there were twelve tribes, made thirteen by the subdivision of the tribe of Joseph, they nevertheless made only twelve portions of the land, and assigned no land to the tribe of Levi, giving them instead the tenth part of all the fruits. So that division was therefore arbitrary. And though a people coming into possession of a land by war does not always exterminate the ancient inhabitants (as the Jews did), but leaves many, most, or all of them their estates, it is plain they afterwards hold those estates from the victor's distribution — as the people of England held all theirs from William the Conqueror.

The Norman king whose conquest is cited three times: as the origin of English land tenures, for his oath to the clergy at his coronation, and to make the point that a king's authority cannot depend on the justice of the conquest that first won his line the crown.

## modern-en · william-rufus · 30:2

Of which this is one: 'That a man to obtain a kingdom is sometimes content with less power than is necessarily required for the peace and defence of the commonwealth.' From this it follows that when the power thus set aside has, for public safety, to be resumed, the resumption has the look of an unjust act — which disposes great numbers of men, when occasion is presented, to rebel; just as the bodies of children begotten by diseased parents are subject either to untimely death or to purging the ill quality derived from their vicious conception by breaking out into boils and scabs. When kings deny themselves some such necessary power, it is not always (though sometimes) out of ignorance of what is necessary to the office they undertake, but often out of a hope to recover the same again at their pleasure. In which they do not reason well; for those who hold them to their promises shall be backed against them by foreign commonwealths, which, for the good of their own subjects, let slip few occasions to weaken the estate of their neighbours. So Thomas Becket, archbishop of Canterbury, was supported against Henry the Second by the pope — the subjection of churchmen to the commonwealth having been dispensed with by William the Conqueror at his accession, when he took an oath not to infringe the liberty of the Church. So too the barons, whose power had been raised by William Rufus (to gain their help in transferring the succession from his elder brother to himself) to a degree inconsistent with the sovereign power, were upheld in their rebellion against King John by the French. Nor does this happen in monarchy alone. For whereas the style of the ancient Roman commonwealth was 'the Senate and people of Rome,' neither the Senate nor the people claimed the whole power; and this first caused the seditions of Tiberius Gracchus, Gaius Gracchus, Lucius Saturninus, and others; and afterwards the wars between the Senate and the people, under Marius and Sulla, and again under Pompey and Caesar, leading to the extinction of their democracy and the setting up of monarchy.

William the Conqueror's son and successor, cited for buying the Barons' support, against the Church's liberties, in transferring the succession from his elder brother.

## modern-en · st-thomas-apostle · 43:55

Besides these magisterial employments in the church — namely apostles, bishops, elders, pastors, and doctors, whose calling was to proclaim Christ to the Jews and the infidels, and to direct and teach those who believed — we read in the New Testament of no other. For by the names of 'evangelists' and 'prophets' is signified not any office, but various gifts, by which various men were profitable to the church. As evangelists, by writing the life and acts of our Saviour, such as were St Matthew and St John, apostles, and St Mark and St Luke, disciples (and whoever else wrote on that subject — as St Thomas, and St Barnabas, are said to have done, though the church has not received the books that have gone under their names). And as prophets, by the gift of interpreting the Old Testament, and sometimes by declaring their special revelations to the church. The Apostle, said -- doubtfully, per Hobbes ("though the Church have not received" it) -- to have written of the Trinity alongside Barnabas; distinct from the book's other, unbound and merely rhetorical "a Thomas" and from Thomas Becket, bound separately above.

## modern-en · frederick-barbarossa · 48:19

But the emperors, and other Christian sovereigns, under whose government these errors, and the like encroachments of ecclesiastics on their office, at first crept in, to the disturbance of their possessions and of the tranquillity of their subjects, though they suffered the same for want of foresight of the sequel, and of insight into the designs of their teachers, may nevertheless be esteemed accessories to their own, and to the public, damage. For without their authority, no seditious doctrine could at first have been publicly preached. I say they might have hindered the same in the beginning; but when the people were once possessed by those spiritual men, there was no human remedy to be applied that any man could invent. And for the remedies that God should provide, who never fails in his good time to destroy all the machinations of men against the truth, we are to attend his good pleasure — who suffers many times the prosperity of his enemies, together with their ambition, to grow to such a height that the violence of it opens the eyes which the wariness of their predecessors had before sealed up, and makes men, by too much grasping, let go all — as Peter's net was broken by the struggling of too great a multitude of fishes; whereas the impatience of those who strive to resist such encroachment, before their subjects' eyes were opened, did but increase the power they resisted. I do not therefore blame the emperor Frederick for holding the stirrup to our countryman Pope Adrian — for such was the disposition of his subjects then, that if he had not done it, he was not likely to have succeeded in the empire. But I blame those who in the beginning, when their power was entire, by suffering such doctrines to be forged in the universities of their own dominions, have held the stirrup to all the succeeding popes, while they mounted into the thrones of all Christian sovereigns, to ride and tire both them and their people, at their pleasure.

Frederick I Barbarossa, whom Hobbes declines to blame for holding the stirrup for Pope Adrian at his reception, given the political necessity of the moment.
