"Our countryman" -- the only English Pope -- for whom Frederick Barbarossa held the stirrup at his imperial reception.

## modern-en · henry-viii · 31:15

As for the means and conduits by which the people may receive this instruction, we should ask by what means so many opinions, contrary to the peace of mankind, founded on weak and false principles, have nevertheless been so deeply rooted in them. I mean those opinions which I specified in the previous chapter: that men shall judge what is lawful and unlawful, not by the law itself, but by their own private judgements; that subjects sin in obeying the commands of the commonwealth, unless they themselves have first judged them to be lawful; that their property in their riches is such as to exclude the dominion which the commonwealth has over the same; that it is lawful for subjects to kill those they call tyrants; that the sovereign power may be divided; and the like. These come to be instilled into the people by this means. Those whom necessity or covetousness keeps intent on their trades and labour, and those, on the other hand, whom superfluity or sloth carries after their sensual pleasures (the two sorts who make up the greatest part of mankind), being diverted from the deep meditation which the learning of truth — not only in the matter of natural justice, but also of all other sciences — necessarily requires, receive the notions of their duty chiefly from divines in the pulpit, and partly from such of their neighbours, or familiar acquaintances, as, having the faculty of discoursing readily and plausibly, seem wiser and better learned in cases of law and conscience than themselves. And the divines, and others who make a show of learning, derive their knowledge from the universities, and from the schools of law, or from the books which by men eminent in those schools and universities have been published. It is therefore plain that the instruction of the people depends wholly on the right teaching of youth in the universities. But (some may say) are not the universities of England learned enough already to do that? Or is it you who will undertake to teach the universities? Hard questions. Yet to the first, I do not doubt to answer that until towards the latter end of Henry the Eighth, the power of the pope was always upheld against the power of the commonwealth, principally by the universities; and that the doctrines maintained by so many preachers against the sovereign power of the king, and by so many lawyers and others who had their education there, is sufficient argument that, though the universities were not the authors of those false doctrines, they did not know how to plant the true. For in such a contradiction of opinions, it is most certain that they were not sufficiently instructed; and it is no wonder if they still retain a relish of that subtle liquor with which they were first seasoned, against the civil authority. But to the latter question, it is neither fitting nor needful for me to say either yes or no: for any man who sees what I am doing may easily perceive what I think.

Named, with Elizabeth, for casting out the Pope's spiritual power in England "by his Exorcisme" -- Hobbes's own ironic figure for the Reformation.

## modern-en · elizabeth-i · 48:22

First, the power of the popes was dissolved totally by Queen Elizabeth; and the bishops, who before exercised their functions in right of the pope, did afterwards exercise the same in right of the queen and her successors — though by retaining the phrase of Jure Divino, they were thought to claim it by immediate right from God. And so was untied the first knot. After this, the Presbyterians lately in England obtained the putting down of episcopacy: and so the second knot was dissolved. And almost at the same time, the power was taken also from the Presbyterians; and so we are reduced to the independence of the primitive Christians, to follow Paul, or Cephas, or Apollos, every man as he likes best — which, if it be without contention, and without measuring the doctrine of Christ by our affection to the person of his minister (the fault which the apostle reproved in the Corinthians), is perhaps the best. First, because there ought to be no power over the consciences of men but of the word itself, working faith in every one — not always according to the purpose of those who plant and water, but of God himself, that gives the increase. And second, because it is unreasonable in those who teach that there is such danger in every little error, to require of a man endowed with reason of his own, to follow the reason of any other man, or of the most voices of many other men — which is little better than venturing his salvation on the toss of a coin. Nor ought those teachers to be displeased with this loss of their ancient authority. For there is none who should know better than they, that power is preserved by the same virtues by which it is acquired — that is, by wisdom, humility, clearness of doctrine, and sincerity of conversation; and not by the suppression of the natural sciences, and of the morality of natural reason; nor by obscure language; nor by claiming to themselves more knowledge than they make appear; nor by pious frauds; nor by such other faults as in the pastors of God's church are not only faults, but also scandals, apt to make men stumble one time or other on the suppression of their authority.

Named for totally dissolving the Pope's power in England, after which the Bishops exercised their functions in the Queen's right rather than the Pope's.

## modern-en · chilperic · 13:31

Lastly, among the points the Church of Rome declares necessary for salvation, there are so many manifestly to the advantage of the Pope and of his spiritual subjects residing in the territories of other Christian princes, that were it not for the mutual rivalry of those princes, they might without war or trouble exclude all foreign authority as easily as it has been excluded in England. For who is there that does not see whose benefit it serves to have it believed: that a king has no authority from Christ unless a bishop crowns him? That a king, if he is a priest, cannot marry? That whether a prince is born in lawful marriage or not must be judged by authority from Rome? That subjects may be freed from their allegiance if, by the Court of Rome, the king is judged a heretic? That a king (like Chilperic of France) may be deposed by a pope (like Pope Zachary) for no cause, and his kingdom given to one of his subjects? That the clergy and the regulars, in whatever country, shall be exempt from the jurisdiction of their king in criminal cases? Or who does not see to whose profit accrue the fees of private masses, and the takings of purgatory, with other signs of private interest — enough to mortify the most lively faith, if (as I said) the civil magistrate and custom did not sustain it more than any opinion they have of the sanctity, wisdom, or probity of their teachers? So that I may attribute all the changes of religion in the world to one and the same cause: displeasing priests — and not only among Catholics, but even in that church which has presumed most of reformation.

The King of France cited (via the printed "Chilperique") as deposed by Pope Zachary for no cause but the Pope's own authority -- an example of Papal usurpation over civil sovereigns.

## modern-en · bellarmine · 43:4

Cardinal Bellarmine, in his third General Controversy, has handled a great many questions concerning the ecclesiastical power of the pope of Rome; and he begins with this: whether it ought to be monarchical, aristocratic, or democratic. All these sorts of power are sovereign and coercive. If now it should appear that there is no coercive power left by our Saviour, but only a power to proclaim the kingdom of Christ, and to persuade men to submit to it; and by precepts and good counsel, to teach those who have submitted what they are to do, that they may be received into the kingdom of God when it comes; and that the apostles, and other ministers of the gospel, are our schoolmasters, and not our commanders, and their precepts not laws, but wholesome counsels — then all that dispute is in vain.

The Catholic controversialist whose arguments for the Pope's temporal power and for Purgatory Hobbes answers at great length across chapters 42-45 and 48, engaging his specific citations point by point.

## modern-en · beza · 45:16

Let us now consider what texts of Scripture seem most to confirm these three general errors I have here touched on. As for those which Cardinal Bellarmine has alleged for the present kingdom of God administered by the pope (than which there are none that make a better show of proof), I have already answered them; and made it plain that the kingdom of God, instituted by Moses, ended in the election of Saul. After which time the priest, of his own authority, never deposed any king. What the high priest did to Athaliah was not done in his own right, but in the right of the young king Joash her son. But Solomon, in his own right, deposed the high priest Abiathar, and set up another in his place. The most difficult passage to answer, of all those that can be brought to prove the kingdom of God by Christ is already in this world, is alleged not by Bellarmine, nor any other of the Church of Rome, but by Beza — who will have it to begin from the resurrection of Christ. But whether he intends by this to entitle the presbytery to the supreme power ecclesiastical in the commonwealth of Geneva (and consequently in every presbytery in every other commonwealth), or to entitle princes and other civil sovereigns to it, I do not know. For the presbytery has claimed the power to excommunicate their own kings, and to be the supreme moderators in religion, in the places where they have that form of church government, no less than the pope claims it universally.

The Reformed theologian who, alone among the authorities Hobbes canvasses, argues Christ's Kingdom began at the Resurrection -- a claim Hobbes spends a full chapter refuting.

## modern-en · ambrose · 43:137

His sixth, and last, argument is from examples. To which I answer, first, that examples prove nothing; second, that the examples he alleges do not even amount to a probability of right. The fact of Jehoiada in killing Athaliah (2 Kings 11) was either by the authority of King Joash, or it was a horrible crime in the high priest — who (ever after the election of King Saul) was a mere subject. The fact of St Ambrose in excommunicating Theodosius the emperor (if it is true he did so) was a capital crime. And for the popes — Gregory I, Gregory II, Zachary, and Leo III — their judgements are void, as given in their own cause; and the acts done by them in keeping with this doctrine are the greatest crimes (especially that of Zachary) that are incident to human nature. And thus much of power ecclesiastical, wherein I had been more brief, forbearing to examine these arguments of Bellarmine, if they had been his as a private man, and not as the champion of the papacy, against all other Christian princes and states.

Cited for excommunicating the Emperor Theodosius, an episode Hobbes treats with some scepticism as to whether it truly happened.

## modern-en · jerome · 34:0

By the books of Holy SCRIPTURE are meant those that ought to be the canon — that is, the rules — of Christian life. And because all the rules of life that men are in conscience bound to observe are laws, the question of Scripture is the question of what is law throughout all Christendom, both natural and civil. For though Scripture does not determine what laws each Christian king shall constitute in his own dominions, it does determine what laws he shall not constitute. Since therefore I have already proved that sovereigns in their own dominions are the sole legislators, only those books are canonical — that is, law — in every nation which are established as such by the sovereign authority. It is true that God is the sovereign of all sovereigns, and so, when he speaks to any subject, he ought to be obeyed, whatever any earthly potentate commands to the contrary. But the question is not of obedience to God, but of when, and what, God has said. To subjects who have no supernatural revelation, this can be known only by that natural reason which guided them, for the obtaining of peace and justice, to obey the authority of their several commonwealths — that is, of their lawful sovereigns. According to this obligation, I can acknowledge no other books of the Old Testament to be Holy Scripture except those that have been commanded to be acknowledged as such by the authority of the Church of England. What books these are is well enough known without setting them all out here; and they are the same as those acknowledged by St Jerome — who holds the rest (namely, the Wisdom of Solomon, Ecclesiasticus, Judith, Tobias, the first and second of Maccabees — though he had seen the first in Hebrew — and the third and fourth of Esdras) for Apocrypha. Of the canonical, Josephus, a learned Jew who wrote in the time of the Emperor Domitian, reckons twenty-two, making the number agree with the Hebrew alphabet. St Jerome does the same, though they reckon them differently. For Josephus numbers five books of Moses, thirteen of prophets who wrote the history of their own times (how that agrees with the prophetic writings contained in the Bible we shall see hereafter), and four of hymns and moral precepts. But St Jerome reckons five books of Moses, eight of prophets, and nine of other holy writings, which he calls Hagiographa. The Septuagint — seventy learned Jewish men sent for by Ptolemy, king of Egypt, to translate the Jewish law from Hebrew into Greek — have left us no other books for Holy Scripture in the Greek tongue than the same ones that are received in the Church of England.

Cited repeatedly in the survey of the Old Testament canon (ch. 34) for his reckoning of the number of scriptural books, and for testifying that Job's original Hebrew was written in prose rather than verse.

## modern-en · cyprian · 43:118

The third is from this: that the See of St Peter is called by St Cyprian the Head, the Source, the Root, the Sun, from which the authority of bishops is derived. But by the law of nature (which is a better principle of right and wrong than the word of any doctor that is but a man), the civil sovereign in every commonwealth is the head, the source, the root, and the sun, from which all jurisdiction is derived. And therefore the jurisdiction of bishops is derived from the civil sovereign.

Cited for calling the See of St. Peter "the Head, the Source, the Roote, the Sun" of the Church -- part of Hobbes's survey of the textual basis claimed for Papal supremacy.

## modern-en · augustine · 45:29

In the other passages which he alleges out of the Old Testament, there is not so much as any show or colour of proof. He brings in every text in which there is the word 'anger,' or 'fire,' or 'burning,' or 'purging,' or 'cleansing,' in case any of the Fathers has but in a sermon rhetorically applied it to the doctrine of purgatory, already believed. The first verse of Psalm 37: 'O Lord, rebuke me not in your wrath, nor chasten me in your hot displeasure.' What were this to purgatory, if Augustine had not applied the 'wrath' to the fire of hell, and the 'displeasure' to that of purgatory? And what is it to purgatory, that of Psalm 66:12, 'We went through fire and water, and you brought us to a moist place'; and other the like texts (with which the doctors of those times intended to adorn, or extend, their sermons or commentaries), dragged to their purposes by force of wit?

Cited as the source of the rhetorical application of Psalm 37 ("rebuke me not in thy wrath") to the doctrine of Purgatory -- an example, per Hobbes, of a text stretched to a use its own words do not support.

## modern-en · st-bernard · 47:40

Lastly, as for the errors brought in from false or uncertain history, what is the whole catalogue of fictitious miracles in the lives of the saints, and all the accounts of apparitions and departed spirits, alleged by the doctors of the Roman Church to support their doctrines of Hell and Purgatory, the power of exorcism, and other doctrines that have no warrant in either reason or Scripture? And what too of those traditions they call the unwritten word of God, but which are old wives' tales? Some bits of these can indeed be found scattered in the writings of the ancient Fathers, but those Fathers were people who could too easily believe false reports; and producing their opinions as testimony of the truth of what they believed has no greater force with those who (according to the counsel of 1 John 4:1) examine spirits, than this: in any matter touching the power of the Roman Church (the abuse of which they either did not suspect, or stood to benefit from), it discredits their testimony, by their too ready belief in such reports—since the most sincere people, without much knowledge of natural causes (as the Fathers were), are commonly the most prone to such credulity. The best people are naturally the least suspicious of fraudulent intentions. Pope Gregory and St. Bernard have something to say about apparitions of departed spirits who said they were in Purgatory, and our own Bede does as well; but nowhere, I believe, except by report from others. If they, or anyone else, were to relate such stories of their own direct knowledge, they would not thereby confirm such empty reports the more; they would only reveal their own weakness, or deception.

Named alongside Pope Gregory as having "somewhat of Apparitions of Ghosts" reportedly seen in Purgatory -- cited, with reservation, among the sincere but overly credulous Fathers whose reported testimony Hobbes treats as report-of-report rather than eyewitness fact.

## modern-en · clement-i · 34:19

The writers of the New Testament all lived less than an age after Christ's ascension, and had all seen our Saviour, or been his disciples, except St Paul and St Luke; and consequently whatever was written by them is as ancient as the time of the apostles. But the time at which the books of the New Testament were received and acknowledged by the Church as their writing is not altogether so ancient. For as the books of the Old Testament come to us from no higher time than that of Ezra, who by the direction of God's Spirit retrieved them when they were lost, so the books of the New Testament — copies of which were not many, nor could easily be all in any one private man's hand — cannot be derived from a higher time than that at which the governors of the Church collected, approved, and recommended them to us as the writings of those apostles and disciples under whose names they go. The first enumeration of all the books, both of the Old and New Testament, is in the Canons of the Apostles, supposed to have been collected by Clement, the first (after St Peter) bishop of Rome. But because that is only supposed, and by many questioned, the Council of Laodicea is the first we know of that recommended the Bible to the then-Christian churches as the writings of the prophets and apostles; and this council was held in the 364th year after Christ. At which time, though ambition had so far prevailed on the great doctors of the Church that they no longer esteemed emperors (even Christian ones) as the shepherds of the people, but as sheep; and emperors who were not Christian as wolves; and they endeavoured to pass their doctrine, not as counsel and information from preachers, but as laws from absolute governors; and thought such frauds as tended to make the people the more obedient to Christian doctrine to be pious — yet I am persuaded they did not on that account falsify the Scriptures, even though the copies of the books of the New Testament were in the hands only of the ecclesiastics. For if they had had any such intention, they would surely have made them more favourable to their power over Christian princes, and to civil sovereignty, than they are. I see therefore no reason to doubt that the Old and New Testaments, as we have them now, are the true records of what was done and said by the prophets and apostles. And so perhaps are some of those books which are called Apocrypha, if left out of the canon not for any disagreement of doctrine with the rest, but only because they are not found in the Hebrew. For after the conquest of Asia by Alexander the Great, there were few learned Jews who were not perfect in the Greek tongue. The seventy interpreters who turned the Bible into Greek were all of them Hebrews; and we have extant the works of Philo and Josephus, both Jews, written by them eloquently in Greek. But it is not the writer, but the authority of the Church, that makes a book canonical.

Credited with having collected the Apostolical Canons, in Hobbes's survey of who assembled the New Testament canon.

## modern-en · pope-innocent-iii · 43:125

When it is said the pope has not (in the territories of other states) the supreme civil power directly, we are to understand that he does not claim it, as other civil sovereigns do, from the original submission to it of those who are to be governed. For it is plain, and has already been sufficiently demonstrated in this treatise, that the right of all sovereigns is derived originally from the consent of every one of those who are to be governed — whether they who choose him, do it for their common defence against an enemy, as when they agree among themselves to appoint a man, or an assembly of men, to protect them; or whether they do it to save their lives, by submission to a conquering enemy. The pope therefore, when he disclaims the supreme civil power over other states directly, denies no more than that his right comes to him by that way. He does not for all that cease to claim it another way; and that is (without the consent of those who are to be governed) by a right given him by God (which he calls indirectly), in his assumption to the papacy. But by what way soever he claims it, the power is the same; and he may (if it is granted to be his right) depose princes and states, as often as it is for the salvation of souls — that is, as often as he will, for he claims also the sole power to judge whether it is to the salvation of men's souls or not. And this is the doctrine, not only that Bellarmine here, and many other doctors, teach in their sermons and books, but also that some councils have decreed, and the popes have decreed, and the popes have accordingly, when the occasion has served them, put in practice. For the fourth Council of Lateran, held under Pope Innocent the Third (in the third chapter De Haereticis), has this canon: 'If a king at the pope's admonition does not purge his kingdom of heretics, and being excommunicated for the same, does not make satisfaction within a year, his subjects are absolved of their obedience.' And the practice of this has been seen on various occasions: as in the deposing of Chilperic, king of France; in the translation of the Roman empire to Charlemagne; in the oppression of John, king of England; in the transferring of the kingdom of Navarre; and of late years, in the league against Henry the Third of France; and in many more occurrences. I think there are few princes who do not consider this as unjust and inconvenient. But I wish they would all resolve to be either kings or subjects. Men cannot serve two masters: they ought therefore to ease themselves, either by holding the reins of government wholly in their own hands, or by wholly delivering them into the hands of the pope, so that such men as are willing to be obedient may be protected in their obedience. For this distinction of temporal and spiritual power is but words. Power is as really divided, and as dangerously to all purposes, by sharing with another indirect power, as with a direct one. But to come now to his arguments.

Named by full title, "Innocent the third," for the Fourth Lateran Council's decree that subjects are absolved of obedience to a king who fails to purge heresy, and for establishing the doctrine of Transubstantiation "not above 500. years agoe." The book's many other, unrelated uses of the common adjective "innocent" are left unbound -- see the namesake note above.

## modern-en · pope-leo-iii · 43:137

His sixth, and last, argument is from examples. To which I answer, first, that examples prove nothing; second, that the examples he alleges do not even amount to a probability of right. The fact of Jehoiada in killing Athaliah (2 Kings 11) was either by the authority of King Joash, or it was a horrible crime in the high priest — who (ever after the election of King Saul) was a mere subject. The fact of St Ambrose in excommunicating Theodosius the emperor (if it is true he did so) was a capital crime. And for the popes — Gregory I, Gregory II, Zachary, and Leo III — their judgements are void, as given in their own cause; and the acts done by them in keeping with this doctrine are the greatest crimes (especially that of Zachary) that are incident to human nature. And thus much of power ecclesiastical, wherein I had been more brief, forbearing to examine these arguments of Bellarmine, if they had been his as a private man, and not as the champion of the papacy, against all other Christian princes and states.

Named by explicit numeral, "Leo 3," in a list of Popes -- Gregory 1, Greg. 2, Zachary, and Leo 3 -- whose judgments in their own cause Hobbes holds void.

## modern-en · pope-zachary · 13:31

Lastly, among the points the Church of Rome declares necessary for salvation, there are so many manifestly to the advantage of the Pope and of his spiritual subjects residing in the territories of other Christian princes, that were it not for the mutual rivalry of those princes, they might without war or trouble exclude all foreign authority as easily as it has been excluded in England. For who is there that does not see whose benefit it serves to have it believed: that a king has no authority from Christ unless a bishop crowns him? That a king, if he is a priest, cannot marry? That whether a prince is born in lawful marriage or not must be judged by authority from Rome? That subjects may be freed from their allegiance if, by the Court of Rome, the king is judged a heretic? That a king (like Chilperic of France) may be deposed by a pope (like Pope Zachary) for no cause, and his kingdom given to one of his subjects? That the clergy and the regulars, in whatever country, shall be exempt from the jurisdiction of their king in criminal cases? Or who does not see to whose profit accrue the fees of private masses, and the takings of purgatory, with other signs of private interest — enough to mortify the most lively faith, if (as I said) the civil magistrate and custom did not sustain it more than any opinion they have of the sanctity, wisdom, or probity of their teachers? So that I may attribute all the changes of religion in the world to one and the same cause: displeasing priests — and not only among Catholics, but even in that church which has presumed most of reformation.

The eighth-century Pope who deposed Chilperic, King of France, for no cause but his own authority; also appears bare in the same numbered list of Popes as Leo III.

## modern-en · jupiter · 11:44

Nor does it change the question of honour whether an action — provided it is great and difficult, and so a sign of much power — is just or unjust. Honour consists only in the opinion of power. So the ancient heathens did not think they were dishonouring their gods, but rather greatly honouring them, when they introduced them in their poems as committing rapes, thefts, and other great but unjust or unclean acts. Nothing is so much celebrated in Jupiter as his adulteries; nor in Mercury as his frauds and thefts. The greatest of Mercury's praises in a hymn of Homer is that, being born in the morning, he had invented music by noon, and before nightfall had stolen the cattle of Apollo from his herdsmen.

Cited repeatedly as chief of the Heathen gods -- for his adulteries celebrated rather than dishonoured in the Poets, for deposing his father Saturn, and as the god invoked in the Heathen oath-form Hobbes contrasts with the Christian one.

## modern-en · mercury · 11:44

So the ancient heathens did not think they were dishonouring their gods, but rather greatly honouring them, when they introduced them in their poems as committing rapes, thefts, and other great but unjust or unclean acts. The greatest of Mercury's praises in a hymn of Homer is that, being born in the morning, he had invented music by noon, and before nightfall had stolen the cattle of Apollo from his herdsmen.

Cited for his frauds and thefts celebrated by the Poets (per Homer's hymn), and as the god to whom Craft and Subtilty were ascribed.

## modern-en · venus · 13:16

The same authors of the religion of the Gentiles, observing the second ground of religion — namely men's ignorance of causes, and their consequent readiness to attribute their fortune to causes on which there was no apparent dependence — took the opportunity to foist on this ignorance, in place of secondary causes, a kind of second-rank, ministering gods: ascribing fertility to Venus, the arts to Apollo, subtlety and craft to Mercury, tempests and storms to Aeolus, and other effects to other gods — so that among the heathen there was almost as great a variety of gods as of occupations.

Cited as the deity to whom the Gentiles ascribed the cause of Foecundity.

## modern-en · apollo · 9:24

There was once a great gathering of people in Abdera, a city of the Greeks, at the performance of the tragedy of Andromeda, on an extremely hot day. As a result, a great many of the spectators, falling into fevers, suffered this peculiar consequence from the heat and the tragedy together: they did nothing but recite iambic verses, with the names of Perseus and Andromeda. This, along with the fever, was cured by the coming on of winter. And the madness was thought to come from the passion imprinted by the tragedy. Likewise a fit of madness once raged in another Greek city, seizing only the young maidens, and causing many of them to hang themselves. Most at the time thought this an act of the devil. But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

Cited (also as Phoebus and Appollo) as the god of Arts, whose cattle Mercury stole per Homer's hymn, and whose oracle at Delphi is named among the famous ambiguous Oracles.

## modern-en · aeolus · 13:16

The same authors of the religion of the Gentiles, observing the second ground of religion — namely men's ignorance of causes, and their consequent readiness to attribute their fortune to causes on which there was no apparent dependence — took the opportunity to foist on this ignorance, in place of secondary causes, a kind of second-rank, ministering gods: ascribing fertility to Venus, the arts to Apollo, subtlety and craft to Mercury, tempests and storms to Aeolus, and other effects to other gods — so that among the heathen there was almost as great a variety of gods as of occupations.

Cited as the deity to whom the Gentiles ascribed Tempests and storms.

## modern-en · bacchus · 13:17

And to the worship which men naturally thought fit to use toward their gods — namely oblations, prayers, thanks, and the rest already named — the same legislators of the Gentiles added images of them, both in painting and in sculpture, so that the more ignorant sort (that is, the greater part of the people), thinking that the gods the images represented were really included, as it were housed within them, might stand all the more in fear of them. They endowed them with lands, houses, officers, and revenues, set apart from all other human uses — that is, consecrated and made holy to these idols — such as caverns, groves, woods, mountains, and whole islands. And they attributed to them not only shapes (of men, of beasts, of monsters), but also the faculties and passions of men and beasts: sense, speech, sex, lust, generation (and this not only by mixing with one another to propagate the kind of gods, but also by mixing with men and women to beget mongrel gods, mere lodgers in heaven, such as Bacchus, Hercules, and others); besides anger, revenge, and other passions of living creatures, and the actions proceeding from them — fraud, theft, adultery, sodomy, and any vice that may be taken either as an effect of power or as a cause of pleasure; and all such vices as, among men, are considered to be against law rather than against honor.

Named among the "mongrill Gods" the Gentiles believed born of a god and a mortal.

## modern-en · hercules-myth · 3:3

Much memory, or memory of many things, is called Experience. Again, Imagination is only of those things that have formerly been perceived by sense, either all at once or in parts at several times. The first (imagining the whole object as it was presented to the sense) is Simple Imagination, as when one imagines a man or a horse one has seen before. The other is Compound, as when, from the sight of a man at one time, and of a horse at another, we conceive in our mind a centaur. So when a man compounds the image of his own person with the image of the actions of another man—as when a man imagines himself a Hercules, or an Alexander (which often happens to those much taken with the reading of Romances)—it is a compound imagination, and properly nothing but a fiction of the mind. There are also other imaginations that rise in men (though waking) from the great impression made in sense; as from gazing on the sun, the impression leaves an image of the sun before our eyes a long time after; and from being long and intently fixed on geometrical figures, a man in the dark (though awake) will have the images of lines and angles before his eyes—a kind of fancy that has no particular name, being something that does not commonly enter into men's discourse.

Named among the deified mortals of Gentile religion, and separately cited for his fight with the many-headed Hydra as an image of a contentious multitude that only grows for every grievance answered.

## modern-en · saturn · 16:3

The fool has said in his heart that there is no such thing as justice — and sometimes also out loud — seriously claiming that since every man's preservation and contentment is committed to his own care, there could be no reason why every man should not do whatever he thinks conduces to it, and therefore that to make or not make, keep or not keep covenants is not against reason when it conduces to one's own benefit. He does not in this deny that there are covenants, that they are sometimes broken and sometimes kept, and that such breach may be called injustice and the observance of them justice. But he asks whether injustice, taking away the fear of God (for the same fool has said in his heart there is no God), may not sometimes stand with that reason which dictates to every man his own good — particularly when it conduces to such a benefit as will put a man in a condition to disregard not only the disapproval and reviling of others but also their power. The kingdom of God is taken by violence — but what if it could be taken by unjust violence? Would it be against reason so to take it, when it is impossible to be hurt by doing so? And if it is not against reason, it is not against justice; otherwise justice is not to be approved as good. From reasoning of this sort, successful wickedness has gotten the name of virtue; and some who in all other things have disallowed the violation of faith have allowed it when it is for the gaining of a kingdom. And the heathen who believed that Saturn was deposed by his son Jupiter still believed the same Jupiter to be the avenger of injustice — somewhat like a piece of law in Coke's Commentaries on Littleton, where he says that if the rightful heir to the crown is attainted of treason, yet the crown shall descend to him, and Eo Instante (at that very instant) the attainder is void. From such examples a man would be very ready to infer that when the heir apparent of a kingdom kills the one in possession — even his own father — you may call it injustice, or by whatever name you like, but it can never be against reason, since all voluntary actions of men aim at their own benefit, and those actions are most reasonable which most conduce to their ends. This specious reasoning is nevertheless false.

Cited for the Heathen belief that he was deposed by his own son Jupiter -- an example, per Hobbes, of successful wickedness gaining the name of virtue.

## modern-en · pan · 7:35

Panic Terror — fear, without an apprehension of why or what, is PANIC TERROR, so named from the fables that make Pan its author. In truth there is always, in the man so frightened, some apprehension of the cause, while the rest of the crowd runs away by example — each supposing his neighbour to know why. This passion therefore arises only in a crowd or multitude of people.

The god from whose fables "Panique Terrour" -- fear without any apprehended cause -- takes its name; also named among the spirits believed to haunt the plains.

## modern-en · neptune · 46:17

But in a larger use of the word 'image' is contained also any representation of one thing by another. So an earthly sovereign may be called the image of God, and an inferior magistrate, the image of an earthly sovereign. And many times in the idolatry of the Gentiles there was little regard to the similitude of their material idol to the idol in their fancy, and yet it was called the image of it. For a stone unhewn has been set up for Neptune, and various other shapes far different from the shapes they conceived of their gods. And at this day we see many images of the Virgin Mary, and other saints, unlike one another, and without correspondence to any one man's fancy; and yet they serve well enough for the purpose they were erected for — which was no more than by the names only to represent the persons mentioned in the history, to which every man applies a mental image of his own making, or none at all. And so an image, in the largest sense, is either the resemblance, or the representation, of some thing visible — or both together, as it happens for the most part.

Cited as an example of a crude, unhewn stone set up to represent a god the worshippers' own fancy pictured quite differently.

## modern-en · chaos-deity · 13:13

The unformed matter of the world was a god, under the name of Chaos.

The unformed matter of the world, worshipped by the Gentiles under this name as their oldest god.

## modern-en · charon · 13:15

Men, women, a bird, a crocodile, a calf, a dog, a snake, an onion, a leek were all deified. Besides, they filled almost every place with spirits called daemons: the plains with Pan and the Panises, or Satyrs; the woods with Fauns and Nymphs; the sea with Tritons and other nymphs; every river and fountain with a ghost of its own name, and with nymphs; every house with its Lares, or household spirits; every man with his Genius; hell with ghosts and spiritual officers such as Charon, Cerberus, and the Furies; and at night every place with Larvae, Lemures, ghosts of the dead, and a whole kingdom of fairies and bugbears. They also ascribed divinity, and built temples, to mere accidents and qualities — such as Time, Night, Day, Peace, Concord, Love, Strife, Virtue, Honor, Health, Rust, Fever, and the like — which, when they prayed for them or against them, they prayed to as if there were ghosts of those names hovering over their heads, letting fall or withholding the good or evil for or against which they prayed. They also invoked their own wit, by the name of the Muses; their own ignorance, by the name of Fortune; their own lust, by the name of Cupid; their own rage, by the name of the Furies; their own private parts, by the name of Priapus; and they attributed their pollutions to Incubi and Succubae — so that there was nothing a poet could introduce as a character in his poem that they did not make into either a god or a devil.

Named among Hell's "spirituall Officers" of Gentile religion -- ferryman of the dead.

## modern-en · cerberus · 13:15

Men, women, a bird, a crocodile, a calf, a dog, a snake, an onion, a leek were all deified. Besides, they filled almost every place with spirits called daemons: the plains with Pan and the Panises, or Satyrs; the woods with Fauns and Nymphs; the sea with Tritons and other nymphs; every river and fountain with a ghost of its own name, and with nymphs; every house with its Lares, or household spirits; every man with his Genius; hell with ghosts and spiritual officers such as Charon, Cerberus, and the Furies; and at night every place with Larvae, Lemures, ghosts of the dead, and a whole kingdom of fairies and bugbears. They also ascribed divinity, and built temples, to mere accidents and qualities — such as Time, Night, Day, Peace, Concord, Love, Strife, Virtue, Honor, Health, Rust, Fever, and the like — which, when they prayed for them or against them, they prayed to as if there were ghosts of those names hovering over their heads, letting fall or withholding the good or evil for or against which they prayed. They also invoked their own wit, by the name of the Muses; their own ignorance, by the name of Fortune; their own lust, by the name of Cupid; their own rage, by the name of the Furies; their own private parts, by the name of Priapus; and they attributed their pollutions to Incubi and Succubae — so that there was nothing a poet could introduce as a character in his poem that they did not make into either a god or a devil.

The three-headed hound of Gentile Hell, named alongside Charon and the Furies as one of Hell's spiritual officers.

## modern-en · hydra-monster · 31:25

In the same way, it belongs to the office and duty of the sovereign to apply his rewards always in such a way that benefit to the commonwealth may arise from them — in which consists their use and end; and this is done when those who have well served the commonwealth are, with as little expense of the common treasure as is possible, so well recompensed that others may be encouraged both to serve the same as faithfully as they can, and to study the arts by which they may be enabled to do it better. To buy with money or with preferment, from a popular ambitious subject, his being quiet and desisting from making ill impressions on the people's minds, has nothing of the nature of reward (which is ordained not for disservice but for past service), nor any sign of gratitude, but of fear; nor does it tend to the benefit but to the damage of the public. It is a contest with ambition, like that of Hercules with the monster Hydra, who, having many heads, for every one that was vanquished, three more sprang up. For in the same way, when the stubbornness of one popular man is overcome with reward, many more arise (by the example) who do the same mischief in the hope of like benefit. And as all sorts of manufacture, so also malice increases by being made vendible. And though sometimes a civil war may be deferred by such ways as that, the danger nevertheless grows greater, and the public ruin more assured. It is therefore against the duty of the sovereign, to whom the public safety is committed, to reward those who aspire to greatness by disturbing the peace of their country, and not rather to oppose the beginnings of such men with a little danger, than after a longer time with greater.

The many-headed monster whose fight with Hercules Hobbes uses as an image of a grievance-driven multitude that grows a new head for every one struck off.

## modern-en · moloch · 39:13

Lastly, whereas there was a place near Jerusalem called the Valley of the Children of Hinnom — in a part of which, called Tophet, the Jews had committed most grievous idolatry, sacrificing their children to the idol Moloch; and in which also God had afflicted his enemies with most grievous punishments; and in which Josiah had burnt the priests of Moloch on their own altars, as appears at large in 2 Kings chapter 23 — that place served afterwards to receive the filth and garbage which was carried there out of the city; and there fires used to be made, from time to time, to purify the air and take away the stench of carrion. From this abominable place, the Jews used ever after to call the place of the damned by the name of Gehenna, or Valley of Hinnom. And this Gehenna is the word that is usually now translated HELL; and from the fires from time to time burning there, we get the notion of 'everlasting and unquenchable fire.'

Named among the deities to whom the Gentiles gave human and animal shapes and passions alike.

## modern-en · beelzebub · 9:24

There was once a great gathering of people in Abdera, a city of the Greeks, at the performance of the tragedy of Andromeda, on an extremely hot day. As a result, a great many of the spectators, falling into fevers, suffered this peculiar consequence from the heat and the tragedy together: they did nothing but recite iambic verses, with the names of Perseus and Andromeda. This, along with the fever, was cured by the coming on of winter. And the madness was thought to come from the passion imprinted by the tragedy. Likewise a fit of madness once raged in another Greek city, seizing only the young maidens, and causing many of them to hang themselves. Most at the time thought this an act of the devil. But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

"The Prince of Daemons" per Scripture -- Hobbes's own name for the single universal king he compares to the Fairies' legendary King Oberon, and cited earlier as "the Prince of the Power of the Air" ruling the Kingdome of Darknesse.

## modern-en · king-oberon · 48:25

The fairies, in whatever nation they keep company, have but one universal king, which some poets of ours call King Oberon; but the Scripture calls Beelzebub, prince of daemons. The ecclesiastics likewise, in whatever dominions they are found, acknowledge but one universal king, the pope.

The Fairies' legendary universal king in English folklore, to whom Hobbes compares Beelzebub as Prince of Daemons in his extended satire equating the Papacy's hierarchy to the Kingdome of Fairies.

## modern-en · thirty-tyrants-athens · 47:34

From Aristotle's civil philosophy they have learned to call every form of commonwealth other than the popular one (such as Athens was at that time) a tyranny. All kings they called tyrants; and the aristocracy of the thirty governors set up there by the Lacedaemonians who subdued them, the Thirty Tyrants. They have likewise learned to call the condition of the people under democracy liberty. A tyrant originally meant nothing more than a monarch; but when, in most parts of Greece, that kind of government was afterwards abolished, the name began to signify not only what it did before, but also the hatred which the popular states bore toward it. The name of king also became odious after the deposing of the kings of Rome, since it is natural to all people to suppose that some great fault is being signified by any title used in spite, and against a great enemy. And when the same people become displeased with those who administer a democracy or aristocracy, they have no trouble finding disgraceful names to express their anger: they readily call the one Anarchy and the other Oligarchy, or the Tyranny of a Few. What offends the people is nothing other than the fact that they are governed not as each of them would govern himself, but as the public representative—whether one man or an assembly of men—thinks fit; that is, by an arbitrary government, for which they give bad names to their superiors, never realizing (until perhaps a little after a civil war) that without such arbitrary government, such war must be perpetual; and that it is men and arms, not words and promises, that give the force and power of the laws.

The Spartan-installed oligarchy at Athens, cited as an example of how the name "Tyranny" came to mean not just monarchy but monarchy hated by the people who lived under it.

## modern-en · septuagint · 34:0

By the books of Holy SCRIPTURE are meant those that ought to be the canon — that is, the rules — of Christian life. And because all the rules of life that men are in conscience bound to observe are laws, the question of Scripture is the question of what is law throughout all Christendom, both natural and civil. For though Scripture does not determine what laws each Christian king shall constitute in his own dominions, it does determine what laws he shall not constitute. Since therefore I have already proved that sovereigns in their own dominions are the sole legislators, only those books are canonical — that is, law — in every nation which are established as such by the sovereign authority. It is true that God is the sovereign of all sovereigns, and so, when he speaks to any subject, he ought to be obeyed, whatever any earthly potentate commands to the contrary. But the question is not of obedience to God, but of when, and what, God has said. To subjects who have no supernatural revelation, this can be known only by that natural reason which guided them, for the obtaining of peace and justice, to obey the authority of their several commonwealths — that is, of their lawful sovereigns. According to this obligation, I can acknowledge no other books of the Old Testament to be Holy Scripture except those that have been commanded to be acknowledged as such by the authority of the Church of England. What books these are is well enough known without setting them all out here; and they are the same as those acknowledged by St Jerome — who holds the rest (namely, the Wisdom of Solomon, Ecclesiasticus, Judith, Tobias, the first and second of Maccabees — though he had seen the first in Hebrew — and the third and fourth of Esdras) for Apocrypha. Of the canonical, Josephus, a learned Jew who wrote in the time of the Emperor Domitian, reckons twenty-two, making the number agree with the Hebrew alphabet. St Jerome does the same, though they reckon them differently. For Josephus numbers five books of Moses, thirteen of prophets who wrote the history of their own times (how that agrees with the prophetic writings contained in the Bible we shall see hereafter), and four of hymns and moral precepts. But St Jerome reckons five books of Moses, eight of prophets, and nine of other holy writings, which he calls Hagiographa. The Septuagint — seventy learned Jewish men sent for by Ptolemy, king of Egypt, to translate the Jewish law from Hebrew into Greek — have left us no other books for Holy Scripture in the Greek tongue than the same ones that are received in the Church of England.

The seventy Jewish scholars Hobbes credits with translating the Hebrew scriptures into Greek at the order of Ptolemy Philadelphus.

## modern-en · ptolemy-philadelphus · 34:18

When Joel and Malachi prophesied is not evident from their writings. But considering the inscriptions, or titles, of their books, it is plain enough that the whole Scripture of the Old Testament was set forth in the form we have it after the return of the Jews from their captivity in Babylon, and before the time of Ptolemy Philadelphus, who had it translated into Greek by seventy men sent to him out of Judea for that purpose. And if the books of Apocrypha (which the Church recommends to us, though not as canonical, yet as profitable books for our instruction) may on this point be credited, the Scripture was set forth in the form we have by Ezra; as may appear from what he himself says in 2 Esdras 14:21–22 (etc.), where, speaking to God, he says, 'Your law is burnt; therefore no man knows the things you have done, or the works that are to begin. But if I have found grace before you, send down the holy Spirit into me, and I shall write all that has been done in the world since the beginning, which were written in your law, that men may find your path, and that those who shall live in the latter days may live.' And verse 45: 'And it came to pass, when the forty days were fulfilled, that the Highest spoke, saying, "The first that you have written, publish openly, that the worthy and unworthy may read it; but keep the seventy last, that you may deliver them only to such as are wise among the people."' And this much concerning the time of the writing of the books of the Old Testament.

The Egyptian king who, per Hobbes, caused the Hebrew scriptures to be translated into Greek by the Septuagint's seventy scholars.

## modern-en · philo · 34:19

The writers of the New Testament all lived less than an age after Christ's ascension, and had all seen our Saviour, or been his disciples, except St Paul and St Luke; and consequently whatever was written by them is as ancient as the time of the apostles. But the time at which the books of the New Testament were received and acknowledged by the Church as their writing is not altogether so ancient. For as the books of the Old Testament come to us from no higher time than that of Ezra, who by the direction of God's Spirit retrieved them when they were lost, so the books of the New Testament — copies of which were not many, nor could easily be all in any one private man's hand — cannot be derived from a higher time than that at which the governors of the Church collected, approved, and recommended them to us as the writings of those apostles and disciples under whose names they go. The first enumeration of all the books, both of the Old and New Testament, is in the Canons of the Apostles, supposed to have been collected by Clement, the first (after St Peter) bishop of Rome. But because that is only supposed, and by many questioned, the Council of Laodicea is the first we know of that recommended the Bible to the then-Christian churches as the writings of the prophets and apostles; and this council was held in the 364th year after Christ. At which time, though ambition had so far prevailed on the great doctors of the Church that they no longer esteemed emperors (even Christian ones) as the shepherds of the people, but as sheep; and emperors who were not Christian as wolves; and they endeavoured to pass their doctrine, not as counsel and information from preachers, but as laws from absolute governors; and thought such frauds as tended to make the people the more obedient to Christian doctrine to be pious — yet I am persuaded they did not on that account falsify the Scriptures, even though the copies of the books of the New Testament were in the hands only of the ecclesiastics. For if they had had any such intention, they would surely have made them more favourable to their power over Christian princes, and to civil sovereignty, than they are. I see therefore no reason to doubt that the Old and New Testaments, as we have them now, are the true records of what was done and said by the prophets and apostles. And so perhaps are some of those books which are called Apocrypha, if left out of the canon not for any disagreement of doctrine with the rest, but only because they are not found in the Hebrew. For after the conquest of Asia by Alexander the Great, there were few learned Jews who were not perfect in the Greek tongue. The seventy interpreters who turned the Bible into Greek were all of them Hebrews; and we have extant the works of Philo and Josephus, both Jews, written by them eloquently in Greek. But it is not the writer, but the authority of the Church, that makes a book canonical.

The Jewish philosopher named alongside Josephus as an example of a Hebrew who wrote eloquently in Greek.

## modern-en · adam · 5:0

Printing, though an ingenious invention, is no great matter compared with the invention of letters. Who first discovered the use of letters is unknown. The man who first brought them into Greece, men say, was Cadmus, son of Agenor, king of Phoenicia. It was a useful invention for preserving the memory of times past and for binding together a mankind dispersed across so many distant regions of the earth — and a difficult one too, since it required close observation of the various motions of the tongue, palate, lips, and the other organs of speech, in order to mark each one with its own character. But the most noble and useful invention of all was speech itself, made up of names (or appellations) and their connection, by which men record their thoughts, recall them when they are past, and declare them to one another for mutual use and conversation. Without speech there would have been no commonwealth, no society, no contract, and no peace among men — no more than there is among lions, bears, and wolves. The first author of speech was God himself, who instructed Adam how to name the creatures he set before his eyes; for Scripture goes no further in this matter. But that much was enough to direct Adam to add more names as the experience and use of things gave him occasion, and to join them, step by step, in ways that made him understood. By this slow accumulation, over time, he gathered as much language as he found use for — though not as copious as an orator or philosopher needs. For I find nothing in Scripture from which we can directly or by consequence gather that Adam was taught the names of all figures, numbers, measures, colours, sounds, fancies, or relations — much less the grammatical names of words and speech, such as General, Special, Affirmative, Negative, Interrogative, Optative, Infinitive, all of which are useful; and least of all the words Entity, Intentionality, Quiddity, and the other significant terms of the Schools.

The first man, taught directly by God to name the creatures; cited at length on the conditional immortality he forfeited by sin, and as the ancestor whose fallen estate the Reprobate are argued to remain in after the Resurrection.

## modern-en · eve · 37:9

In what manner God spoke to Adam, and Eve, and Cain, and Noah, is not expressed; nor how he spoke to Abraham, until the time when he came out of his own country to Shechem in the land of Canaan; and then (Gen. 12:7) God is said to have appeared to him. So there is one way by which God made his presence manifest: by an apparition, or vision. And again (Gen. 15:1), the word of the Lord came to Abraham in a vision — that is, something, as a sign of God's presence, appeared as God's messenger, to speak to him. Again, the Lord appeared to Abraham (Gen. 18:1) by an apparition of three angels; and to Abimelech (Gen. 20:3) in a dream; to Lot (Gen. 19:1) by an apparition of two angels; and to Hagar (Gen. 21:17) by the apparition of one angel; and to Abraham again (Gen. 22:11) by the apparition of a voice from heaven; and (Gen. 26:24) to Isaac in the night (that is, in his sleep, or by a dream); and to Jacob (Gen. 28:12) in a dream — that is (in the words of the text), 'Jacob dreamed that he saw a ladder, etc.' And (Gen. 32:1) in a vision of angels. And to Moses (Exod. 3:2) in the apparition of a flame of fire out of the midst of a bush. And after the time of Moses, wherever the manner in which God spoke directly to a man in the Old Testament is expressed, he always spoke by a vision, or by a dream — as to Gideon, Samuel, Elijah, Elisha, Isaiah, Ezekiel, and the rest of the prophets; and often in the New Testament, as to Joseph, to St Peter, to St Paul, and to St John the Evangelist in the Apocalypse.

Named alongside Adam as the first humans, and cited for the temptation and fall recounted in Genesis.
