## modern-en · enoch · 39:4

That the place where men are to live eternally, after the resurrection, is the heavens — meaning by 'heaven' those parts of the world most remote from earth, where the stars are, or above the stars in another, higher, heaven called Caelum Empyreum (of which there is no mention in Scripture, nor ground in reason) — is not easily to be drawn from any text I can find. By 'the kingdom of heaven' is meant the kingdom of the king who dwells in heaven; and his kingdom was the people of Israel, whom he ruled by the prophets, his lieutenants — first Moses, and after him Eleazar, and the sovereign priests — until in the days of Samuel they rebelled and would have a mortal man for their king, after the manner of other nations. And when our Saviour Christ, by the preaching of his ministers, shall have persuaded the Jews to return, and called the Gentiles to his obedience, then shall there be a new kingdom of heaven, because our king shall then be God, whose throne is heaven — without any necessity evident in Scripture that man shall ascend to his happiness any higher than God's footstool, the earth. On the contrary, we find written (John 3:13) that 'no man has ascended into heaven, but he that came down from heaven, even the Son of man, that is in heaven.' Where I observe in passing that these words are not, like those immediately before, the words of our Saviour, but of St John himself; for Christ was then not in heaven, but on the earth. The like is said of David (Acts 2:34), where St Peter, to prove the ascension of Christ, using the words of the Psalmist (Ps. 16:10), 'You will not leave my soul in hell, nor allow your Holy One to see corruption,' says they were spoken not of David, but of Christ; and to prove it, adds this reason: 'For David is not ascended into heaven.' But to this a man may easily answer, and say, that though their bodies were not to ascend until the general day of judgement, yet their souls were in heaven as soon as they were departed from their bodies. Which also seems to be confirmed by the words of our Saviour (Luke 20:37–38), who, proving the resurrection out of the word of Moses, says: 'That the dead are raised, even Moses showed at the bush, when he calls the Lord the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob. For he is not a God of the dead, but of the living, for they all live to him.' But if these words are to be understood only of the immortality of the soul, they do not prove at all what our Saviour intended to prove — which was the resurrection of the body, that is, the immortality of the man. So our Saviour means that those patriarchs were immortal, not by a property consequent on the essence and nature of mankind, but by the will of God, who was pleased of his mere grace to bestow eternal life on the faithful. And though at that time the patriarchs, and many other faithful men, were dead, yet, as it is in the text, they 'lived to God' — that is, they were written in the Book of Life with those who were absolved of their sins, and ordained to life eternal at the resurrection. That the soul of man is in its own nature eternal, and a living creature independent of the body — or that any mere man is immortal, otherwise than by the resurrection on the last day (except Enoch and Elijah) — is a doctrine not apparent in Scripture. The whole 14th chapter of Job, which is the speech not of his friends but of himself, is a complaint of this mortality of nature, and yet no contradiction of immortality at the resurrection. 'There is hope of a tree,' he says (verse 7), 'if it be cast down, that though the root of it grows old, and the stock of it dies in the ground, yet when it scents the water it will bud, and bring forth boughs like a plant. But man dies, and wastes away — yea, man gives up the ghost, and where is he?' And (verse 12), 'Man lies down, and rises not, till the heavens be no more.' But when is it that the heavens shall be no more? St Peter tells us it is at the general resurrection. For in his second Epistle, chapter 3, verse 7, he says, 'The heavens and the earth that are now are reserved to fire, against the day of judgement and perdition of ungodly men'; and (verse 12), 'looking for, and hastening to, the coming of God, in which the heavens shall be on fire, and shall be dissolved, and the elements shall melt with fervent heat. Nevertheless, we, according to the promise, look for new heavens and a new earth, in which dwells righteousness.' So where Job says man does not rise till the heavens be no more, it is all one as if he had said, the immortal life (and 'soul' and 'life' in Scripture usually mean the same thing) does not begin in man until the resurrection and the day of judgement; and has for its cause not his specific nature and generation, but the promise. For St Peter does not say 'We look for new heavens and a new earth (from nature),' but 'from promise.'

Cited directly, quoting Genesis 5:24, "Enoch walked with God, and he was not; for God took him" -- the clearest scriptural case Hobbes allows for a bodily translation without death, restricted (he argues) to those who please God rather than a universal natural immortality.

## modern-en · noah · 37:9

In what manner God spoke to Adam, and Eve, and Cain, and Noah, is not expressed; nor how he spoke to Abraham, until the time when he came out of his own country to Shechem in the land of Canaan; and then (Gen. 12:7) God is said to have appeared to him. So there is one way by which God made his presence manifest: by an apparition, or vision. And again (Gen. 15:1), the word of the Lord came to Abraham in a vision — that is, something, as a sign of God's presence, appeared as God's messenger, to speak to him. Again, the Lord appeared to Abraham (Gen. 18:1) by an apparition of three angels; and to Abimelech (Gen. 20:3) in a dream; to Lot (Gen. 19:1) by an apparition of two angels; and to Hagar (Gen. 21:17) by the apparition of one angel; and to Abraham again (Gen. 22:11) by the apparition of a voice from heaven; and (Gen. 26:24) to Isaac in the night (that is, in his sleep, or by a dream); and to Jacob (Gen. 28:12) in a dream — that is (in the words of the text), 'Jacob dreamed that he saw a ladder, etc.' And (Gen. 32:1) in a vision of angels. And to Moses (Exod. 3:2) in the apparition of a flame of fire out of the midst of a bush. And after the time of Moses, wherever the manner in which God spoke directly to a man in the Old Testament is expressed, he always spoke by a vision, or by a dream — as to Gideon, Samuel, Elijah, Elisha, Isaiah, Ezekiel, and the rest of the prophets; and often in the New Testament, as to Joseph, to St Peter, to St Paul, and to St John the Evangelist in the Apocalypse.

Cited for God's post-Flood promise never again to "smite every Living Creature," and for the length of his household's stay in the Ark, in the survey of Genesis's chronology.

## modern-en · abraham · 9:24

There was once a great gathering of people in Abdera, a city of the Greeks, at the performance of the tragedy of Andromeda, on an extremely hot day. As a result, a great many of the spectators, falling into fevers, suffered this peculiar consequence from the heat and the tragedy together: they did nothing but recite iambic verses, with the names of Perseus and Andromeda. This, along with the fever, was cured by the coming on of winter. And the madness was thought to come from the passion imprinted by the tragedy. Likewise a fit of madness once raged in another Greek city, seizing only the young maidens, and causing many of them to hang themselves. Most at the time thought this an act of the devil. But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

Named repeatedly as a founder of Divine Politiques and receiver of God's covenant, and cited for God speaking to him "by a Voyce, that is, by Vision" the same way he spoke to Samuel.

## modern-en · isaac · 35:21

For we read in Genesis 16 that the same apparition is called not only an angel but God. Where what (verse 7) is called 'the angel of the Lord,' in verse 10 says to Hagar, 'I will multiply your seed exceedingly' — that is, speaks in the person of God. Nor was this apparition a fancy with a shape, but a voice. By which it is plain that 'angel' there signifies nothing but God himself, who caused Hagar supernaturally to apprehend a voice supernatural, testifying God's special presence there. Why therefore may not the angels that appeared to Lot, and are called in Gen. 19:13 men, and to whom — though they were but two — Lot speaks (verse 18) as to one, and that one as God (for the words are, 'And Lot said to them, Oh not so, my Lord') be understood of images of men supernaturally formed in the fancy, as before by 'angel' was understood a fancied voice? When the angel called to Abraham out of heaven, to stay his hand (Gen. 22:11) from slaying Isaac, there was no apparition, but a voice; which nevertheless was called properly enough a messenger, or angel, of God — because it declared God's will supernaturally, and saves the labour of supposing any permanent ghosts. The angels that Jacob saw on the ladder of heaven (Gen. 28:12) were a vision of his sleep — therefore only fancy, and a dream — yet, being supernatural and signs of God's special presence, those apparitions are not improperly called angels. The same is to be understood in Gen. 31:11, where Jacob says: 'The angel of the Lord appeared to me in my sleep.' For an apparition made to a man in his sleep is what all men call a dream, whether such a dream is natural or supernatural; and what there Jacob calls an angel was God himself — for the same angel says (verse 13), 'I am the God of Bethel.'

Named with Abraham and Jacob as living "by promise" of the Resurrection in Christ's own argument against the Sadducees, per Hobbes's reading.

## modern-en · esau · 39:27

In these words we have the place from which salvation is to proceed: 'Jerusalem, a quiet habitation'; the eternity of it: 'a tabernacle that shall not be taken down,' etc.; the Saviour of it: 'the Lord, their judge, their lawgiver, their king; he will save us'; the salvation: 'the Lord shall be to them as a broad moat of swift waters,' etc.; the condition of their enemies: 'their tacklings are loose, their masts weak; the lame shall take the spoil of them'; the condition of the saved: 'the inhabitants shall not say, I am sick'; and lastly, all this is comprehended in forgiveness of sin: 'the people that dwell therein shall be forgiven their iniquity.' By which it is plain that salvation shall be on earth, then, when God shall reign (at the coming again of Christ) in Jerusalem; and from Jerusalem shall proceed the salvation of the Gentiles, who shall be received into God's kingdom. As is also more expressly declared by the same prophet, chapter 66:20–21: 'And they' (that is, the Gentiles who had any Jew in bondage) 'shall bring all your brethren, for an offering to the Lord, out of all nations, on horses, and in chariots, and in litters, and on mules, and on swift beasts, to my holy mountain, Jerusalem, says the Lord, as the children of Israel bring an offering in a clean vessel into the house of the Lord. And I will also take of them for priests and for Levites, says the Lord.' Whereby it is plain that the chief seat of God's kingdom (which is the place from which the salvation of us who were Gentiles shall proceed) shall be Jerusalem. And the same is also confirmed by our Saviour, in his discourse with the woman of Samaria, concerning the place of God's worship; to whom he says (John 4:22) that the Samaritans worshipped they knew not what, but the Jews worshipped what they knew, 'For salvation is of the Jews' (Ex Judais, that is, begins with the Jews) — as if he should say, you worship God, but do not know by whom he will save you, as we do, who know it shall be one of the tribe of Judah, a Jew, not a Samaritan. And so also the woman not impertinently answered him again: 'We know the Messiah shall come.' So what our Saviour says, 'Salvation is from the Jews,' is the same as Paul says (Rom. 1:16–17): 'The gospel is the power of God to salvation to every one that believes — to the Jew first, and also to the Greek. For in it is the righteousness of God revealed from faith to faith' — from the faith of the Jew, to the faith of the Gentile. In the like sense the prophet Joel, describing the day of judgement (chapter 2:30–31), says that God would 'show wonders in heaven and in earth, blood, and fire, and pillars of smoke. The sun shall be turned to darkness, and the moon into blood, before the great and terrible day of the Lord come,' and adds (verse 32), 'and it shall come to pass, that whoever shall call upon the name of the Lord shall be saved. For in Mount Zion, and in Jerusalem, shall be salvation.' And Obadiah verse 17 says the same: 'Upon Mount Zion shall be deliverance; and there shall be holiness, and the house of Jacob shall possess their possessions' — that is, the possessions of the heathen, which he expresses more particularly in the following verses, by the Mount of Esau, the land of the Philistines, the fields of Ephraim, of Samaria, Gilead, and the cities of the south; and concludes with these words: 'The kingdom shall be the Lord's.' All these passages are for salvation, and the kingdom of God (after the day of judgement) upon earth. On the other hand, I have not found any text that can probably be drawn to prove any ascension of the saints into heaven — that is, into any Caelum Empyreum or other ethereal region — except that it is called the kingdom of heaven. Which name it may have because God, who was king of the Jews, governed them by his commands sent to Moses by angels from heaven, to reduce them to their obedience; and shall send him from there again, to rule both them and all other faithful men, from the day of judgement, everlastingly; or from this, that the throne of this our great king is in heaven, whereas the earth is only his footstool. But that the subjects of God should have any place as high as his throne, or higher than his footstool, seems not suitable to the dignity of a king; nor can I find any plain text for it in Holy Scripture.

Named as Isaac's son in the genealogical survey of Genesis's authorship and chronology.

## modern-en · joseph-patriarch · 25:6

In this distribution, the first law is for the division of the land itself, in which the sovereign assigns to each man a portion according as he — and not as any subject, or any number of subjects — shall judge agreeable to equity and the common good. The children of Israel were a commonwealth in the wilderness, but lacked the commodities of the earth until they were masters of the Land of Promise; which was afterwards divided among them, not by their own discretion but by the discretion of Eleazar the priest and Joshua their general. When there were twelve tribes, made thirteen by the subdivision of the tribe of Joseph, they nevertheless made only twelve portions of the land, and assigned no land to the tribe of Levi, giving them instead the tenth part of all the fruits. So that division was therefore arbitrary. And though a people coming into possession of a land by war does not always exterminate the ancient inhabitants (as the Jews did), but leaves many, most, or all of them their estates, it is plain they afterwards hold those estates from the victor's distribution — as the people of England held all theirs from William the Conqueror.

The Genesis patriarch sold into Egypt, who interpreted Pharaoh's dreams and was blessed, through his sons Ephraim and Manasseh, by his father Jacob. Bare "Joseph" is bound here as the dominant sense; see the namesake note above for two minority occurrences (Joseph, husband of Mary) that the same bare string cannot be distinguished from.

## modern-en · joseph-just · 43:51

The first apostle of those not constituted by Christ while he was on earth was Matthias, chosen in this way. There were assembled together in Jerusalem about 120 Christians (Acts 1:15). These appointed two — Joseph the Just, and Matthias (verse 23) — and caused lots to be drawn; 'and (verse 26) the lot fell on Matthias, and he was numbered with the apostles.' So that here we see that the ordination of this apostle was the act of the congregation, and not of St Peter, nor of the eleven, otherwise than as members of the assembly.

One of the two disciples put forward, with Matthias, to fill Judas's vacated place among the Twelve by lot (Acts 1:23) -- not chosen, and named by his own distinguishing epithet, distinct from the patriarch Joseph and from Joseph the husband of Mary.

## modern-en · lot-genesis · 16:26

But there are some things that can neither be divided nor enjoyed in common. Then the law of nature, which prescribes equity, requires: “That the Entire Right; or else, (making the use alternate,) the First Possession, be determined by Lot.” For equal distribution is of the law of nature, and no other means of equal distribution can be imagined.

Named for receiving a dream apparition from angels, in the same survey of how divine messages came to Genesis's figures.

## modern-en · abimelech-genesis · 37:6

The name PROPHET signifies in Scripture sometimes Prolocutor — that is, one who speaks from God to man, or from man to God; and sometimes Praedictor, or foreteller of things to come; and sometimes one who speaks incoherently, as men who are distracted. It is most frequently used in the sense of speaking from God to the people. So Moses, Samuel, Elijah, Isaiah, Jeremiah, and others were prophets. And in this sense the high priest was a prophet, for he alone went into the Sanctum Sanctorum to inquire of God, and was to declare God's answer to the people. So when Caiaphas said it was expedient that one man should die for the people, St John says (chapter 11:51) that 'he spoke not this of himself, but, being high priest that year, he prophesied that one man should die for the nation.' Also those who in Christian congregations taught the people (1 Cor. 14:3) are said to prophesy. In the like sense it is that God says to Moses (Exod. 4:16), concerning Aaron, 'He shall be your spokesman to the people; and he shall be to you a mouth, and you shall be to him in the place of God' — and what here is 'spokesman' is (chapter 7:1) interpreted as 'prophet': 'See (says God) I have made you a god to Pharaoh, and Aaron your brother shall be your prophet.' In the sense of speaking from man to God, Abraham is called a prophet (Gen. 20:7), where God in a dream speaks to Abimelech in this way: 'Now therefore restore the man his wife, for he is a prophet, and shall pray for you' — whereby it may also be gathered that the name of prophet may not improperly be given to those who in Christian churches have a calling to say public prayers for the congregation. In the same sense, the prophets who came down from the high place (or hill of God) with a psaltery, and a tabret, and a pipe, and a harp (1 Sam. 10:5–6), and (verse 10) Saul among them, are said to prophesy, in that they praised God in that way publicly. In the like sense, Miriam is called a prophetess (Exod. 15:20). So it is also to be taken in 1 Cor. 11:4–5, where St Paul says, 'Every man that prays or prophesies with his head covered, etc., and every woman that prays or prophesies with her head uncovered' — for 'prophecy' in that place signifies nothing more than praising God in psalms and holy songs, which women might do in the church, though it were not lawful for them to speak to the congregation. And in this signification it is that the poets of the heathen, who composed hymns and other sorts of poems in honour of their gods, were called vates (prophets), as is well enough known by all who are versed in the books of the Gentiles, and as is evident in Titus 1:12, where St Paul says of the Cretans that a prophet of their own said they were liars — not that St Paul held their poets for prophets, but acknowledges that the word 'prophet' was commonly used to signify those who celebrated the honour of God in verse.

The king to whom God spoke in a dream (Genesis 20) warning him against Sarah, cited in the same survey of scriptural dream-revelations.

The founder of Divine Politiques and lieutenant of God over Israel, cited constantly -- for personating God without Scripture ever specifying how God spoke to him supernaturally, for consecrating the Tabernacle and Aaron without any Exorcism, and as the source of the Kingdom of God that "ended in the election of Saul."

## modern-en · aaron · 9:24

There was once a great gathering of people in Abdera, a city of the Greeks, at the performance of the tragedy of Andromeda, on an extremely hot day. As a result, a great many of the spectators, falling into fevers, suffered this peculiar consequence from the heat and the tragedy together: they did nothing but recite iambic verses, with the names of Perseus and Andromeda. This, along with the fever, was cured by the coming on of winter. And the madness was thought to come from the passion imprinted by the tragedy. Likewise a fit of madness once raged in another Greek city, seizing only the young maidens, and causing many of them to hang themselves. Most at the time thought this an act of the devil. But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

Moses's brother and the first High Priest, consecrated by washing and anointing rather than any charm; also named for the making of the Golden Calf, done, per Hobbes, without divine authority and therefore Idolatry.

## modern-en · miriam · 37:6

The name PROPHET signifies in Scripture sometimes Prolocutor — that is, one who speaks from God to man, or from man to God; and sometimes Praedictor, or foreteller of things to come; and sometimes one who speaks incoherently, as men who are distracted. It is most frequently used in the sense of speaking from God to the people. So Moses, Samuel, Elijah, Isaiah, Jeremiah, and others were prophets. And in this sense the high priest was a prophet, for he alone went into the Sanctum Sanctorum to inquire of God, and was to declare God's answer to the people. So when Caiaphas said it was expedient that one man should die for the people, St John says (chapter 11:51) that 'he spoke not this of himself, but, being high priest that year, he prophesied that one man should die for the nation.' Also those who in Christian congregations taught the people (1 Cor. 14:3) are said to prophesy. In the like sense it is that God says to Moses (Exod. 4:16), concerning Aaron, 'He shall be your spokesman to the people; and he shall be to you a mouth, and you shall be to him in the place of God' — and what here is 'spokesman' is (chapter 7:1) interpreted as 'prophet': 'See (says God) I have made you a god to Pharaoh, and Aaron your brother shall be your prophet.' In the sense of speaking from man to God, Abraham is called a prophet (Gen. 20:7), where God in a dream speaks to Abimelech in this way: 'Now therefore restore the man his wife, for he is a prophet, and shall pray for you' — whereby it may also be gathered that the name of prophet may not improperly be given to those who in Christian churches have a calling to say public prayers for the congregation. In the same sense, the prophets who came down from the high place (or hill of God) with a psaltery, and a tabret, and a pipe, and a harp (1 Sam. 10:5–6), and (verse 10) Saul among them, are said to prophesy, in that they praised God in that way publicly. In the like sense, Miriam is called a prophetess (Exod. 15:20). So it is also to be taken in 1 Cor. 11:4–5, where St Paul says, 'Every man that prays or prophesies with his head covered, etc., and every woman that prays or prophesies with her head uncovered' — for 'prophecy' in that place signifies nothing more than praising God in psalms and holy songs, which women might do in the church, though it were not lawful for them to speak to the congregation. And in this signification it is that the poets of the heathen, who composed hymns and other sorts of poems in honour of their gods, were called vates (prophets), as is well enough known by all who are versed in the books of the Gentiles, and as is evident in Titus 1:12, where St Paul says of the Cretans that a prophet of their own said they were liars — not that St Paul held their poets for prophets, but acknowledges that the word 'prophet' was commonly used to signify those who celebrated the honour of God in verse.

Named in the genealogical survey of Exodus's early chapters.

## modern-en · jethro · 37:15

In the time of Moses there were seventy men besides himself who prophesied in the camp of the Israelites. In what manner God spoke to them is declared in Numbers 11:25: 'The Lord came down in a cloud, and spoke to Moses, and took of the spirit that was upon him, and gave it to the seventy elders. And it came to pass, when the spirit rested on them, they prophesied, and did not cease.' By which it is plain, first, that their prophesying to the people was subservient and subordinate to the prophesying of Moses; for God took of the spirit of Moses to put on them, so that they prophesied as Moses would have them — otherwise they had not been allowed to prophesy at all. For there was (verse 27) a complaint made against them to Moses, and Joshua would have had Moses forbid them; which Moses did not, but said to Joshua, 'Be not jealous on my behalf.' Second, that the Spirit of God in that place signifies nothing but the mind and disposition to obey, and to assist Moses, in the administration of the government. For if it were meant that they had the substantial Spirit of God — that is, the divine nature, inspired into them — then they had it no less than Christ himself, in whom alone the Spirit of God dwelt bodily. It is meant therefore of the gift and grace of God, that guided them to co-operate with Moses, from whom their spirit was derived. And it appears (verse 16) that they were such as Moses himself should appoint for elders and officers of the people; for the words are, 'Gather to me seventy men, whom you know to be elders and officers of the people' — where 'you know' is the same as 'you appoint,' or 'have appointed to be such.' For we are told before (Exod. 18) that Moses, following the counsel of Jethro his father-in-law, appointed judges and officers over the people, such as feared God; and of these were those seventy whom God, by putting Moses' spirit on them, inclined to aid Moses in the administration of the kingdom. And in this sense the Spirit of God is said (1 Sam. 16:13–14), immediately on the anointing of David, to have come on David, and left Saul — God giving his graces to him whom he chose to govern his people, and taking them away from the one he rejected. So that by 'the Spirit' is meant inclination to God's service, and not any supernatural revelation.

Moses's father-in-law, named in the survey of the events and authorship of Exodus.

## modern-en · joshua · 13:28

All these causes of the weakening of men's faith are clearly apparent in the following examples. First, we have the example of the children of Israel, who, when Moses (who had proved his calling to them by miracles and by the happy leading of them out of Egypt) was absent only forty days, revolted from the worship of the true God that he had commended to them, and setting up (Exod. 32:1,2) a Golden Calf for their God, relapsed into the idolatry of the Egyptians, from whom they had been so recently delivered. And again, after Moses, Aaron, Joshua, and the generation that had seen the great works of God in Israel were dead (Judges 2:11), another generation arose and served Baal. So that, miracles failing, faith also failed.

Moses's successor, quoted directly (Deut. 34:9) as "full of the Spirit of Wisdome" from Moses's laying on of hands, and named as the presumed author of the Book of Joshua in Hobbes's survey of scriptural authorship.

## modern-en · achan · 34:5

That the book of Joshua was also written long after the time of Joshua may be gathered from many places in the book itself. Joshua had set up twelve stones in the middle of the Jordan as a monument to their crossing (Josh. 4:9); of which the writer says, 'They are there to this day.' For 'to this day' is a phrase that signifies a time past, beyond the memory of man. In the same way, upon the saying of the Lord that he had rolled off from the people the reproach of Egypt, the writer says, 'The place is called Gilgal to this day' (Josh. 5:9) — which would have been improper to say in the time of Joshua. So too of the name of the Valley of Achor, from the trouble Achan raised in the camp, the writer says it 'remains to this day' (Josh. 7:26) — which must therefore have been long after the time of Joshua. There are many other arguments of this kind, as Josh. 8:29; 13:13; 14:14; 15:63.

Named in the survey of Joshua's narrative for his transgression and its consequences.

## modern-en · othniel · 35:7

In the book of Judges, an extraordinary zeal and courage in the defence of God's people is called the Spirit of God; as when it stirred up Othniel, Gideon, Jephthah, and Samson to deliver them from servitude (Judg. 3:10; 6:34; 11:29; 13:25; 14:6, 19). And of Saul, on the news of the insolence of the Ammonites toward the men of Jabesh-Gilead, it is said (1 Sam. 11:6) that 'the Spirit of God came upon Saul, and his anger (or, as it is in the Latin, his fury) was kindled greatly.' Where it is not probable that a ghost was meant, but an extraordinary zeal to punish the cruelty of the Ammonites. In the same way, by the Spirit of God that came upon Saul when he was among the prophets that praised God in songs and music (1 Sam. 19:20), is to be understood not a ghost, but an unexpected and sudden zeal to join with them in their devotions.

Named (as printed, "Othoniel") as the first Judge in the survey of the Book of Judges's chronology.

## modern-en · jephthah · 22:6

Nevertheless, we are not to understand that by such liberty the sovereign power of life and death is either abolished or limited. For it has already been shown that nothing the sovereign representative does to a subject, on any pretext, can properly be called injustice or injury, because every subject is the author of every act the sovereign does — so that the sovereign never wants right to anything, except insofar as he himself is the subject of God, and is bound by that to observe the laws of nature. And so it may, and often does, happen in commonwealths that a subject may be put to death by the command of the sovereign power, and yet neither party do the other a wrong — as when Jephthah caused his daughter to be sacrificed. In that and similar cases, the one so dying had liberty to do the action for which he is nevertheless, without injury, put to death. The same holds when a sovereign prince puts to death an innocent subject. For though the action is against the law of nature, being contrary to equity (as was David's killing of Uriah), yet it was not an injury to Uriah; it was an injury to God. Not to Uriah, because the right to do as he pleased had been given to David by Uriah himself; and yet to God, because David was God's subject, and forbidden all iniquity by the law of nature. This distinction David himself, when he repented the act, plainly confirmed, saying, 'To thee only have I sinned.' In the same way the people of Athens, when they banished the most powerful man in their commonwealth for ten years, thought they committed no injustice — and yet they never inquired what crime he had done, but only what hurt he might do. They even commanded the banishment of men they did not know: every citizen brought his oyster-shell to the marketplace with the name written on it of the man he wanted banished, without actually accusing him; and they sometimes banished an Aristides for his reputation for justice, and sometimes a scurrilous jester such as Hyperbolus, to make a joke of it. And yet one cannot say that the sovereign people of Athens lacked the right to banish them, or that any Athenian lacked the liberty to jest or to be just.

Named (as printed, "Jeptha") among the Judges, in the survey of the Book of Judges.

## modern-en · corah · 39:7

As the kingdom of God, and eternal life, so also God's enemies and their torments after judgement appear by Scripture to have their place on earth. The name of the place where all men remain till the resurrection — those who were either buried, or swallowed up by the earth — is usually called in Scripture by words that signify 'under ground'; which the Latins generally read as Infernus and Inferni, and the Greeks Hades — that is, a place where men cannot see; and it includes the grave as well as any deeper place. But for the place of the damned after the resurrection, it is not determined either in the Old or in the New Testament by any note of location, but only by the company; as that it shall be where such wicked men were as God had in former times in an extraordinary and miraculous manner destroyed from off the face of the earth. As, for example, that they are in Inferno, in Tartarus, or in the bottomless pit, because Korah, Dathan, and Abiram were swallowed up alive into the earth. Not that the writers of Scripture would have us believe there could be in the globe of the earth — which is not only finite, but also (compared to the height of the stars) of no considerable size — a pit without a bottom, that is, a hole of infinite depth, such as the Greeks in their daemonology (their doctrine concerning daemons), and after them the Romans, called Tartarus; of which Virgil says:

Named (as printed, "Corah") with Dathan and Abiram as leaders of the rebellion against Moses's authority in the wilderness, swallowed alive by the earth.

## modern-en · samuel · 13:29

Again, when the sons of Samuel (1 Sam. 8:3), having been appointed by their father as judges in Beersheba, took bribes and judged unjustly, the people of Israel refused any longer to have God as their king in any way different from how he was king of other peoples, and therefore cried out to Samuel to choose them a king after the manner of the nations. So that, justice failing, faith also failed — to the point that they deposed their God from reigning over them.

The last Judge and first of the great prophets after Moses, cited for anointing Saul, for his sons' corrupt judging that led Israel to demand a king, and for the disputed apparition raised at Endor.

## modern-en · king-saul · 21:15

Let us now consider what Scripture teaches on the same point. To Moses, the children of Israel say: 'Speak thou to us, and we will hear thee; but let not God speak to us, lest we die' (Exodus 20:19). This is absolute obedience to Moses. Concerning the right of kings, God himself, by the mouth of Samuel, says (1 Samuel 8:11–12 etc.): 'This shall be the right of the king you will have to reign over you. He shall take your sons, and set them to drive his chariots, and to be his horsemen, and to run before his chariots; and gather in his harvest; and to make his engines of war, and instruments of his chariots; and shall take your daughters to make perfumes, to be his cooks and bakers. He shall take your fields, your vineyards, and your olive yards, and give them to his servants. He shall take the tithe of your corn and wine, and give it to the men of his chamber, and to his other servants. He shall take your manservants, and your maidservants, and the choice of your youth, and employ them in his business. He shall take the tithe of your flocks; and you shall be his servants.' This is absolute power, summed up in the last words: 'you shall be his servants.' Again, when the people heard what power their king was to have, they still consented, saying (verse 19 etc.): 'We will be as all other nations, and our king shall judge our causes, and go before us, to conduct our wars.' Here is confirmed the right that sovereigns have, both to the militia and to all judicature — which contains as absolute a power as one man can possibly transfer to another. Again, King Solomon's prayer to God was this (1 Kings 3:9): 'Give to thy servant understanding, to judge thy people, and to discern between good and evil.' It belongs therefore to the sovereign to be judge, and to prescribe the rules of discerning good and evil; and these rules are laws; and so in him is the legislative power. Saul sought David's life; yet when it was in David's power to slay Saul, and his servants would have done it, David forbade them, saying (1 Samuel 24:9): 'God forbid I should do such an act against my lord, the anointed of God.' For the obedience of servants, St Paul says (Colossians 3:22): 'Servants, obey your masters in all things,' and (verse 20): 'Children, obey your parents in all things.' That is plain obedience in those who are subject to paternal or despotical dominion. Again (Matthew 23:2–3): 'The scribes and Pharisees sit in Moses' chair, and therefore all that they shall bid you observe, that observe and do.' There again is plain obedience. And St Paul (Titus 3:1): 'Warn them that they subject themselves to princes, and to those that are in authority, and obey them.' This obedience is also plain. Lastly, our Saviour himself acknowledges that men ought to pay such taxes as kings impose, when he says, 'Render unto Caesar the things which are Caesar's' — and paid such taxes himself. And that the king's word is enough to take anything from any subject when there is need, and that the king is judge of that need: for he himself, as king of the Jews, ordered his disciples to take the ass and the ass's colt to carry him into Jerusalem, saying (Matthew 21:2–3): 'Go into the village over against you, and you shall find an ass tied, and her colt with her; loose them, and bring them to me. And if any man asks you what you mean by it, say, The Lord has need of them; and they will let them go.' They are not to ask whether his necessity is a sufficient title, nor whether he is the judge of that necessity, but to acquiesce in the will of the Lord.

The first King of Israel, whose election Hobbes repeatedly cites as the moment the direct Kingdom of God "ended" -- discussed at length for his pursuit of David, his prophesying under the Spirit of God, and his death at the Witch of Endor's séance. Bare "Saul" binds here as the dominant sense; see the namesake note above for the minority "Saul of Tarsus" occurrences the same bare string does not distinguish.

Named throughout for his conduct toward Saul, his sin against Uriah, his fasting for Saul and Jonathan and for Abner, and his appointment of Temple officers -- cited as the model of a sovereign whose personal grief at his own child's death did not extend to those whose deaths cost him nothing.

## modern-en · abner · 45:28

Upon this doctrine of the natural eternity of separated souls is founded (as I said) the doctrine of purgatory. For supposing eternal life by grace only, there is no life but the life of the body, and no immortality till the resurrection. The texts for purgatory alleged by Bellarmine out of the canonical Scripture of the Old Testament are first, the fasting of David for Saul and Jonathan, mentioned (2 Sam. 1:12); and again (2 Sam. 3:35) for the death of Abner. This fasting of David, he says, was for the obtaining of something for them at God's hands, after their death — because after he had fasted to procure the recovery of his own child, as soon as he knew it was dead, he called for meat. Seeing then the soul has an existence separate from the body, and nothing can be obtained by men's fasting for the souls that are already either in heaven, or hell, it follows that there are some souls of dead men, which are neither in heaven nor in hell; and therefore they must be in some third place, which must be purgatory. And thus with hard straining, he has wrested those places to the proof of a purgatory. Whereas it is plain that the ceremonies of mourning and fasting, when they are used for the death of men whose life was not profitable to the mourners, are used for honour's sake to their persons; and when it is done for the death of those by whose life the mourners had benefit, it proceeds from their particular damage. And so David honoured Saul and Abner with his fasting; and in the death of his own child, recomforted himself by receiving his ordinary food.

Saul's general, named for David's mourning at his death (2 Sam. 3), cited alongside Saul as an example of honorific rather than self-interested fasting.

As for the two books of the Kings and the two books of the Chronicles, beyond the passages that mention such monuments as the writer says remained to his own day — such as 1 Kings 9:13; 9:21; 10:12; 12:19; 2 Kings 2:22; 8:22; 10:27; 14:7; 16:6; 17:23; 17:34; 17:41; 1 Chron. 4:41; 5:26 — it is sufficient argument that they were written after the captivity in Babylon that their history is continued to that time. For the facts recorded are always more ancient than such books as mention and quote the register; as these books do in various places, referring the reader to the chronicles of the kings of Judah, to the chronicles of the kings of Israel, to the books of the prophet Samuel or the prophet Nathan, of the prophet Ahijah, to the vision of Iddo, to the books of the prophet Shemaiah and of the prophet Iddo.

## modern-en · uriah-hittite · 22:6

Nevertheless, we are not to understand that by such liberty the sovereign power of life and death is either abolished or limited. For it has already been shown that nothing the sovereign representative does to a subject, on any pretext, can properly be called injustice or injury, because every subject is the author of every act the sovereign does — so that the sovereign never wants right to anything, except insofar as he himself is the subject of God, and is bound by that to observe the laws of nature. And so it may, and often does, happen in commonwealths that a subject may be put to death by the command of the sovereign power, and yet neither party do the other a wrong — as when Jephthah caused his daughter to be sacrificed. In that and similar cases, the one so dying had liberty to do the action for which he is nevertheless, without injury, put to death. The same holds when a sovereign prince puts to death an innocent subject. For though the action is against the law of nature, being contrary to equity (as was David's killing of Uriah), yet it was not an injury to Uriah; it was an injury to God. Not to Uriah, because the right to do as he pleased had been given to David by Uriah himself; and yet to God, because David was God's subject, and forbidden all iniquity by the law of nature. This distinction David himself, when he repented the act, plainly confirmed, saying, 'To thee only have I sinned.' In the same way the people of Athens, when they banished the most powerful man in their commonwealth for ten years, thought they committed no injustice — and yet they never inquired what crime he had done, but only what hurt he might do. They even commanded the banishment of men they did not know: every citizen brought his oyster-shell to the marketplace with the name written on it of the man he wanted banished, without actually accusing him; and they sometimes banished an Aristides for his reputation for justice, and sometimes a scurrilous jester such as Hyperbolus, to make a joke of it. And yet one cannot say that the sovereign people of Athens lacked the right to banish them, or that any Athenian lacked the liberty to jest or to be just.

David's officer, killed by David's own contrivance -- cited as an act unjust to God, per Hobbes, though not, strictly, an injury to Uriah, who as a soldier owed his sovereign unlimited obedience.

## modern-en · solomon · 21:15

Let us now consider what Scripture teaches on the same point. To Moses, the children of Israel say: 'Speak thou to us, and we will hear thee; but let not God speak to us, lest we die' (Exodus 20:19). This is absolute obedience to Moses. Concerning the right of kings, God himself, by the mouth of Samuel, says (1 Samuel 8:11–12 etc.): 'This shall be the right of the king you will have to reign over you. He shall take your sons, and set them to drive his chariots, and to be his horsemen, and to run before his chariots; and gather in his harvest; and to make his engines of war, and instruments of his chariots; and shall take your daughters to make perfumes, to be his cooks and bakers. He shall take your fields, your vineyards, and your olive yards, and give them to his servants. He shall take the tithe of your corn and wine, and give it to the men of his chamber, and to his other servants. He shall take your manservants, and your maidservants, and the choice of your youth, and employ them in his business. He shall take the tithe of your flocks; and you shall be his servants.' This is absolute power, summed up in the last words: 'you shall be his servants.' Again, when the people heard what power their king was to have, they still consented, saying (verse 19 etc.): 'We will be as all other nations, and our king shall judge our causes, and go before us, to conduct our wars.' Here is confirmed the right that sovereigns have, both to the militia and to all judicature — which contains as absolute a power as one man can possibly transfer to another. Again, King Solomon's prayer to God was this (1 Kings 3:9): 'Give to thy servant understanding, to judge thy people, and to discern between good and evil.' It belongs therefore to the sovereign to be judge, and to prescribe the rules of discerning good and evil; and these rules are laws; and so in him is the legislative power. Saul sought David's life; yet when it was in David's power to slay Saul, and his servants would have done it, David forbade them, saying (1 Samuel 24:9): 'God forbid I should do such an act against my lord, the anointed of God.' For the obedience of servants, St Paul says (Colossians 3:22): 'Servants, obey your masters in all things,' and (verse 20): 'Children, obey your parents in all things.' That is plain obedience in those who are subject to paternal or despotical dominion. Again (Matthew 23:2–3): 'The scribes and Pharisees sit in Moses' chair, and therefore all that they shall bid you observe, that observe and do.' There again is plain obedience. And St Paul (Titus 3:1): 'Warn them that they subject themselves to princes, and to those that are in authority, and obey them.' This obedience is also plain. Lastly, our Saviour himself acknowledges that men ought to pay such taxes as kings impose, when he says, 'Render unto Caesar the things which are Caesar's' — and paid such taxes himself. And that the king's word is enough to take anything from any subject when there is need, and that the king is judge of that need: for he himself, as king of the Jews, ordered his disciples to take the ass and the ass's colt to carry him into Jerusalem, saying (Matthew 21:2–3): 'Go into the village over against you, and you shall find an ass tied, and her colt with her; loose them, and bring them to me. And if any man asks you what you mean by it, say, The Lord has need of them; and they will let them go.' They are not to ask whether his necessity is a sufficient title, nor whether he is the judge of that necessity, but to acquiesce in the will of the Lord.

Named for his wisdom, his dedication of the Temple without any exorcism or charm, his deposing of the High Priest Abiathar in his own right, and as the presumed author of Ecclesiastes, Proverbs, and the Song of Songs.

## modern-en · abiathar · 37:12

To prophets of perpetual calling, and supreme, God spoke in the Old Testament from the mercy seat, in a manner not expressed in the Scripture. Of prophets who were so by a perpetual calling in the Old Testament, some were supreme and some subordinate. The supreme were first Moses; and after him the high priest, each one for his time, as long as the priesthood was royal. And after the people of the Jews had rejected God, that he should no more reign over them, those kings who submitted themselves to God's government were also his chief prophets; and the high priest's office became ministerial. When God was to be consulted, they put on the holy vestments and inquired of the Lord, as the king commanded them, and were deprived of their office when the king thought fit. For King Saul (1 Sam. 13:9) commanded the burnt offering to be brought; and (1 Sam. 14:18) he commands the priest to bring the ark near him, and (verse 19) again to let it alone, because he saw an advantage over his enemies. And in the same chapter Saul asks counsel of God. In the same way King David, after his being anointed, though before he had possession of the kingdom, is said to 'inquire of the Lord' (1 Sam. 23:2) whether he should fight against the Philistines at Keilah; and (verse 10) David commands the priest to bring him the ephod, to inquire whether he should stay in Keilah, or not. And King Solomon (1 Kings 2:27) took the priesthood from Abiathar, and gave it (verse 35) to Zadok. So Moses, and the high priests, and the pious kings who inquired of God on all extraordinary occasions how they were to conduct themselves, or what outcome they were to have, were all sovereign prophets. But in what manner God spoke to them is not plain. To say that when Moses went up to God on Mount Sinai, it was a dream, or vision, such as other prophets had, is contrary to the distinction God made between Moses and other prophets (Numb. 12:6–8). To say God spoke or appeared as he is in his own nature is to deny his infiniteness, invisibility, incomprehensibility. To say he spoke by inspiration, or infusion, of the Holy Spirit — as the Holy Spirit signifies the Deity — is to make Moses equal with Christ, in whom alone the Godhead (as St Paul says, Col. 2:9) 'dwells bodily.' And lastly, to say he spoke by the Holy Spirit, as it signifies the graces or gifts of the Holy Spirit, is to attribute nothing supernatural to him. For God disposes men to piety, justice, mercy, truth, faith, and every kind of virtue — moral and intellectual — by doctrine, example, and various occasions, natural and ordinary.

The High Priest whom Solomon deposed and replaced by his own civil right, cited as proof the Priesthood was subordinate to the Crown even before Saul's reign fully ended sacerdotal rule.

## modern-en · rehoboam · 41:14

And afterwards, when they demanded a king after the manner of the nations, it was not with a design to depart from the worship of God their king; but despairing of the justice of the sons of Samuel, they would have a king to judge them in civil actions — though they would not allow their king to change the religion which they thought was recommended to them by Moses. So they always kept in store a pretext, either of justice or of religion, to discharge themselves of their obedience whenever they had hope to prevail. Samuel was displeased with the people for desiring a king (for God was their king already, and Samuel had only an authority under him); yet Samuel, when Saul did not follow his counsel in destroying Agag, as God had commanded, anointed another king — namely David — to take the succession from his heirs. Rehoboam was no idolater; but when the people thought him an oppressor, that civil pretext carried away from him ten tribes to Jeroboam, an idolater. And generally, through the whole history of the kings, both of Judah and of Israel, there were prophets who always controlled the kings for transgressing the religion — and sometimes also for errors of state (2 Chron. 19:2): as Jehoshaphat was reproved by the prophet Jehu for aiding the king of Israel against the Syrians, and Hezekiah by Isaiah for showing his treasures to the ambassadors of Babylon. By all this it appears that, though the power both of state and of religion was in the kings, none of them were uncontrolled in the use of it except such as were gracious for their own natural abilities, or felicities. So that from the practice of those times no argument can be drawn that the right of supremacy in religion was not in the kings — unless we place it in the prophets, and conclude that because Hezekiah, praying to the Lord before the cherubim, was not answered from there, nor then, but afterwards by the prophet Isaiah, Isaiah was therefore the supreme head of the church; or because Josiah consulted Huldah the prophetess concerning the book of the law, that therefore neither he nor the high priest, but Huldah the prophetess, had the supreme authority in matters of religion — which I think is not the opinion of any doctor.

Solomon's son, named for the loss of ten tribes to Jeroboam, and for the plundering of the Temple by Shishak, King of Egypt.

## modern-en · jeroboam · 33:6

How then can a man to whom God has never revealed his will directly (saving by the way of natural reason) know when he is to obey, or not to obey, his word delivered by one who says he is a prophet? Of the four hundred prophets of whom the king of Israel asked counsel concerning the war he made against Ramoth-Gilead (1 Kings 22), only Micaiah was a true one. The prophet who was sent to prophesy against the altar set up by Jeroboam (1 Kings 13), though a true prophet — as the two miracles done in his presence show him to be one sent from God — was yet deceived by another, old, prophet, who persuaded him, as from the mouth of God, to eat and drink with him. If one prophet can deceive another, what certainty is there of knowing the will of God by any way other than that of reason? To which I answer out of Holy Scripture: there are two marks by which, taken together (not separately), a true prophet is to be known. One is the doing of miracles; the other is the not teaching of any other religion than that which is already established. Apart, I say, neither of these is sufficient. 'If a prophet rise among you,' says Deuteronomy 13:1–5, 'or a dreamer of dreams, and shall pretend the doing of a miracle, and the miracle come to pass; if he say, Let us follow strange gods, which you have not known, you shall not listen to him, etc. But that prophet, and dreamer of dreams, shall be put to death, because he has spoken to you to revolt from the Lord your God.' In these words two things are to be noted. First, that God will not have miracles alone serve as arguments to approve the prophet's calling, but (as it is in the third verse) as an experiment of the constancy of our adherence to himself. For the works of the Egyptian sorcerers, though not as great as those of Moses, were still great miracles. Second, that however great the miracle may be, if it tends to stir up revolt against the king, or against him who governs by the king's authority, the one who does such a miracle is not to be considered otherwise than as sent to make trial of their allegiance. For the words 'revolt from the Lord your God' are in this place equivalent to 'revolt from your king.' For they had made God their king by pact at the foot of Mount Sinai; who ruled them by Moses alone — for he alone spoke with God, and from time to time declared God's commandments to the people. In the same way, after our Saviour Christ had brought his disciples to acknowledge him for the Messiah (that is, for God's anointed, whom the nation of the Jews daily expected for their king, but rejected when he came), he did not fail to warn them of the danger of miracles: 'There shall arise,' he says, 'false Christs, and false prophets, and shall do great wonders and miracles, even to the seducing (if it were possible) of the very elect' (Matthew 24:24). By this it appears that false prophets may have the power of miracles; and yet we are not to take their doctrine for God's word. St Paul says further to the Galatians that 'if I myself, or an angel from heaven, preach any other gospel to you than the one I preached, let him be accursed' (Galatians 1:8). That gospel was that Christ was king; so all preaching against the power of the king received, in consequence of these words, is by St Paul accursed. For his speech is addressed to those who, by his preaching, had already received Jesus for the Christ — that is, for king of the Jews.

The idolatrous king to whom ten of Israel's tribes seceded under Rehoboam; named as the object of an unfulfilled prophecy against his altar.

## modern-en · ahab · 37:18

Since then all prophecy supposes vision, or dream (which two, when they are natural, are the same), or some special gift of God so rarely seen in mankind as to be admired where seen; and since both such gifts and the most extraordinary dreams and visions may proceed from God, not only by his supernatural and immediate operation, but also by his natural operation, and through the mediation of secondary causes — there is need of reason and judgement to discern between natural and supernatural gifts, and between natural and supernatural visions or dreams. And consequently men have need to be very circumspect and wary in obeying the voice of a man who, claiming to be a prophet, requires us to obey God in the way that he, in God's name, tells us is the way to happiness. For he who claims to teach men the way to so great a felicity claims to govern them — that is, to rule and reign over them; which is a thing that all men naturally desire, and is therefore to be suspected of ambition and imposture; and consequently ought to be examined, and tested, by every man before he yields them obedience — unless he has yielded it to them already, in the institution of a commonwealth, as when the prophet is the civil sovereign, or is authorised by the civil sovereign. And if this examination of prophets and spirits were not allowed to every one of the people, it would have been to no purpose to set out the marks by which every man might be able to distinguish between those he ought, and those he ought not, to follow. Since therefore such marks are set out (Deut. 13:1ff.) to know a prophet by, and (1 John 4:1ff.) to know a spirit by; and since there is so much prophesying in the Old Testament, and so much preaching in the New Testament against prophets; and so much greater a number ordinarily of false prophets than of true — every one is to beware of obeying their directions, at his own peril. And first, that there were many more false than true prophets appears from this, that when Ahab (1 Kings 22 [which Hobbes here numbers as 12]) consulted four hundred prophets, they were all false impostors, except only one — Micaiah. And a little before the time of the captivity, the prophets were generally liars. 'The prophets,' says the Lord through Jeremiah 14:14, 'prophesy lies in my name. I sent them not, neither have I commanded them, nor spoken to them; they prophesy to you a false vision, a thing of naught, and the deceit of their heart.' So much so that God commanded the people, by the mouth of the prophet Jeremiah (chapter 23:16), not to obey them: 'Thus says the Lord of hosts: listen not to the words of the prophets that prophesy to you. They make you vain; they speak a vision of their own heart, and not out of the mouth of the Lord.'

The king whose war for Ramoth Gilead is the setting for the story of the true prophet among four hundred false ones.

## modern-en · athaliah · 43:137

His sixth, and last, argument is from examples. To which I answer, first, that examples prove nothing; second, that the examples he alleges do not even amount to a probability of right. The fact of Jehoiada in killing Athaliah (2 Kings 11) was either by the authority of King Joash, or it was a horrible crime in the high priest — who (ever after the election of King Saul) was a mere subject. The fact of St Ambrose in excommunicating Theodosius the emperor (if it is true he did so) was a capital crime. And for the popes — Gregory I, Gregory II, Zachary, and Leo III — their judgements are void, as given in their own cause; and the acts done by them in keeping with this doctrine are the greatest crimes (especially that of Zachary) that are incident to human nature. And thus much of power ecclesiastical, wherein I had been more brief, forbearing to examine these arguments of Bellarmine, if they had been his as a private man, and not as the champion of the papacy, against all other Christian princes and states.

The queen deposed by the High Priest acting in the right of the young king Joash, not in his own right -- an example Hobbes uses against claims of independent priestly power to depose monarchs.

## modern-en · king-jehu · 9:24

There was once a great gathering of people in Abdera, a city of the Greeks, at the performance of the tragedy of Andromeda, on an extremely hot day. As a result, a great many of the spectators, falling into fevers, suffered this peculiar consequence from the heat and the tragedy together: they did nothing but recite iambic verses, with the names of Perseus and Andromeda. This, along with the fever, was cured by the coming on of winter. And the madness was thought to come from the passion imprinted by the tragedy. Likewise a fit of madness once raged in another Greek city, seizing only the young maidens, and causing many of them to hang themselves. Most at the time thought this an act of the devil. But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

Anointed by a young prophet whom Jehu's own soldiers dismissed as "that mad-man" -- cited as evidence the Jews of the time used "mad-man" and "Prophet" almost interchangeably for unusual, spirit-driven behaviour.

## modern-en · prophet-jehu · 41:14

And afterwards, when they demanded a king after the manner of the nations, it was not with a design to depart from the worship of God their king; but despairing of the justice of the sons of Samuel, they would have a king to judge them in civil actions — though they would not allow their king to change the religion which they thought was recommended to them by Moses. So they always kept in store a pretext, either of justice or of religion, to discharge themselves of their obedience whenever they had hope to prevail. Samuel was displeased with the people for desiring a king (for God was their king already, and Samuel had only an authority under him); yet Samuel, when Saul did not follow his counsel in destroying Agag, as God had commanded, anointed another king — namely David — to take the succession from his heirs. Rehoboam was no idolater; but when the people thought him an oppressor, that civil pretext carried away from him ten tribes to Jeroboam, an idolater. And generally, through the whole history of the kings, both of Judah and of Israel, there were prophets who always controlled the kings for transgressing the religion — and sometimes also for errors of state (2 Chron. 19:2): as Jehoshaphat was reproved by the prophet Jehu for aiding the king of Israel against the Syrians, and Hezekiah by Isaiah for showing his treasures to the ambassadors of Babylon. By all this it appears that, though the power both of state and of religion was in the kings, none of them were uncontrolled in the use of it except such as were gracious for their own natural abilities, or felicities. So that from the practice of those times no argument can be drawn that the right of supremacy in religion was not in the kings — unless we place it in the prophets, and conclude that because Hezekiah, praying to the Lord before the cherubim, was not answered from there, nor then, but afterwards by the prophet Isaiah, Isaiah was therefore the supreme head of the church; or because Josiah consulted Huldah the prophetess concerning the book of the law, that therefore neither he nor the high priest, but Huldah the prophetess, had the supreme authority in matters of religion — which I think is not the opinion of any doctor.

A different, later prophet of the same name, who reproved King Jehosaphat for aiding the King of Israel against the Syrians (2 Chron. 19:2) -- bound separately from King Jehu via his own "the Prophet Jehu" epithet.
