## modern-en · uzzah · 34:7

That the books of Samuel were also written after his own time, there are the like arguments: 1 Sam. 5:5; 7:13, 15; 27:6; and 30:25 — where, after David had adjudged an equal share of the spoils to those who guarded the baggage as to those who fought, the writer says, 'He made it a statute and an ordinance to Israel to this day.' Again, when David (displeased that the Lord had slain Uzzah for putting out his hand to steady the ark) called the place Perez-Uzzah, the writer says it is called so 'to this day' (2 Sam. 6:4). The time of the writing of that book must therefore be long after the time of the fact — that is, long after the time of David.

Struck dead for touching the Ark to steady it, per 2 Sam. 6 -- cited in the survey of 2 Samuel's authorship and events.

## modern-en · naaman · 43:10

But what (some may object) if a king, or a senate, or some other sovereign person forbid us to believe in Christ? To this I answer, that such forbidding is of no effect, because belief and unbelief never follow men's commands. Faith is a gift of God, which man can neither give nor take away by promise of rewards, or by menaces of torture. And if it is further asked, what if we are commanded by our lawful prince to say with our tongue that we do not believe — must we obey such a command? Profession with the tongue is only an external thing, and no more than any other gesture by which we signify our obedience; and in this a Christian, holding firmly in his heart the faith of Christ, has the same liberty that the prophet Elisha allowed to Naaman the Syrian. Naaman was converted in his heart to the God of Israel; for he says (2 Kings 5:17): 'Your servant will henceforth offer neither burnt offering, nor sacrifice, to other gods but to the Lord. In this thing the Lord pardon your servant: that when my master goes into the house of Rimmon to worship there, and he leans on my hand, and I bow myself in the house of Rimmon — when I bow myself in the house of Rimmon, the Lord pardon your servant in this thing.' This the prophet approved, and bade him 'Go in peace.' Here Naaman believed in his heart; but by bowing before the idol Rimmon, he denied the true God in effect, as much as if he had done it with his lips. But then, what shall we answer to our Saviour's saying, 'Whoever denies me before men, I will deny him before my Father which is in heaven'? This we may say: that whatever a subject (as Naaman was) is compelled to do in obedience to his sovereign, and does not in order to satisfy his own mind, but in order to satisfy the laws of his country, that action is not his, but his sovereign's. Nor is it he that in this case denies Christ before men, but his governor and the law of his country. If any man shall accuse this doctrine as repugnant to true and unfeigned Christianity, I ask him: in case there should be a subject in any Christian commonwealth who in his heart is inwardly of the Mahometan religion, then if his sovereign commands him to be present at the divine service of the Christian church, on pain of death, whether he thinks that Mahometan obliged in conscience to suffer death for that cause, rather than obey that command of his lawful prince? If he says he ought rather to suffer death, then he authorises all private men to disobey their princes in maintenance of their religion, true or false. If he says he ought to be obedient, then he allows to himself what he denies to another — contrary to the words of our Saviour, 'Whatever you would that men should do to you, that do to them'; and contrary to the law of nature (which is the indubitable everlasting law of God), 'Do not to another what you would not he should do to you.'

The Syrian officer healed of leprosy and converted "in his heart" by Elisha, cited as scriptural precedent for the liberty of outward conformity to an idolatrous sovereign's ceremony while inwardly rejecting it.

## modern-en · elisha · 37:9

In what manner God spoke to Adam, and Eve, and Cain, and Noah, is not expressed; nor how he spoke to Abraham, until the time when he came out of his own country to Shechem in the land of Canaan; and then (Gen. 12:7) God is said to have appeared to him. So there is one way by which God made his presence manifest: by an apparition, or vision. And again (Gen. 15:1), the word of the Lord came to Abraham in a vision — that is, something, as a sign of God's presence, appeared as God's messenger, to speak to him. Again, the Lord appeared to Abraham (Gen. 18:1) by an apparition of three angels; and to Abimelech (Gen. 20:3) in a dream; to Lot (Gen. 19:1) by an apparition of two angels; and to Hagar (Gen. 21:17) by the apparition of one angel; and to Abraham again (Gen. 22:11) by the apparition of a voice from heaven; and (Gen. 26:24) to Isaac in the night (that is, in his sleep, or by a dream); and to Jacob (Gen. 28:12) in a dream — that is (in the words of the text), 'Jacob dreamed that he saw a ladder, etc.' And (Gen. 32:1) in a vision of angels. And to Moses (Exod. 3:2) in the apparition of a flame of fire out of the midst of a bush. And after the time of Moses, wherever the manner in which God spoke directly to a man in the Old Testament is expressed, he always spoke by a vision, or by a dream — as to Gideon, Samuel, Elijah, Elisha, Isaiah, Ezekiel, and the rest of the prophets; and often in the New Testament, as to Joseph, to St Peter, to St Paul, and to St John the Evangelist in the Apocalypse.

The prophet who permitted Naaman's outward conformity in the house of Rimmon; also named among the prophets to whom God spoke "by a Vision, or by a Dream."

## modern-en · elijah · 37:3

Second, for the effect of his word — that is, for the thing itself which by his word is affirmed, commanded, threatened, or promised. As in Psalm 105:19, where Joseph is said to have been kept in prison 'till his word was come' — that is, till what he had (Gen. 40:13) foretold to Pharaoh's butler, concerning his being restored to his office, had come to pass. There, 'his word was come' means the thing itself had come to pass. So also (1 Kings 18:36) Elijah says to God, 'I have done all these your words,' instead of 'I have done all these things at your word,' or commandment. And (Jer. 17:15), 'Where is the word of the Lord?' is put for 'Where is the evil he threatened?' And (Ezek. 12:28), 'There shall none of my words be prolonged any more' — by 'words' are meant the things God promised to his people. And in the New Testament (Matt. 24:35), 'Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away' — that is, there is nothing I have promised or foretold that shall not come to pass. And in this sense it is that St John the Evangelist — and, I think, St John alone — calls our Saviour himself, in the flesh, 'the Word of God,' as in John 1:14: 'The Word was made flesh.' That is, the word, or promise, that Christ should come into the world, 'who in the beginning was with God' — that is, it was in the purpose of God the Father to send God the Son into the world, to enlighten men in the way of eternal life; but it was not till then put into execution, and actually incarnate. So our Saviour is there called 'the Word' not because he was the promise, but the thing promised. Those who, taking occasion from this passage, commonly call him 'the Verbum of God,' do but render the text more obscure. They might as well call him the noun of God: for by noun, as also by verb, men understand nothing but a part of speech, a voice, a sound, that neither affirms nor denies, nor commands nor promises, nor is any substance corporeal or spiritual; and therefore it cannot be said to be either God or man — whereas our Saviour is both. And this Word, which St John in his Gospel says was with God, is (in his First Epistle, verse 1) called 'the Word of life'; and (verse 2) 'the eternal life, which was with the Father.' So he can in no other sense be called the Word than in the sense in which he is called eternal life — that is, 'he who has procured us eternal life,' by his coming in the flesh. So also (Revelation 19:13) the Apostle, speaking of Christ clothed in a garment dipped in blood, says his name is 'the Word of God' — which is to be understood as if he had said his name had been 'He that has come according to the purpose of God from the beginning, and according to his word and promises delivered by the prophets.' So there is nothing here of the incarnation of a Word, but of the incarnation of God the Son — therefore called the Word, because his incarnation was the performance of the promise; just as the Holy Ghost is called the Promise (Acts 1:4; Luke 24:49).

Named (also as Elias and Eliah) among the two scriptural exceptions to universal death, and among the prophets who received visions; appears with Moses at the Transfiguration, which Hobbes reads as itself a Vision.

The king who broke the Brazen Serpent Moses had set up, because the people had begun to burn incense to it -- cited as the scriptural precedent for a Christian sovereign's duty to break down images his subjects have taken to worshipping.

## modern-en · manasseh-king · 41:11

To the judges succeeded kings. And whereas before all authority, both in religion and in polity, was in the high priest, so now it was all in the king. For the sovereignty over the people — which had been, not only by virtue of the divine power, but also by a particular pact of the Israelites with God, and next under him, in the high priest as his vice-regent on earth — was cast off by the people, with the consent of God himself. For when they said to Samuel (1 Sam. 8:5), 'Make us a king to judge us, like all the nations,' they signified that they would no more be governed by the commands that should be laid on them by the priest in the name of God, but by one who should command them in the same manner as all other nations were commanded; and consequently, in deposing the high priest from royal authority, they deposed that peculiar government of God. And yet God consented to it, saying to Samuel (verse 7), 'Listen to the voice of the people, in all that they shall say to you; for they have not rejected you, but they have rejected me, that I should not reign over them.' Having therefore rejected God, in whose right the priests governed, there was no authority left to the priests but such as the king was pleased to allow them — which was more, or less, according as the kings were good or evil. And for the government of civil affairs, it is plain it was all in the hands of the king. For in the same chapter, verse 20, they say they will be like all the nations: that their king shall be their judge, and go before them, and fight their battles; that is, he shall have the whole authority both in peace and in war. In which is contained also the ordering of religion; for there was no other word of God in that time by which to regulate religion, but the law of Moses, which was their civil law. Besides, we read (1 Kings 2:27) that Solomon 'thrust out Abiathar from being priest before the Lord.' He therefore had authority over the high priest, as over any other subject — which is a great mark of supremacy in religion. And we read also (1 Kings 8) that he dedicated the temple; that he blessed the people; and that he himself in person made that excellent prayer used at the consecration of all churches, and houses of prayer — which is another great mark of supremacy in religion. Again, we read (2 Kings 22) that when there was a question concerning the book of the law found in the temple, the matter was not decided by the high priest; but Josiah sent both him and others to inquire concerning it of Huldah the prophetess — which is another mark of the supremacy in religion. Lastly, we read (1 Chron. 26:30) that David made Hashabiah and his brethren, Hebronites, officers of Israel among them westward, 'in all business of the Lord, and in the service of the king.' Likewise (verse 32), that he made other Hebronites 'rulers over the Reubenites, the Gadites, and the half tribe of Manasseh' (these were the rest of Israel that dwelt beyond Jordan), 'for every matter pertaining to God, and affairs of the king.' Is this not full power, both temporal and spiritual, as they call it, who would divide it? To conclude: from the first institution of God's kingdom to the captivity, the supremacy of religion was in the same hand as that of the civil sovereignty; and the priest's office, after the election of Saul, was not magisterial but ministerial.

Named in the survey of the Books of Kings' chronology and authorship.

## modern-en · josiah · 34:4

But though Moses did not compile those books entirely, and in the form we have them, he wrote everything he is there said to have written: for example, the volume of the law, which is contained, as it seems, in Deuteronomy 11 and the following chapters to chapter 27 — which was also commanded to be written on stones on their entry into the land of Canaan (Deut. 31:9). And this Moses himself wrote, and delivered to the priests and elders of Israel, to be read every seventh year to all Israel at their assembling for the feast of Tabernacles. And this is the law which God commanded that their kings (once they had established that form of government) should take a copy of from the priests and Levites, to lay in the side of the ark (Deut. 31:26); and the same which, having been lost, was long afterward found again by Hilkiah and sent to King Josiah, who, causing it to be read to the people, renewed the covenant between God and them (2 Kings 22:8 and 23:1–3).

The good king to whom Pharaoh Necho's warning words are cited as an example of true prophecy from an idolater's mouth; named (also as Josias) in the survey of 2 Kings.

The High Priest who found the Book of the Law in Josiah's reign, named in the survey of 2 Kings's events.

## modern-en · necho · 37:4

There are also passages of Scripture where, by the word of God, are signified such words as are consonant with reason and equity, though spoken sometimes neither by a prophet, nor by a holy man. For Pharaoh Necho was an idolater; yet his words to the good King Josiah, in which he advised him by messengers not to oppose his march against Carchemish, are said to have proceeded from the mouth of God; and that Josiah, not heeding them, was slain in the battle — as may be read in 2 Chron. 35:21–23. It is true that, as the same history is related in the first book of Esdras, it was not Pharaoh but Jeremiah who spoke these words to Josiah from the mouth of the Lord. But we are to give credit to the canonical Scripture, whatever is written in the Apocrypha.

The Egyptian Pharaoh whose warning to King Josiah, though he was "an Idolator," Hobbes cites as scriptural evidence that true prophecy is not restricted to a holy man's own mouth.

## modern-en · isaiah · 34:15

Of the prophets, the most ancient are Zephaniah, Jonah, Amos, Hosea, Isaiah, and Micah, who lived in the time of Amaziah and Azariah (otherwise Uzziah), kings of Judah. But the book of Jonah is not properly a record of his prophecy (for that is contained in these few words: 'Forty days, and Nineveh shall be destroyed'), but a history or narrative of his perverseness in disputing God's commandments; so there is little probability he should be the author, given that he is the subject. But the book of Amos is his prophecy.

Named (also as Esay) among the prophets to whom God spoke by vision, and as author of the Book of Isaiah in Hobbes's survey of the prophetic canon.

## modern-en · jeremiah · 34:16

Jeremiah, Obadiah, Nahum, and Habakkuk prophesied in the time of Josiah.

Named as a major prophet of the Captivity era in the survey of the prophetic books' authorship and chronology.

## modern-en · ezekiel · 34:11

The book of Job has no mark in it of the time when it was written. And though it sufficiently appears (from Ezekiel 14:14 and James 5:11) that he was no fictitious person, the book itself does not seem to be a history but a treatise on a question much disputed in ancient times — namely, 'why wicked men have often prospered in this world, and good men have been afflicted.' This is the most likely reading because, from the beginning to the third verse of the third chapter (where Job's complaint begins), the Hebrew is (as St Jerome testifies) in prose; and from there to the sixth verse of the last chapter, in hexameter verses; and the rest of that chapter again in prose. So the dispute is all in verse, and the prose is added only as a preface at the beginning and an epilogue at the end. But verse is not the usual style of men who are themselves in great pain, like Job, or of those who come to comfort them, like his friends — yet in philosophy, especially moral philosophy, it was frequent in ancient times.

Named (also as printed "Exekiel") as a prophet of the Captivity, alongside Daniel, Haggai, and Zechariah.

## modern-en · daniel-prophet · 34:17

Ezekiel, Daniel, Haggai, and Zechariah prophesied in the captivity.

Named as a prophet of the Captivity era, in the same survey as Ezekiel, Haggai, and Zechariah.

But the book of Amos is his prophecy.

Named among the most ancient prophets, alongside Zephaniah, Jonah, Amos, Isaiah, and Micah.

## modern-en · amos · 34:15

Of the prophets, the most ancient are Zephaniah, Jonah, Amos, Hosea, Isaiah, and Micah, who lived in the time of Amaziah and Azariah (otherwise Uzziah), kings of Judah. But the book of Jonah is not properly a record of his prophecy (for that is contained in these few words: 'Forty days, and Nineveh shall be destroyed'), but a history or narrative of his perverseness in disputing God's commandments; so there is little probability he should be the author, given that he is the subject. But the book of Amos is his prophecy.

Named among the most ancient prophets in the same survey as Hosea and Isaiah.

## modern-en · joel-prophet · 34:18

When Joel and Malachi prophesied is not evident from their writings. But considering the inscriptions, or titles, of their books, it is plain enough that the whole Scripture of the Old Testament was set forth in the form we have it after the return of the Jews from their captivity in Babylon, and before the time of Ptolemy Philadelphus, who had it translated into Greek by seventy men sent to him out of Judea for that purpose. And if the books of Apocrypha (which the Church recommends to us, though not as canonical, yet as profitable books for our instruction) may on this point be credited, the Scripture was set forth in the form we have by Ezra; as may appear from what he himself says in 2 Esdras 14:21–22 (etc.), where, speaking to God, he says, 'Your law is burnt; therefore no man knows the things you have done, or the works that are to begin. But if I have found grace before you, send down the holy Spirit into me, and I shall write all that has been done in the world since the beginning, which were written in your law, that men may find your path, and that those who shall live in the latter days may live.' And verse 45: 'And it came to pass, when the forty days were fulfilled, that the Highest spoke, saying, "The first that you have written, publish openly, that the worthy and unworthy may read it; but keep the seventy last, that you may deliver them only to such as are wise among the people."' And this much concerning the time of the writing of the books of the Old Testament.

Named among the minor prophets in Hobbes's survey of the prophetic canon.

Of the prophets, the most ancient are Zephaniah, Jonah, Amos, Hosea, Isaiah, and Micah, who lived in the time of Amaziah and Azariah (otherwise Uzziah), kings of Judah. But the book of Amos is his prophecy.

## modern-en · ezra · 34:0

By the books of Holy SCRIPTURE are meant those that ought to be the canon — that is, the rules — of Christian life. And because all the rules of life that men are in conscience bound to observe are laws, the question of Scripture is the question of what is law throughout all Christendom, both natural and civil. For though Scripture does not determine what laws each Christian king shall constitute in his own dominions, it does determine what laws he shall not constitute. Since therefore I have already proved that sovereigns in their own dominions are the sole legislators, only those books are canonical — that is, law — in every nation which are established as such by the sovereign authority. It is true that God is the sovereign of all sovereigns, and so, when he speaks to any subject, he ought to be obeyed, whatever any earthly potentate commands to the contrary. But the question is not of obedience to God, but of when, and what, God has said. To subjects who have no supernatural revelation, this can be known only by that natural reason which guided them, for the obtaining of peace and justice, to obey the authority of their several commonwealths — that is, of their lawful sovereigns. According to this obligation, I can acknowledge no other books of the Old Testament to be Holy Scripture except those that have been commanded to be acknowledged as such by the authority of the Church of England. What books these are is well enough known without setting them all out here; and they are the same as those acknowledged by St Jerome — who holds the rest (namely, the Wisdom of Solomon, Ecclesiasticus, Judith, Tobias, the first and second of Maccabees — though he had seen the first in Hebrew — and the third and fourth of Esdras) for Apocrypha. Of the canonical, Josephus, a learned Jew who wrote in the time of the Emperor Domitian, reckons twenty-two, making the number agree with the Hebrew alphabet. St Jerome does the same, though they reckon them differently. For Josephus numbers five books of Moses, thirteen of prophets who wrote the history of their own times (how that agrees with the prophetic writings contained in the Bible we shall see hereafter), and four of hymns and moral precepts. But St Jerome reckons five books of Moses, eight of prophets, and nine of other holy writings, which he calls Hagiographa. The Septuagint — seventy learned Jewish men sent for by Ptolemy, king of Egypt, to translate the Jewish law from Hebrew into Greek — have left us no other books for Holy Scripture in the Greek tongue than the same ones that are received in the Church of England.

Credited by Hobbes as the probable compiler of the Pentateuch and much of the historical Old Testament after the Captivity, under the name Esdras.

The king of Persia honoured Mordecai when he ordered that he be led through the streets in the king's own garment, on one of the king's horses, with a crown on his head, and with a prince walking before him proclaiming, 'Thus shall it be done to the man whom the king will honour.' And yet another king of Persia (or the same king at another time) gave one of his subjects, who had asked for permission to wear one of the royal robes as reward for some great service, leave to do so — but with the condition that he wear it as the king's fool. That was dishonour. So much for civil honours, such as magistracies, offices, titles, and in some places painted coats and escutcheons; and men honour those who have them as bearing so many marks of favour in the commonwealth — and that favour is power.

Honoured by the King of Persia -- paraded through the streets in royal robes on a royal horse -- cited as Hobbes's example of Civil Honour conferred entirely at a sovereign's discretion.

## modern-en · hashabiah · 41:11

To the judges succeeded kings. And whereas before all authority, both in religion and in polity, was in the high priest, so now it was all in the king. For the sovereignty over the people — which had been, not only by virtue of the divine power, but also by a particular pact of the Israelites with God, and next under him, in the high priest as his vice-regent on earth — was cast off by the people, with the consent of God himself. For when they said to Samuel (1 Sam. 8:5), 'Make us a king to judge us, like all the nations,' they signified that they would no more be governed by the commands that should be laid on them by the priest in the name of God, but by one who should command them in the same manner as all other nations were commanded; and consequently, in deposing the high priest from royal authority, they deposed that peculiar government of God. And yet God consented to it, saying to Samuel (verse 7), 'Listen to the voice of the people, in all that they shall say to you; for they have not rejected you, but they have rejected me, that I should not reign over them.' Having therefore rejected God, in whose right the priests governed, there was no authority left to the priests but such as the king was pleased to allow them — which was more, or less, according as the kings were good or evil. And for the government of civil affairs, it is plain it was all in the hands of the king. For in the same chapter, verse 20, they say they will be like all the nations: that their king shall be their judge, and go before them, and fight their battles; that is, he shall have the whole authority both in peace and in war. In which is contained also the ordering of religion; for there was no other word of God in that time by which to regulate religion, but the law of Moses, which was their civil law. Besides, we read (1 Kings 2:27) that Solomon 'thrust out Abiathar from being priest before the Lord.' He therefore had authority over the high priest, as over any other subject — which is a great mark of supremacy in religion. And we read also (1 Kings 8) that he dedicated the temple; that he blessed the people; and that he himself in person made that excellent prayer used at the consecration of all churches, and houses of prayer — which is another great mark of supremacy in religion. Again, we read (2 Kings 22) that when there was a question concerning the book of the law found in the temple, the matter was not decided by the high priest; but Josiah sent both him and others to inquire concerning it of Huldah the prophetess — which is another mark of the supremacy in religion. Lastly, we read (1 Chron. 26:30) that David made Hashabiah and his brethren, Hebronites, officers of Israel among them westward, 'in all business of the Lord, and in the service of the king.' Likewise (verse 32), that he made other Hebronites 'rulers over the Reubenites, the Gadites, and the half tribe of Manasseh' (these were the rest of Israel that dwelt beyond Jordan), 'for every matter pertaining to God, and affairs of the king.' Is this not full power, both temporal and spiritual, as they call it, who would divide it? To conclude: from the first institution of God's kingdom to the captivity, the supremacy of religion was in the same hand as that of the civil sovereignty; and the priest's office, after the election of Saul, was not magisterial but ministerial.

Named (1 Chron. 26:30) as one of the Hebronite officers David appointed over Israel, in Hobbes's survey of the priesthood's civil subordination.

## modern-en · balaam · 37:19

Since, then, there was in the time of the Old Testament such quarrelling among the visionary prophets — one contesting with another, and asking 'When did the Spirit depart from me, to go to you?' as between Micaiah and the rest of the four hundred; and such giving of the lie to one another (as in Jeremiah 14:14); and such controversies, in the New Testament and at this day, among the spiritual prophets — every man then was, and now is, bound to make use of his natural reason, to apply to all prophecy the rules God has given us to discern the true from the false. Of which rules, in the Old Testament one was the conformity of doctrine to what Moses the sovereign prophet had taught them, and the other the miraculous power of foretelling what God would bring to pass, as I have already shown out of Deut. 13:1ff. And in the New Testament there was only one mark, and that was the preaching of this doctrine: that Jesus is the Christ — that is, the king of the Jews, promised in the Old Testament. Whoever denied that article was a false prophet, whatever miracles he might seem to work; and he who taught it was a true prophet. For St John (1 Epistle 4:2ff.), speaking expressly of the means to examine spirits, whether they are of God or not — after he has told them that there would arise false prophets — says: 'Hereby know the Spirit of God. Every spirit that confesses that Jesus Christ is come in the flesh is of God' — that is, is approved and allowed as a prophet of God; not that he is a godly man, or one of the elect, just because he confesses, professes, or preaches Jesus to be the Christ, but that he is an avowed prophet. For God sometimes speaks by prophets whose persons he has not accepted, as he did by Balaam; and as he foretold Saul of his death, by the witch of Endor. Again in the next verse: 'Every spirit that confesses not that Jesus Christ is come in the flesh, is not of Christ. And this is the spirit of antichrist.' So the rule is perfect on both sides: that he is a true prophet who preaches the Messiah already come, in the person of Jesus; and a false one who denies him come, and looks for him in some future impostor who will take that honour on himself falsely — whom the apostle there properly calls antichrist. Every man therefore ought to consider who is the sovereign prophet — that is, who it is that is God's viceregent on earth, and has, next under God, the authority of governing Christian men; and to observe as a rule that doctrine which, in the name of God, he commands to be taught; and by this to examine and try out the truth of those doctrines which pretended prophets, with miracles or without, may at any time advance. And if they find it contrary to that rule, to do as those did who came to Moses and complained that there were some who prophesied in the camp, whose authority so to do they doubted; and leave it to the sovereign, as they did to Moses, to uphold them or to forbid them as he shall see cause; and if he disavows them, then no more to obey their voice; or if he approves them, then to obey them, as men to whom God has given a part of the spirit of their sovereign. For when Christian men do not take their Christian sovereign for God's prophet, they must either take their own dreams for the prophecy they mean to be governed by, and the swelling of their own hearts for the Spirit of God; or they must suffer themselves to be led by some strange prince, or by some of their fellow subjects, that can bewitch them, by slander of the government, into rebellion, without other miracle to confirm their calling than sometimes an extraordinary success, and impunity. And by this means, destroying all laws both divine and human, they reduce all order, government, and society to the first chaos of violence and civil war.

Named (as printed, "Baalam") as an example of a false prophet whose divination God nonetheless is said to have foretold through, per Numbers.

## modern-en · jesus-christ · 4:2

This train of thoughts, or mental discourse, is of two sorts. The first is Unguided, without design, and inconstant, in which there is no passionate thought to govern and direct those that follow it as the end and goal of some desire or other passion: in such a case, the thoughts are said to wander, and seem unconnected one to another, as in a dream. Such are commonly the thoughts of men who are not only without company but also without care for anything; though even then their thoughts are as busy as at other times, but without harmony, like the sound that a lute out of tune would yield to anyone, or in tune, to one who could not play. And yet in this wild ranging of the mind, a man may often perceive the way of it, and the dependence of one thought on another. For in a discussion of our present civil war, what could seem more impertinent than to ask (as someone did) what was the value of a Roman penny? Yet the coherence to me was clear enough. For the thought of the war introduced the thought of the delivering up of the King to his enemies; the thought of that brought in the thought of the delivering up of Christ; and that, again, the thought of the thirty pence, which was the price of that treason; and from that easily followed that malicious question—all this in a moment of time, for thought is quick.

The book's central religious figure throughout Parts III-IV: the sole article of faith necessary for salvation ("Jesus Is The Christ"), whose Kingdom Hobbes argues has not yet come, and whose Transfiguration, temptation, and miracles are read closely for whether Scripture requires literal possession by spirits.

## modern-en · john-the-baptist · 35:24

To mention all the places of the Old Testament where the name of angel is found would be too long. So to take them all at once, I say: there is no text in that part of the Old Testament which the Church of England holds canonical from which we can conclude there is, or has been created, any permanent thing (understood by the name of spirit or angel) that has no quantity, and that may not be, by the understanding, divided — that is, considered in parts, so that one part may be in one place, and the next part in the place next to it; and, in sum, which is not (taking 'body' for what is somewhat, or somewhere) corporeal. But in every place, the sense will bear the interpretation of angel as messenger; as John the Baptist is called an angel, and Christ the angel of the covenant; and as (by the same analogy) the dove and the fiery tongues, in that they were signs of God's special presence, might also be called angels. Though we find in Daniel two names of angels — Gabriel and Michael — it is clear from the text itself (Dan. 12:1) that by Michael is meant Christ, not as an angel but as a prince; and that Gabriel (as with the like apparitions made to other holy men in their sleep) was nothing but a supernatural phantasm, by which it seemed to Daniel, in his dream, that two saints being in conversation, one of them said to the other, 'Gabriel, let us make this man understand his vision.' For God has no need to distinguish his celestial servants by names, which are useful only to the short memories of mortals. Nor in the New Testament is there any passage from which it can be proved that angels (except where they are used for such men as God has made the messengers and ministers of his word or works) are things permanent, and also incorporeal. That they are permanent may be gathered from the words of our Saviour himself (Matt. 25:41), where he says that it shall be said to the wicked on the last day, 'Go, you cursed, into everlasting fire prepared for the devil and his angels' — which passage is plain for the permanence of evil angels (unless we might think the name 'devil and his angels' refers to the Church's adversaries and their ministers); but then it is repugnant to their immateriality, because everlasting fire is no punishment to impassible substances, such as are all incorporeal things. Angels are therefore not from this proved to be incorporeal. In the same way, where St Paul says (1 Cor. 6:3), 'Know you not that we shall judge the angels?'; and (2 Pet. 2:4), 'For if God spared not the angels that sinned, but cast them down into hell'; and (Jude 6), 'And the angels that kept not their first estate, but left their own habitation, he has reserved in everlasting chains under darkness to the judgement of the last day' — though they prove the permanence of the angelic nature, they confirm also their materiality. And (Matt. 22:30): 'In the resurrection men neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are as the angels of God in heaven' — but in the resurrection men shall be permanent, and not incorporeal; so therefore also are the angels.

Named for his singular fasting, which the Jews of his time took as a sign he "had a Devill" -- an example, per Hobbes, of how loosely that accusation was actually meant.

## modern-en · st-peter · 34:19

The writers of the New Testament all lived less than an age after Christ's ascension, and had all seen our Saviour, or been his disciples, except St Paul and St Luke; and consequently whatever was written by them is as ancient as the time of the apostles. But the time at which the books of the New Testament were received and acknowledged by the Church as their writing is not altogether so ancient. For as the books of the Old Testament come to us from no higher time than that of Ezra, who by the direction of God's Spirit retrieved them when they were lost, so the books of the New Testament — copies of which were not many, nor could easily be all in any one private man's hand — cannot be derived from a higher time than that at which the governors of the Church collected, approved, and recommended them to us as the writings of those apostles and disciples under whose names they go. The first enumeration of all the books, both of the Old and New Testament, is in the Canons of the Apostles, supposed to have been collected by Clement, the first (after St Peter) bishop of Rome. But because that is only supposed, and by many questioned, the Council of Laodicea is the first we know of that recommended the Bible to the then-Christian churches as the writings of the prophets and apostles; and this council was held in the 364th year after Christ. At which time, though ambition had so far prevailed on the great doctors of the Church that they no longer esteemed emperors (even Christian ones) as the shepherds of the people, but as sheep; and emperors who were not Christian as wolves; and they endeavoured to pass their doctrine, not as counsel and information from preachers, but as laws from absolute governors; and thought such frauds as tended to make the people the more obedient to Christian doctrine to be pious — yet I am persuaded they did not on that account falsify the Scriptures, even though the copies of the books of the New Testament were in the hands only of the ecclesiastics. For if they had had any such intention, they would surely have made them more favourable to their power over Christian princes, and to civil sovereignty, than they are. I see therefore no reason to doubt that the Old and New Testaments, as we have them now, are the true records of what was done and said by the prophets and apostles. And so perhaps are some of those books which are called Apocrypha, if left out of the canon not for any disagreement of doctrine with the rest, but only because they are not found in the Hebrew. For after the conquest of Asia by Alexander the Great, there were few learned Jews who were not perfect in the Greek tongue. The seventy interpreters who turned the Bible into Greek were all of them Hebrews; and we have extant the works of Philo and Josephus, both Jews, written by them eloquently in Greek. But it is not the writer, but the authority of the Church, that makes a book canonical.

The Apostle Simon, "surnamed Stone" -- Cephas in Syriac, Petrus in Greek -- whose confession that Jesus is the Christ founds Hobbes's central doctrine; also cited for the parable of his overfull net of fish as an image of Church-power outgrowing its restraint.

## modern-en · st-paul · 9:11

If to prudence you add the use of unjust or dishonest means — such as are usually prompted by fear or want — you have that crooked wisdom which is called CRAFT, and which is a sign of pusillanimity. For magnanimity is contempt of unjust or dishonest helps. And what the Latins call versutia (translated into English as 'shifting'), which is the putting off of a present danger or inconvenience by entering on a greater — as when a man robs Peter to pay Paul — is only a shorter-sighted craft. The name versutia comes from versura, which means borrowing money at interest for the present payment of interest.

Named constantly across the doctrinal chapters -- for his epistles' teaching on the Resurrection, on marriage and the clergy, on spiritual bodies, and (as Saul of Tarsus before his renaming) for persecuting the Church and later being commissioned at Antioch with Barnabas.

## modern-en · st-john-evangelist · 37:3

Second, for the effect of his word — that is, for the thing itself which by his word is affirmed, commanded, threatened, or promised. As in Psalm 105:19, where Joseph is said to have been kept in prison 'till his word was come' — that is, till what he had (Gen. 40:13) foretold to Pharaoh's butler, concerning his being restored to his office, had come to pass. There, 'his word was come' means the thing itself had come to pass. So also (1 Kings 18:36) Elijah says to God, 'I have done all these your words,' instead of 'I have done all these things at your word,' or commandment. And (Jer. 17:15), 'Where is the word of the Lord?' is put for 'Where is the evil he threatened?' And (Ezek. 12:28), 'There shall none of my words be prolonged any more' — by 'words' are meant the things God promised to his people. And in the New Testament (Matt. 24:35), 'Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away' — that is, there is nothing I have promised or foretold that shall not come to pass. And in this sense it is that St John the Evangelist — and, I think, St John alone — calls our Saviour himself, in the flesh, 'the Word of God,' as in John 1:14: 'The Word was made flesh.' That is, the word, or promise, that Christ should come into the world, 'who in the beginning was with God' — that is, it was in the purpose of God the Father to send God the Son into the world, to enlighten men in the way of eternal life; but it was not till then put into execution, and actually incarnate. So our Saviour is there called 'the Word' not because he was the promise, but the thing promised. Those who, taking occasion from this passage, commonly call him 'the Verbum of God,' do but render the text more obscure. They might as well call him the noun of God: for by noun, as also by verb, men understand nothing but a part of speech, a voice, a sound, that neither affirms nor denies, nor commands nor promises, nor is any substance corporeal or spiritual; and therefore it cannot be said to be either God or man — whereas our Saviour is both. And this Word, which St John in his Gospel says was with God, is (in his First Epistle, verse 1) called 'the Word of life'; and (verse 2) 'the eternal life, which was with the Father.' So he can in no other sense be called the Word than in the sense in which he is called eternal life — that is, 'he who has procured us eternal life,' by his coming in the flesh. So also (Revelation 19:13) the Apostle, speaking of Christ clothed in a garment dipped in blood, says his name is 'the Word of God' — which is to be understood as if he had said his name had been 'He that has come according to the purpose of God from the beginning, and according to his word and promises delivered by the prophets.' So there is nothing here of the incarnation of a Word, but of the incarnation of God the Son — therefore called the Word, because his incarnation was the performance of the promise; just as the Holy Ghost is called the Promise (Acts 1:4; Luke 24:49).

Named as a Gospel author and cited for the report, based on a misunderstood saying of Christ's, that he would not die before the Second Coming.

## modern-en · st-matthew · 9:25

But why then does our Saviour proceed to cure them as though they were possessed, and not as though they were mad? To this I can give no other kind of answer than the one given to those who press Scripture against the opinion that the earth moves. Scripture was written to show men the kingdom of God and to prepare their minds to become his obedient subjects, leaving the world and the philosophy of it to the disputation of men, for the exercise of their natural reason. Whether it is the motion of the earth or of the sun that makes day and night, or whether the disorderly actions of men come from passion or from the devil (so long as we do not worship him), it makes no difference to our obedience and subjection to God Almighty — which is the thing for which Scripture was written. As for our Saviour speaking to the disease as if to a person, that is the usual phrase of all who cure by words only, as Christ did (and as enchanters pretend to do, whether or not they really speak to a devil). For is not Christ also said (Matthew 8:26) to have rebuked the winds? Is he not also said (Luke 4:39) to rebuke a fever? Yet this does not argue that a fever is a devil. And whereas many of these devils are said to have confessed Christ, there is no need to interpret those passages as anything more than that those madmen confessed him. And whereas our Saviour (Matthew 12:43) speaks of an unclean spirit which, having gone out of a man, wanders through dry places, seeking rest and finding none, and returns into the same man with seven other spirits worse than itself — this is plainly a parable, alluding to a man who, after some little effort to give up his lusts, is overcome by their strength and becomes seven times worse than he was. So I see nothing at all in Scripture that requires us to believe that daemoniacks were anything other than madmen.

Named as a Gospel author, cited directly for his account of Christ's temptation in the wilderness and the Transfiguration.

## modern-en · st-mark · 9:24

There was once a great gathering of people in Abdera, a city of the Greeks, at the performance of the tragedy of Andromeda, on an extremely hot day. As a result, a great many of the spectators, falling into fevers, suffered this peculiar consequence from the heat and the tragedy together: they did nothing but recite iambic verses, with the names of Perseus and Andromeda. This, along with the fever, was cured by the coming on of winter. And the madness was thought to come from the passion imprinted by the tragedy. Likewise a fit of madness once raged in another Greek city, seizing only the young maidens, and causing many of them to hang themselves. Most at the time thought this an act of the devil. But one man, suspecting that this contempt of life might come from some passion of the mind, and supposing that they did not also despise their honour, advised the magistrates to strip the bodies of those who had so hanged themselves and let them hang out naked. This, the story says, cured the madness. But on the other side, those same Greeks often ascribed madness to the operation of the Eumenides, or Furies — and sometimes of Ceres, Phoebus, and other gods. So far did men attribute reality to phantasms, taking them to be airy living bodies, and generally calling them spirits. And as the Romans on this point held the same opinion as the Greeks, so also did the Jews; for they called madmen prophets, or (depending on whether they thought the spirits good or bad) daemoniacks. Some of them called both prophets and daemoniacks madmen; and some called the same man both daemoniack and madman. As for the Gentiles, it is no wonder; for diseases and health, vices and virtues, and many natural accidents, were among them named and worshipped as daemons. So that by 'daemon' a man might understand, on occasion, an ague as well as a devil. But for the Jews to have such an opinion is somewhat strange. For neither Moses nor Abraham claimed to prophesy by the possession of a spirit, but from the voice of God, or by a vision or dream; nor is there anything in his Law, moral or ceremonial, by which they were taught that there is any such enthusiasm, or any possession. When God is said (Numbers 11:25) to take from the spirit that was in Moses, and to give it to the seventy elders, the Spirit of God (taking it for the substance of God) is not divided. By 'the Spirit of God' in a man, the Scriptures mean a man's spirit inclined to godliness. And where it is said (Exodus 28:3) 'whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom to make garments for Aaron,' what is meant is not a spirit put into them that can make garments, but the wisdom of their own spirits in that kind of work. In the same sense, the spirit of a man, when it produces unclean actions, is ordinarily called an unclean spirit; and so other spirits, though not always, yet as often as the virtue or vice so named is extraordinary and eminent. Nor did the other prophets of the Old Testament claim enthusiasm, or that God spoke in them; they claimed that he spoke to them by voice, vision, or dream; and the burden of the Lord was not possession but command. How then could the Jews fall into this opinion of possession? I can imagine no reason but the one common to all men: lack of curiosity to search for natural causes, and the placing of felicity in the acquisition of the coarse pleasures of the senses, and of the things that most directly conduce to those pleasures. For those who see any strange and unusual ability or defect in a man's mind, unless they also see from what cause it might probably proceed, can hardly think it natural; and if not natural, they must think it supernatural; and then what can it be but that either God or the devil is in him? Hence it came about that, when our Saviour (Mark 3:21) was surrounded by the multitude, those of his household feared he was mad and went out to restrain him; but the scribes said he had Beelzebub, and that this was how he cast out devils — as if the greater madman had cowed the lesser. And again (John 10:20) some said, 'He has a devil, and is mad,' whereas others, holding him for a prophet, said, 'These are not the words of one that has a devil.' So too in the Old Testament: he who came to anoint Jehu (2 Kings 9:11) was a prophet, but some of the company asked Jehu, 'What did that madman come for?' In short, it is plain that whoever behaved in an extraordinary way was thought by the Jews to be possessed either with a good or with an evil spirit — except by the Sadducees, who erred so far on the other side as not to believe there were any spirits at all (which is very close to outright atheism), and so perhaps the more provoked others to call such men daemoniacks rather than madmen.

Named as a Gospel author, cited for his account of the Transfiguration and of the signs promised to true believers.

## modern-en · st-james · 20:22

But if it is lawful for a monarch to dispose of the succession by words of contract or by testament, men may perhaps object a great inconvenience: that he may sell, or give, his right of governing to a stranger; which, because strangers (that is, men not used to living under the same government, not speaking the same language) commonly undervalue one another, may turn to the oppression of his subjects. This is indeed a great inconvenience; but it does not necessarily follow from subjection to a stranger's government, but from the unskillfulness of the governors, ignorant of the true rules of politics. And so the Romans, when they had subdued many nations, in order to make their government digestible, used to remove that grievance as far as they thought necessary by giving, sometimes to whole nations, and sometimes to the principal men of every nation they conquered, not only the privileges but also the name of Romans, and by taking many of them into the Senate and into offices of charge, even in the city of Rome itself. This was what our most wise king, King James, aimed at in seeking the union of his two realms of England and Scotland. If he could have achieved it, in all likelihood it would have prevented the civil wars that make both those kingdoms now miserable. So it is no injury to the people for a monarch to dispose of the succession by will, though by the fault of many princes it has sometimes proved inconvenient. As another argument for its lawfulness: whatever inconvenience may come from giving a kingdom to a stranger may also come from marrying so with strangers that the right of succession may descend to them — yet this is by everyone accounted lawful.

Named among the Apostles present at the Transfiguration.

## modern-en · barnabas · 37:1

Again, when we say 'the word of God,' or 'of man,' it may sometimes be understood of the speaker (as the words that God has spoken, or that a man has spoken). In that sense, when we say 'the Gospel of St Matthew,' we understand St Matthew to be the writer of it. And sometimes it is understood of the subject: in this sense, when we read in the Bible 'The words of the days of the kings of Israel, or of Judah,' it is meant that the acts done in those days were the subject of those 'words.' And in the Greek, which (in Scripture) retains many Hebraisms, by 'the word of God' is often meant not what is spoken by God, but what is spoken concerning God, and his government — that is, the doctrine of religion. So much so that to say logos theou and theologia is all one — which is the doctrine we usually call divinity, as is plain from the following passages. Acts 13:46: 'Then Paul and Barnabas grew bold, and said: It was necessary that the word of God should first have been spoken to you; but seeing you put it from you, and judge yourselves unworthy of everlasting life, lo, we turn to the Gentiles.' What is here called the word of God was the doctrine of the Christian religion, as is evident from what goes before. And in Acts 5:20, where it is said to the apostles by an angel, 'Go, stand and speak in the temple, all the words of this life' — by 'the words of this life' is meant the doctrine of the gospel, as is evident from what they did in the temple, and is expressed in the last verse of the same chapter: 'Daily in the temple, and in every house, they ceased not to teach and preach Christ Jesus.' From this it is plain that Jesus Christ was the subject of this 'word of life,' or (which is all one) the subject of the 'words of this life eternal' that our Saviour offered them. So (Acts 15:7) the word of God is called the word of the gospel, because it contains the doctrine of the kingdom of Christ; and the same word (Rom. 10:8–9) is called the word of faith — that is, as is there explained, the doctrine of Christ come, and raised from the dead. Also (Matt. 13:19): 'When any one hears the word of the kingdom,' that is, the doctrine of the kingdom taught by Christ. Again, the same word is said (Acts 12:24) to 'grow and to be multiplied'; which to understand of the evangelical doctrine is easy, but of the voice or speech of God, hard and strange. In the same sense the 'doctrine of devils' signifies not the words of any devil, but the doctrine of heathen men concerning daemons, and those phantasms which they worshipped as gods (1 Tim. 4:1).

Named among the Antioch prophets and teachers set apart with Saul for missionary work, and (rhetorically, in the satire on repurposed pagan images) as an example of a Jupiter-image renamed for a saint.

## modern-en · philip-deacon · 43:59

For their employment, as deacons, was to serve the congregation; though on occasion they did not refrain from preaching the gospel and maintaining the doctrine of Christ, every one according to his gifts, as St Stephen did, and both preaching and baptising as Philip did. For the Philip who (Acts 8:5) preached the gospel at Samaria, and (verse 38) baptised the eunuch, was Philip the deacon, not Philip the apostle. For it is plain (verse 1) that when Philip preached in Samaria, the apostles were at Jerusalem; and (verse 14) 'When they heard that Samaria had received the word of God, they sent Peter and John to them' — by the imposition of whose hands those who were baptised (verse 15) received (what before, by the baptism of Philip, they had not received) the Holy Ghost. For it was necessary for the conferring of the Holy Ghost that their baptism should be administered, or confirmed, by a minister of the word, not by a minister of the church. And therefore, to confirm the baptism of those that Philip the deacon had baptised, the apostles sent out of their own number from Jerusalem to Samaria Peter and John, who conferred on those who had before been only baptised those graces that were signs of the Holy Spirit — which at that time accompanied all true believers. What these were may be understood from what St Mark says (chapter 16:17): 'These signs follow those that believe in my name: they shall cast out devils; they shall speak with new tongues; they shall take up serpents, and if they drink any deadly thing, it shall not hurt them; they shall lay hands on the sick, and they shall recover.' This Philip could not give; but the apostles could, and (as appears by this passage) effectually did, to every man who truly believed and was by a minister of Christ himself baptised — which power either Christ's ministers in this age cannot confer, or else there are very few true believers, or Christ has very few ministers.

One of the seven appointed to serve tables in Acts 6, who preached and baptized in Samaria and baptized the Ethiopian eunuch -- explicitly distinguished by the text itself from Philip the Apostle, who remained at Jerusalem throughout.

## modern-en · ananias-and-sapphira · 43:64

After our Saviour's ascension, the Christians of every city lived in common (Acts 4:34), on the money which was made from the sale of their lands and possessions, and laid down at the feet of the apostles, of good will, not of duty. For 'while it remained' (says St Peter to Ananias, Acts 5:4), 'was it not your own? And after it was sold, was it not in your power?' — which shows that he need not have saved his land, or his money, by lying, as he was not bound to contribute anything at all unless he had pleased. And as in the time of the apostles, so also all the time downward, until after Constantine the Great, we shall find that the maintenance of the bishops and pastors of the Christian church was nothing but the voluntary contribution of those who had embraced their doctrine. There was as yet no mention of tithes. But such was, in the time of Constantine and his sons, the affection of Christians for their pastors, as Ammianus Marcellinus says (describing the sedition of Damasus and Ursinicus about the bishopric), that 'it was worth their contention'; in that the bishops of those times, by the liberality of their flock — and especially of matrons — lived splendidly, were carried in coaches, and were sumptuous in their fare and apparel.

The disciple struck down for lying about the price of his land (Acts 5) -- Peter's rebuke, "whilest the Land remained, was it not thine?", is cited by Hobbes on the limits of communal property obligations.

## modern-en · ananias-of-damascus · 43:79

According to this ancient rite, the apostles, and the presbyters, and the presbytery itself, laid hands on those whom they ordained pastors, and prayed for them that they might receive the Holy Ghost; and that not only once, but sometimes more often, when a new occasion was presented. But the end was still the same — namely, a punctual and religious designation of the person, ordained either to the pastoral charge in general or to a particular mission. So (Acts 6:6) 'The apostles prayed, and laid their hands' on the seven deacons — which was done, not to give them the Holy Ghost (for they were full of the Holy Ghost before they were chosen, as appears immediately before, verse 3), but to designate them to that office. And after Philip the deacon had converted certain persons in Samaria, Peter and John went down (Acts 8:17) 'and laid their hands on them, and they received the Holy Ghost.' And not only an apostle, but a presbyter, had this power. For St Paul advises Timothy (1 Tim. 5:22), 'Lay hands suddenly on no man' — that is, designate no man rashly to the office of a pastor. The whole presbytery laid their hands on Timothy, as we read 1 Tim. 4:14; but this is to be understood as that some did it by the appointment of the presbytery, and most likely their proestos, or prolocutor — which may have been St Paul himself. For in his Second Epistle to Timothy (verse 6), he says to him, 'Stir up the gift of God that is in you by the laying on of my hands.' Where note, by the way, that by 'the Holy Ghost' is meant not the third person in the Trinity, but the gifts necessary for the pastoral office. We read also that St Paul had imposition of hands twice: once from Ananias at Damascus (Acts 9:17–18), at the time of his baptism; and again (Acts 13:3) at Antioch, when he was first sent out to preach. The use, then, of this ceremony, considered in the ordination of pastors, was to designate the person to whom they gave such power. But if there had been then any Christian who had had the power of teaching before, the baptising of him — that is, the making of him a Christian — had given him no new power, but had only caused him to preach true doctrine, that is, to use his power aright; and therefore the imposition of hands would have been unnecessary; baptism itself would have been sufficient. But every sovereign, before Christianity, had the power of teaching and of ordaining teachers; and therefore Christianity gave them no new right, but only directed them in the way of teaching truth; and consequently they needed no imposition of hands (besides that which is done in baptism) to authorise them to exercise any part of the pastoral function, as namely, to baptise and consecrate. And in the Old Testament, though the priest only had the right to consecrate during the time that the sovereignty was in the high priest, it was not so when the sovereignty was in the king. For we read (1 Kings 8) that Solomon blessed the people, consecrated the temple, and pronounced that public prayer which is the pattern now for the consecration of all Christian churches and chapels: whereby it appears he had not only the right of ecclesiastical government, but also of exercising ecclesiastical functions.

The disciple who restored Paul's sight and laid hands on him at his baptism (Acts 9) -- a different Ananias from the one struck down in Acts 5, distinguished in the text by his own epithet, "Ananias at Damascus."

## modern-en · timothy · 43:25

Excommunication lies for injustice: as (Matt. 18) 'If your brother offend you, tell it him privately; then with witnesses; lastly, tell the church; and then if he does not obey, let him be to you as a heathen man, and a publican.' And excommunication lies for a scandalous life, as (1 Cor. 5:11): 'If any man that is called a brother be a fornicator, or covetous, or an idolater, or a drunkard, or an extortioner, with such a one you are not to eat.' But to excommunicate a man who held this foundation — that Jesus was the Christ — for difference of opinion in other points, by which that foundation was not destroyed, there appears no authority in Scripture, nor example in the apostles. There is indeed in St Paul (Titus 3:10) a text that seems to be to the contrary: 'A man that is a heretic, after the first and second admonition, reject.' For a heretic is one who, being a member of the church, nevertheless teaches some private opinion which the church has forbidden; and such a one, St Paul advises Titus, after the first and second admonition, to reject. But to 'reject' (in this place) is not to excommunicate the man, but to give over admonishing him, to let him alone, to set by disputing with him, as one who is to be convinced only by himself. The same apostle says (2 Tim. 2:23), 'Foolish and unlearned questions avoid.' The word 'avoid' in this place, and 'reject' in the former, is the same in the original: paraitou. But foolish questions may be set by, without excommunication. And again (Tit. 3:9), 'Avoid foolish questions,' where the original, periistaso ('set them by'), is equivalent to the former word 'reject.' There is no other place that can so much as colourably be drawn to countenance the casting out of the church faithful men — such as believed the foundation, only for a singular superstructure of their own, proceeding perhaps from a good and pious conscience. But on the contrary, all such passages as command avoiding such disputes are written for a lesson to pastors (such as Timothy and Titus were) not to make new articles of faith, by determining every small controversy, which obliges men to a needless burden of conscience, or provokes them to break the union of the church. Which lesson the apostles themselves observed well. St Peter and St Paul, though their controversy was great (as we may read in Gal. 2:11), yet they did not cast one another out of the church. Nevertheless, during the apostles' time, there were other pastors who did not observe it: as Diotrephes (3 John 9 etc.), who cast out of the church such as St John himself thought fit to be received into it, out of a pride he took in pre-eminence; so early it was, that vainglory and ambition had found entrance into the church of Christ.

Paul's disciple and correspondent, named as an Elder (also called a Bishop in Hobbes's reading), cited for the instructions Paul gives him on laying on of hands and other pastoral advice.

## modern-en · titus-companion · 21:15

Let us now consider what Scripture teaches on the same point. To Moses, the children of Israel say: 'Speak thou to us, and we will hear thee; but let not God speak to us, lest we die' (Exodus 20:19). This is absolute obedience to Moses. Concerning the right of kings, God himself, by the mouth of Samuel, says (1 Samuel 8:11–12 etc.): 'This shall be the right of the king you will have to reign over you. He shall take your sons, and set them to drive his chariots, and to be his horsemen, and to run before his chariots; and gather in his harvest; and to make his engines of war, and instruments of his chariots; and shall take your daughters to make perfumes, to be his cooks and bakers. He shall take your fields, your vineyards, and your olive yards, and give them to his servants. He shall take the tithe of your corn and wine, and give it to the men of his chamber, and to his other servants. He shall take your manservants, and your maidservants, and the choice of your youth, and employ them in his business. He shall take the tithe of your flocks; and you shall be his servants.' This is absolute power, summed up in the last words: 'you shall be his servants.' Again, when the people heard what power their king was to have, they still consented, saying (verse 19 etc.): 'We will be as all other nations, and our king shall judge our causes, and go before us, to conduct our wars.' Here is confirmed the right that sovereigns have, both to the militia and to all judicature — which contains as absolute a power as one man can possibly transfer to another. Again, King Solomon's prayer to God was this (1 Kings 3:9): 'Give to thy servant understanding, to judge thy people, and to discern between good and evil.' It belongs therefore to the sovereign to be judge, and to prescribe the rules of discerning good and evil; and these rules are laws; and so in him is the legislative power. Saul sought David's life; yet when it was in David's power to slay Saul, and his servants would have done it, David forbade them, saying (1 Samuel 24:9): 'God forbid I should do such an act against my lord, the anointed of God.' For the obedience of servants, St Paul says (Colossians 3:22): 'Servants, obey your masters in all things,' and (verse 20): 'Children, obey your parents in all things.' That is plain obedience in those who are subject to paternal or despotical dominion. Again (Matthew 23:2–3): 'The scribes and Pharisees sit in Moses' chair, and therefore all that they shall bid you observe, that observe and do.' There again is plain obedience. And St Paul (Titus 3:1): 'Warn them that they subject themselves to princes, and to those that are in authority, and obey them.' This obedience is also plain. Lastly, our Saviour himself acknowledges that men ought to pay such taxes as kings impose, when he says, 'Render unto Caesar the things which are Caesar's' — and paid such taxes himself. And that the king's word is enough to take anything from any subject when there is need, and that the king is judge of that need: for he himself, as king of the Jews, ordered his disciples to take the ass and the ass's colt to carry him into Jerusalem, saying (Matthew 21:2–3): 'Go into the village over against you, and you shall find an ass tied, and her colt with her; loose them, and bring them to me. And if any man asks you what you mean by it, say, The Lord has need of them; and they will let them go.' They are not to ask whether his necessity is a sufficient title, nor whether he is the judge of that necessity, but to acquiesce in the will of the Lord.

Paul's disciple, named alongside Timothy as a Pastor to whom apostolic letters of advice, not new articles of faith, were addressed.

## modern-en · apollos · 48:22

First, the power of the popes was dissolved totally by Queen Elizabeth; and the bishops, who before exercised their functions in right of the pope, did afterwards exercise the same in right of the queen and her successors — though by retaining the phrase of Jure Divino, they were thought to claim it by immediate right from God. And so was untied the first knot. After this, the Presbyterians lately in England obtained the putting down of episcopacy: and so the second knot was dissolved. And almost at the same time, the power was taken also from the Presbyterians; and so we are reduced to the independence of the primitive Christians, to follow Paul, or Cephas, or Apollos, every man as he likes best — which, if it be without contention, and without measuring the doctrine of Christ by our affection to the person of his minister (the fault which the apostle reproved in the Corinthians), is perhaps the best. First, because there ought to be no power over the consciences of men but of the word itself, working faith in every one — not always according to the purpose of those who plant and water, but of God himself, that gives the increase. And second, because it is unreasonable in those who teach that there is such danger in every little error, to require of a man endowed with reason of his own, to follow the reason of any other man, or of the most voices of many other men — which is little better than venturing his salvation on the toss of a coin. Nor ought those teachers to be displeased with this loss of their ancient authority. For there is none who should know better than they, that power is preserved by the same virtues by which it is acquired — that is, by wisdom, humility, clearness of doctrine, and sincerity of conversation; and not by the suppression of the natural sciences, and of the morality of natural reason; nor by obscure language; nor by claiming to themselves more knowledge than they make appear; nor by pious frauds; nor by such other faults as in the pastors of God's church are not only faults, but also scandals, apt to make men stumble one time or other on the suppression of their authority.

Named with Paul and Cephas as one of the factional leaders the Corinthian church divided behind (1 Cor. 1:12), cited again as one of the names the post-Episcopal Independency of England could follow "every man as he liketh best."

## modern-en · crispus · 43:74

There is no doubt that any king, if he were skilful in the sciences, might by the same right of his office read lectures on them himself, just as he authorises others to read them in the universities. Nevertheless, because the care of the sum of the business of the commonwealth takes up his whole time, it would not be convenient for him to apply himself in person to that particular task. A king may also, if he please, sit in judgement to hear and determine all manner of causes, as well as give others authority to do it in his name; but the charge of command and government that lies on him constrains him to be continually at the helm, and to commit the ministerial offices to others under him. In the same way our Saviour (who surely had power to baptise) baptised none himself, but sent his apostles and disciples to baptise (John 4:2). So also St Paul, by the necessity of preaching in various and far-distant places, baptised few. Among all the Corinthians, he baptised only Crispus, Gaius, and Stephanus (1 Cor. 1:14, 16); and the reason was, because his principal charge was to preach (1 Cor. 1:17). By which it is plain that the greater charge (such as is the government of the church) is a dispensation for the lesser. The reason therefore why Christian kings do not use to baptise is plain, and is the same for which at this day there are few baptised by bishops, and by the pope, fewer.

One of the only three people Paul personally baptized at Corinth (1 Cor. 1:14,16), cited as evidence baptizing was properly a Deacon's office, not the Apostle's own.

## modern-en · jason-thessalonica · 44:14

My second argument is taken from the subject of the sermons of the apostles, both while our Saviour lived on earth, and after his ascension. The apostles in our Saviour's time were sent, Luke 9:2, to preach the kingdom of God; for neither there, nor Matt. 10:7, does he give them any commission other than this: 'As you go, preach, saying, The kingdom of heaven is at hand' — that is, that Jesus is the Messiah, the Christ, the king who was to come. That their preaching also after his ascension was the same, is plain from Acts 17:6: 'They drew' (says St Luke) 'Jason and certain brethren to the rulers of the city, crying, These that have turned the world upside down are come hither also, whom Jason has received. And these all do contrary to the decrees of Caesar, saying that there is another king, one Jesus.' And from verses 2 and 3 of the same chapter, where it is said that St Paul, 'as his manner was, went in to them; and three Sabbath days reasoned with them out of the Scriptures, opening and alleging that Christ must needs have suffered, and risen again from the dead, and that this Jesus (whom he preached) is Christ.'

The disciple at Thessalonica dragged before the city's rulers for harbouring Paul and Silas (Acts 17:6), cited for the charge brought against them, "that there is another King, one Jesus."

## modern-en · martha · 44:16

The fourth argument is taken from passages express, and such as receive no controversy of interpretation. As first, John 5:39: 'Search the Scriptures, for in them you think you have eternal life; and they are they that testify of me.' Our Saviour here speaks of the Scriptures only of the Old Testament, for the Jews at that time could not search the Scriptures of the New Testament, which were not written. But the Old Testament has nothing of Christ but the marks by which men might know him when he came: that he should descend from David, be born at Bethlehem, and of a virgin; do great miracles, and the like. Therefore to believe that this Jesus was He was sufficient to eternal life; but more than sufficient is not necessary, and consequently no other article is required. Again (John 11:26), 'Whoever lives and believes in me shall not die eternally.' Therefore to believe in Christ is faith sufficient to eternal life, and consequently no more faith than that is necessary. But to believe in Jesus, and to believe that Jesus is the Christ, are all one, as appears in the verses immediately following. For when our Saviour (verse 26) had said to Martha, 'Do you believe this?', she answers (verse 27), 'Yes, Lord, I believe that you are the Christ, the Son of God, which should come into the world.' Therefore this article alone is faith sufficient to life eternal, and more than sufficient is not necessary. Third, John 20:31: 'These things are written, that you might believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God; and that, believing, you might have life through his name.' There, to believe that Jesus is the Christ is faith sufficient to the obtaining of life; and therefore no other article is necessary. Fourth, 1 John 4:2: 'Every spirit that confesses that Jesus Christ is come in the flesh, is of God.' And 1 John 5:1, 'Whoever believes that Jesus is the Christ, is born of God.' And verse 5: 'Who is he that overcomes the world, but he that believes that Jesus is the Son of God?' Fifth, Acts 8:36–37: 'See (says the eunuch) here is water, what does hinder me to be baptised? And Philip said, If you believe with all your heart, you may. And he answered and said, I believe that Jesus Christ is the Son of God.' Therefore this article believed — Jesus is the Christ — is sufficient to baptism, that is, to our reception into the kingdom of God, and by consequence the only necessary one. And generally in all places where our Saviour says to any man, 'Your faith has saved you,' the cause he says it is some confession, which directly, or by consequence, implies a belief that Jesus is the Christ.

Named for her confession at Lazarus's tomb, "I beleeve that thou art the Christ" (John 11:26-27), cited as an example of the single article of faith Hobbes argues is sufficient for salvation.

## modern-en · mary-magdalene · 43:63

Of the maintenance of our Saviour and his apostles, we read only that they had a purse (which was carried by Judas Iscariot); and that of the apostles, those who were fishermen sometimes used their trade; and that when our Saviour sent the twelve apostles to preach, he forbade them 'to carry gold, and silver, and brass in their purses, for that the workman is worthy of his hire' (Matt. 10:9–10). By which it is probable that their ordinary maintenance was not unsuited to their employment; for their employment was (verse 8) 'freely to give, because they had freely received'; and their maintenance was the free gift of those who believed the good tidings they carried about of the coming of the Messiah their Saviour. To which we may add what was contributed out of gratitude, by such as our Saviour had healed of diseases — of which are mentioned 'certain women (Luke 8:2–3) who had been healed of evil spirits and infirmities: Mary Magdalene, out of whom went seven devils; and Joanna the wife of Chuza, Herod's steward; and Susanna; and many others, who ministered to him of their substance.'

Named among the women who ministered to Christ, "out of whom went seven Devills" (Luke 8:2-3).
