## modern-en · zacchaeus · 43:21

The use and effect of excommunication, while it was not yet strengthened with the civil power, was no more than that those who were not excommunicate were to avoid the company of those who were. It was not enough to regard them as heathen who had never been Christians, for with such they might eat and drink — which with excommunicate persons they might not — as appears by the words of St Paul (1 Cor. 5:9 etc.), where he tells them he had formerly forbidden them to 'company with fornicators'; but (because that could not be done without going out of the world) he restrained it to such fornicators, and otherwise vicious persons, as were of the brethren: 'with such a one,' he says, they ought not to keep company, 'no, not to eat.' And this is no more than what our Saviour says (Matt. 18:17), 'Let him be to you as a heathen, and as a publican.' For publicans (which signifies tax-farmers, and receivers of the revenue of the commonwealth) were so hated and detested by the Jews who had to pay it that 'publican' and 'sinner' were taken among them for the same thing — so much so that when our Saviour accepted the invitation of Zacchaeus, a publican, although it was to convert him, it was raised against him as a crime. And so, when our Saviour added 'publican' to 'heathen,' he did forbid them to eat with a man excommunicate.

Named in Hobbes's survey of Luke's Gospel narrative.

## modern-en · jairus · 43:78

This ceremony was not therefore new in our Saviour's time. For Jairus (Mark 5:23), whose daughter was sick, besought our Saviour (not to heal her, but) 'to lay his hands on her, that she might be healed.' And (Matt. 19:13), 'they brought to him little children, that he should put his hands on them, and pray.'

Named as the ruler whose daughter's raising is recounted in the Gospels, in Hobbes's survey of Christ's miracles.

## modern-en · pontius-pilate · 42:5

Up to this point, then, there is nothing done, or taught, by Christ that tends to the diminution of the civil right of the Jews, or of Caesar. For as touching the commonwealth that then was among the Jews, both those who bore rule among them and those who were governed all expected the Messiah, and the kingdom of God; which they could not have done if their laws had forbidden him (when he came) to manifest, and declare, himself. Since therefore he did nothing but, by preaching and miracles, go about to prove himself to be that Messiah, he did nothing in this against their laws. The kingdom he claimed was to be in another world; he taught all men to obey, in the meantime, those who sat in Moses's seat; he allowed them to give Caesar his tribute, and refused to take upon himself the role of a judge. How then could his words, or actions, be seditious, or tend to the overthrow of their then civil government? But God, having determined his sacrifice for the reduction of his elect to their former covenanted obedience, used their malice and ingratitude as the means by which he would bring this to effect. Nor was it contrary to the laws of Caesar. For though Pilate himself (to gratify the Jews) delivered him to be crucified, yet before he did so he pronounced openly that he found no fault in him; and put as the title of his condemnation, not (as the Jews required) 'that he claimed to be king,' but simply 'That he was king of the Jews'; and notwithstanding their clamour, refused to alter it, saying, 'What I have written, I have written.'

Named for delivering Christ to be crucified "to gratifie the Jews" despite finding no fault in him -- cited by Hobbes as not contrary to the laws of Caesar, since Pilate acted from Roman authority, not against it.

## modern-en · herod-the-great · 44:13

For proof that the belief of this article — Jesus is the Christ — is all the faith required for salvation, my first argument shall be from the scope of the evangelists, which was, by the description of the life of our Saviour, to establish that one article: Jesus is the Christ. The sum of St Matthew's gospel is this: that Jesus was of the stock of David; born of a virgin (which are the marks of the true Christ); that the Magi came to worship him as king of the Jews; that Herod, for the same cause, sought to kill him; that John the Baptist proclaimed him; that he preached, by himself and his apostles, that he was that king; that he taught the law, not as a scribe, but as a man of authority; that he cured diseases by his word only, and did many other miracles which were foretold the Christ should do; that he was saluted king when he entered into Jerusalem; that he forewarned them to beware of all others who should pretend to be Christ; that he was taken, accused, and put to death, for saying he was king; that the cause of his condemnation written on the cross was JESUS OF NAZARETH, THE KING OF THE JEWS. All which tend to no other end than this: that men should believe that Jesus is the Christ. Such therefore was the scope of St Matthew's gospel. But the scope of all the evangelists (as may appear by reading them) was the same. Therefore the scope of the whole gospel was the establishing of that one article. And St John expressly makes it his conclusion, John 20:31: 'These things are written, that you may know that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of the living God.'

The king who, warned by the Magi's visit, sought to kill the infant Christ -- distinct from Herod the Tetrarch, whom the text names separately with his own epithet; see the namesake note above.

## modern-en · herod-antipas · 43:52

After him there was never any other apostle ordained, except Paul and Barnabas, which was done (as we read Acts 13:1–3) in this way: 'There were in the church that was at Antioch certain prophets and teachers — Barnabas, and Simeon that was called Niger, and Lucius of Cyrene, and Manaen (which had been brought up with Herod the tetrarch), and Saul. As they ministered to the Lord and fasted, the Holy Ghost said, "Separate me Barnabas and Saul for the work to which I have called them." And when they had fasted, and prayed, and laid their hands on them, they sent them away.'

Named by his own epithet, "Herod the Tetrarch," as the ruler with whom the Antioch teacher Manaen had been "brought up" -- distinct from Herod the Great, named separately above.
